<a id="topics"></a>

## Topics

Paper selection prompt and criteria (jump to the section by clicking the link):

[1. Application of diffusion models and vision-language models (VLMs) to robot manipulation.
](#topic-1)

[2. New methodological improvements to self-supervised learning (SSL) for image or video representation.
](#topic-2)

[3. Video segmentation using unsupervised or self-supervised methods.
](#topic-3)

[4. Transfer learning across modalities (e.g., audio-to-video, optical flow, language-to-video) for improved video understanding.
](#topic-4)

[5. Advances in 3D generation using generative models, including image-to-3D and text-to-3D.
](#topic-5)

[6. Recent progress in 3D reconstruction and generation with Gaussian Splatting, NeRF, or mesh generation.
](#topic-6)

[7. Agentic frameworks and environments for robot learning.
](#topic-7)

[Go beyond](#go-beyond)

## Full paper list
 <a id="1000-vlms-are-native-action-models-unleashing-the-manipulation-capabilities-of-vlms-with-explicit-action-space-grounding"></a>

### 1000\. [VLMs are Native Action Models: Unleashing the Manipulation Capabilities of VLMs with Explicit Action-Space Grounding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12832)
**ArXiv:** 2610.12832 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12832)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.12832.pdf)]

**Authors:** Jingkun Yi, Shujie Zhang, Zirui Zhou, ..., Yong Liu, Jiangning Zhang

**Abstract:** Pretrained vision-language models (VLMs) already exhibit strong visual, spatial and reasoning capabilities relevant to robotic manipulation. The key challenge in turning pretrained VLMs into generalist manipulation policies is to expose these capabilities through an appropriate action interface. Vision-language-action (VLA) models provide a unified trainable policy, but typically introduce learned action tokens, action heads, or action experts whose semantics must be learned from robot data pretraining. In contrast, modular foundation-model-based manipulation methods can exploit pretrained VLM capabilities for zero-shot manipulation, but rely on external planning and control scaffolds that are not end-to-end trainable as a unified continuous-action policy. In this work, we introduce EASG, an Explicit Action-Space Grounding framework that bridges these two paradigms. By projecting a tool center point (TCP)-centered action frame into the wrist camera observation and expressing actions in the form of VLM's native textual generation interface, EASG makes the semantics of a continuous robot action space directly observable in the VLM's perceptual context. This allows pretrained VLMs to directly generate executable robot actions using their existing visual-spatial reasoning capabilities, without robot-action pretraining or additional action modules. Besides, the same action interface supports direct fine-tuning with robot trajectories, allowing the models to continuously improve beyond their manipulation capabilities unleashed by EASG. Overall, EASG enables zero-shot manipulation and generalization across object positions, categories, and robot embodiments, while supporting effective fine-tuning in the same continuous action space. These results demonstrate that, given an appropriate action interface, pretrained VLMs can themselves serve as native action models for robotic manipulation.

**Comment:** Criterion 1: EASG grounds a VLM's native text interface in continuous robot actions by projecting a TCP-centered action frame into wrist-camera observations, enabling zero-shot manipulation and subsequent fine-tuning on robot trajectories.

**Relevance:** 10
Back to [[topic](#topic-1)] [[top](#topics)]

<a id="1001-leapbot-jac-joint-action-chains-for-world-action-models"></a>

### 1001\. [LeapBot-JAC: Joint Action Chains for World Action Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12886)
**ArXiv:** 2610.12886 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12886)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.12886.pdf)]

**Authors:** Chenhao Zhu, Baibing Liu, Pei Liu, ..., Yaonong Wang, Jun Ma

**Abstract:** Action-chunking policies predict future controls but execute only a prefix before replanning. How should a fixed action-token budget be organized to support execution? We propose LeapBot-JAC, which allocates sparse future-control references and dense execution targets within one shared action expert. Both chains are supervised by demonstrated controls and jointly denoised at aligned physical-time positions. Directed attention allows execution tokens to read reference tokens subject to the host's visibility constraints. JAC adds neither action tokens nor parameters and retains the host's world-modeling process; both chains participate in inference, while only the execution chain drives the robot. On LIBERO, JAC reaches 98.3% success with FastWAM and LeapBot-WA, up from 97.0% and 97.3%. On zero-shot LIBERO-Plus, their success rates improve from 53.5% to 70.4% and from 73.1% to 77.3%, respectively. RoboTwin and two real-world tasks provide additional deployment evaluations. Single-training-seed comparisons and FastWAM ablations support action-role organization as a practical design choice under a fixed action-token budget.

**Comment:** Criterion 1: LeapBot-JAC jointly denoises sparse future-control references and dense execution targets within world action models, improving FastWAM's zero-shot LIBERO-Plus success from 53.5% to 70.4% without adding action tokens or parameters.

**Relevance:** 10
Back to [[topic](#topic-1)] [[top](#topics)]

<a id="1002-forward-dynamics-models"></a>

### 1002\. [Forward Dynamics Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13117)
**ArXiv:** 2610.13117 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13117)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.13117.pdf)]

**Authors:** Zipei Ma, Junzhe Jiang, Jiahui Zhang, ..., Xiatian Zhu, Li Zhang

**Abstract:** Vision-language-action (VLA) models and world action models (WAMs) have advanced robotic policy learning by building on pretrained vision-language models (VLMs) and video generative models (VGMs), respectively. Yet VLAs learn without explicit supervision from future world states, while WAMs that condition actions on predicted futures incur extra inference cost and let visual prediction errors propagate into actions. We present Forward Dynamics Models (FDM), a family of robotic action models that couples action and future world-state prediction through an action-to-future paradigm. Future prediction is conditioned on action features, never the reverse: end-to-end training lets ground-truth future states supervise the policy through these features, while prediction errors cannot propagate into actions. At inference, FDM skips future generation entirely, avoiding its computational cost. The paradigm is backbone-agnostic, and we instantiate it with both VLM and VGM backbones. Experiments in simulation and on real robots demonstrate the effectiveness of FDM across both backbone families, achieving a state-of-the-art 99.4% average success rate on LIBERO and absolute gains of up to 13% over VLA and WAM methods on real-world tasks.

**Comment:** Criterion 1: FDM couples robotic policy learning with action-conditioned future-state prediction using VLM or video-generative backbones, skips future generation at inference, and achieves 99.4% average success on LIBERO.

**Relevance:** 10
Back to [[topic](#topic-1)] [[top](#topics)]

<a id="1003-evo-world-a-lightweight-vision-language-action-model-with-future-motion-prediction-via-optical-flow-supervision"></a>

### 1003\. [Evo-World: A Lightweight Vision-Language-Action Model with Future Motion Prediction via Optical-Flow Supervision](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12936)
**ArXiv:** 2610.12936 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12936)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.12936.pdf)]

**Authors:** Zile Zhong, Yuxin Du, Tao Lin, ..., Binhua Zuo, Bo Zhao

**Abstract:** Vision-language-action (VLA) models provide a unified framework for visual understanding, language grounding, and robot control, yet anticipating how the scene will evolve beyond the current observation can provide additional information for control. Action supervision alone does not explicitly encourage such anticipation, while future-image prediction entangles task-relevant motion with largely static appearance. To address these limitations, we introduce Evo-World, a lightweight VLA model that augments policy learning with future motion prediction through optical-flow supervision, thereby providing a task-relevant predictive signal focused on motion rather than appearance. We further design the training procedure to preserve these predictive representations during policy learning, regulating representation diversity while decoupling representation learning from action optimization. Across simulation benchmarks, Evo-World demonstrates strong performance, achieving a tier-averaged success rate of 91.1% on Meta-World MT50 , with a 13.0% relative improvement over the non-predictive baseline. On real-world AgileX robots, Evo-World outperforms the evaluated baselines across single-arm and bimanual manipulation tasks while using only 0.87B parameters and delivering lower GPU memory usage and higher inference efficiency. These results demonstrate that future motion prediction through optical-flow supervision provides an effective and lightweight approach to enhancing the predictive capabilities of VLA models.

**Comment:** Criterion 1: Augments a vision-language-action policy with optical-flow-supervised future motion prediction, achieving 91.1% tier-averaged success on Meta-World MT50 and demonstrating real-world single-arm and bimanual manipulation.

**Relevance:** 10
Back to [[topic](#topic-1)] [[top](#topics)]

<a id="1004-grounded-dreamer-robot-learning-from-world-model-synthetic-data-with-physical-grounding"></a>

### 1004\. [Grounded-Dreamer: Robot Learning from World Model Synthetic Data with Physical Grounding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13164)
**ArXiv:** 2610.13164 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13164)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.13164.pdf)]

**Authors:** Feng Jiang, Shu Chen, Yuanfei Wang, ..., Chen Xie, Ruihai Wu

**Abstract:** Scaling robot manipulation policies requires large amounts of diverse, action-labeled data, yet collecting such data in the real world remains expensive and difficult to scale. Existing methods either depend on human teleoperation, rely on simulation with limited visual realism, or use human videos that lack embodiment-specific annotations. In this paper, we introduce Grounded-Dreamer, a framework for synthesizing physically grounded robot manipulation data with video world models while avoiding human teleoperation demonstrations. Grounded-Dreamer combines the visual realism of generative video models with simulator physics engines. Starting from a small set of real images depicting robot task initial states, our method automatically reconstructs task-relevant objects and camera geometry, aligns them in simulation, and collects physically valid trajectories. These trajectories are used to post-train both a video world model and an inverse dynamics model, enabling the generation of visually realistic robot manipulation videos and corresponding robot actions from real images. To filter physically implausible rollouts, Grounded-Dreamer replays generated trajectories in the reconstructed simulation and retains only those that successfully complete the tasks. The real-world robot policies are trained solely on the grounded synthetic trajectories without any human demonstrations. Empirical results in real-world tasks demonstrate that Grounded-Dreamer can synthesize physically grounded data and substantially facilitate policy learning without requiring human teleoperation effort.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 1: Grounded-Dreamer combines video world models with simulator physics and an inverse dynamics model to generate action-labeled manipulation trajectories, training real-world robot policies solely on physically validated synthetic data without human demonstrations.

**Relevance:** 10
Back to [[topic](#topic-1)] [[top](#topics)]

<a id="1006-what-visual-representations-do-world-action-models-need"></a>

### 1006\. [What Visual Representations Do World Action Models Need?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13024)
**ArXiv:** 2610.13024 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13024)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.13024.pdf)]

**Authors:** Kai Liu, Jiangran Lyu, Tingyu Deng, ..., Zhizheng Zhang, He Wang

**Abstract:** World Action Models (WAMs) inherit powerful priors from pretrained video generation models, but their variational autoencoder (VAE) representations are optimized for visual reconstruction rather than manipulation. External visual representations can improve manipulation, but existing methods couple representation choices with different integration mechanisms, obscuring their individual effects. We systematically compare visual representations, five integration mechanisms, and feature combinations under controlled settings. Our study yields three main findings. First, strong general-purpose visual representations provide broader robustness gains and can match or outperform representations pretrained for specific visual capabilities. Second, channel-wise joint prediction achieves the highest average success rate; further analyses suggest that future-feature prediction and the action expert's effective use of added visual information contribute to its gains. Third, additional sources should be selected for the task-relevant information they contribute beyond existing representations, as the same source can help one combination but harm another. However, even the best evaluated composition provides a smaller additional gain than the initial improvement from adding a strong general-purpose representation to the VAE-only baseline. We instantiate the resulting Representation Recipe as R$^2$-WAM. It achieves state-of-the-art performance on RoboCasa GR1 Tabletop with an 84.7% success rate and reaches 92.8% on RoboTwin 2.0, improving over the VAE-only baselines by 5.9 and 2.2 percentage points, respectively. Taken together, these findings suggest that future work should prioritize stronger general-purpose visual representations and more effective mechanisms for integrating them into WAMs, with feature composition serving as a supplementary route to improvement.

**Comment:** Criterion 1: R²-WAM improves video-generation-based World Action Models through visual representation integration, achieving 84.7% manipulation success on RoboCasa GR1 Tabletop and 92.8% on RoboTwin 2.0.

**Relevance:** 10
Back to [[topic](#topic-1)] [[top](#topics)]

<a id="1008-beyond-actions-learning-future-operation-targets-for-vision-language-action-models"></a>

### 1008\. [Beyond Actions: Learning Future Operation Targets for Vision-Language-Action Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13212)
**ArXiv:** 2610.13212 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13212)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.13212.pdf)]

**Authors:** Min Lin, Bokai Zhou, Jianqi Lin, ..., Shen Zhao, Xiaodan Liang

**Abstract:** Actions specify what a robot should execute, but do not describe what those commands will achieve. Vision-language-action (VLA) policies nevertheless learn primarily by predicting action chunks from images, language, and state. We introduce Action--Future Coupled Effect Learning (AFCE), which makes a future-grounded physical Effect the policy's primary prediction target. An offline tokenizer constructs temporally ordered Effect tokens from demonstrated actions, realized robot responses, and sparse visual transitions. Reconstruction objectives encourage the representation to retain executable detail and observed physical change. A flow-matching VLA predicts Effects from current images, instruction, and robot state, while a state-conditioned decoder converts them into action chunks. Embodiment-specific interfaces allow single- and dual-arm tasks to share the Effect model. On DexJoCo, AFCE achieves $50.97\%$ overall success, improving over $π_{0.5}$ and UVT by 11.82 and 15.21 percentage points, respectively. It leads on all five bimanual tasks, highlighting the value of Effect supervision for coordinated dexterous manipulation. The project page is available at https://afceproject.github.io/AFCE-Web/.

**Comment:** Criterion 1: AFCE trains a flow-matching VLA to predict future physical Effect tokens and decode them into robot action chunks, achieving 50.97% success on DexJoCo and outperforming π0.5 by 11.82 percentage points.

**Relevance:** 10
Back to [[topic](#topic-1)] [[top](#topics)]

<a id="1010-beyond-successful-robot-demonstrations-learning-from-human-interactions-and-modeling-failure-dynamics"></a>

### 1010\. [Beyond Successful Robot Demonstrations: Learning from Human Interactions and Modeling Failure Dynamics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13522)
**ArXiv:** 2610.13522 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13522)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.13522.pdf)]

**Authors:** Peiyan Tu, Cong Wang, Hanxin Zhu, Xin Jin, Zhibo Chen

**Abstract:** Action-conditioned world models offer a promising foundation for generalist robotic intelligence by predicting the consequences of actions. However, existing robotic datasets provide limited coverage of the diverse interaction dynamics required for generalizable world modeling. Large-scale egocentric human videos offer complementary interaction experience, yet the embodiment gap between human hands and robotic manipulators hinders effective cross-embodiment learning. Meanwhile, the predominance of successful demonstrations leaves failure dynamics substantially underrepresented, limiting the ability of world models to anticipate unsuccessful executions. To address these limitations, we introduce HF-WM, an action-conditioned world model that combines Human interaction learning with Failure-aware post-training. To bridge the embodiment gap, we develop a dual-branch framework that couples video generation with auxiliary pointflow prediction, using stabilized pointflow as a unified motion representation across human and robot interactions while suppressing egocentric camera motion. To improve failure modeling, we further perform Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) post-training on perturbed actions derived from successful demonstrations, guided by action alignment and physical plausibility rewards, enabling the model to learn plausible failure dynamics without paired ground-truth videos. Extensive experiments demonstrate that HF-WM achieves state-of-the-art performance in action-conditioned video generation and exhibits strong utility for policy evaluation in downstream robotic tasks.

**Comment:** Criterion 1: HF-WM combines action-conditioned video generation with stabilized pointflow for human-to-robot transfer and GRPO-based failure-aware post-training, achieving state-of-the-art generation performance and demonstrating utility for downstream robotic policy evaluation.

**Relevance:** 10
Back to [[topic](#topic-1)] [[top](#topics)]

<a id="1011-dual-stream-memory-for-vision-language-action-policies"></a>

### 1011\. [Dual-Stream Memory for Vision-Language-Action Policies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13262)
**ArXiv:** 2610.13262 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13262)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.13262.pdf)]

**Authors:** Yang Wang, Ping Liu, Ruiyu Wang, ..., Lingfeng Zhang, Tao Zhang

**Abstract:** Memory-dependent manipulation requires a policy to track execution state and recover visual evidence that is no longer observable. We propose dual-stream memory for vision-language-action policies, assigning these requirements to complementary representations. A task-agnostic Gated DeltaNet compresses history into one temporal token, supervised by progress prediction and action-conditioned feature reconstruction. A task-conditioned retriever retains frames above a fixed relevance threshold; linear multi-scale fusion encodes each frame into sixteen spatial tokens. We then freeze the temporal compressor and jointly optimize retrieval and policy learning. Across sixteen RoboMME tasks, the complete model achieves 49.22% mean success versus 44.51% for the published FrameSamp+Modul reference, improving fourteen tasks and all four suite means. On four real-robot tasks, mean success reaches 62.5% versus 37.5%, with the largest gains on repeated actions requiring execution-stage discrimination. These results support combining execution context with selective visual access, while failures under cup swaps expose persistent identity tracking as an unresolved requirement.

**Comment:** Criterion 1: Combines a Gated DeltaNet temporal compressor with task-conditioned frame retrieval for vision-language-action manipulation policies, achieving 49.22% mean success on 16 RoboMME tasks and improving real-robot success from 37.5% to 62.5%.

**Relevance:** 10
Back to [[topic](#topic-1)] [[top](#topics)]

<a id="1012-rara-robust-policy-fine-tuning-through-retrieval-anchored-representation-alignment-for-robotic-manipulation"></a>

### 1012\. [RARA: Robust Policy Fine-Tuning through Retrieval-Anchored Representation Alignment for Robotic Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13578)
**ArXiv:** 2610.13578 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13578)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.13578.pdf)]

**Authors:** Xuhui Kang, Sung-Wook Lee, Yan Zhuang, Tianle Zhong, Yen-Ling Kuo

**Abstract:** Pre-trained robot policies, such as Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models, provide a strong foundation for generalist robotic manipulation, but adapting them to new tasks with only a few demonstrations remains challenging. Directly fine-tuning a pre-trained policy often achieves strong in-distribution performance, yet it can distort the representations learned during pre-training and weaken the model's generalization ability under out-of-distribution shifts. We propose Retrieval-Anchored Representation Alignment (RARA), a two-stage adaptation method, to address this issue. First, RARA retrieves representation features from pretraining with similar task intent and motion, and aligns the policy's representations of the target demonstrations with the pre-trained representations of the retrieved demonstrations. Second, RARA fine-tunes the policy on the target demonstrations while limiting how much its representations of the retrieved demonstrations change during fine-tuning. By reducing the representation mismatch before action learning and preserving pre-trained knowledge during adaptation, RARA limits unnecessary representation drift during fine-tuning. Experiments with Diffusion Policy and GR00T on 13 tasks in RoboTwin, RoboCasa, and on a real robot show that RARA improves the average success of fine-tuning by 9.5 points in-distribution and 13.6 points out-of-distribution, and outperforms all compared baselines. Project page: https://shua-kang.github.io/RARA_robot/

**Comment:** Criterion 1: RARA improves robotic manipulation policy adaptation through retrieval-anchored representation alignment, demonstrating average success gains of 9.5 percentage points in-distribution and 13.6 points out-of-distribution with Diffusion Policy and GR00T across 13 simulated and real-robot tasks.

**Relevance:** 10
Back to [[topic](#topic-1)] [[top](#topics)]

<a id="1013-lapt-scale-scaling-embodied-policies-with-latent-action-pre-training-on-human-videos"></a>

### 1013\. [LAPT-Scale: Scaling Embodied Policies with Latent Action Pre-Training on Human Videos](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13732)
**ArXiv:** 2610.13732 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13732)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.13732.pdf)]

**Authors:** Zhiyuan Xiang, Peidong Liu, Mingyang Li, ..., Wenbo Ding, Xiang Deng

**Abstract:** Scaling generalist robotic policies is limited by the high cost of collecting embodied interaction data. Large-scale egocentric human videos offer a rich alternative, but they lack robot action labels, and existing action annotation methods are often noisy under real-world visual complexity and diverse manipulation dynamics. We introduce LAPT-Scale, a scalable latent action pre-training paradigm with two components: LAPT-Scale-LAM learns continuous latent actions by explaining frame-to-frame visual transitions in human videos and uses them as human-derived supervision, while LAPT-Scale-Policy trains a VLA policy through human-video latent-action pre-training, target-robot embodiment alignment, and task-specific post-training. Unlike prior vector-quantized LAMs, LAPT-Scale-LAM uses a continuous latent space to preserve fine-grained motion and interaction dynamics. We further combine perceptual, semantic, and optical-flow alignment signals to improve transition-level supervision beyond pixel reconstruction. Trained on 23.3K hours of egocentric video, LAPT-Scale-LAM provides implicit action supervision for policy pre-training, yielding consistent scaling gains and improving real-robot success from 63.3% to 85.0% as annotated human video scales from 1K to 5K hours. The learned policy also generalizes to unseen visual conditions.

**Comment:** Criterion 1: LAPT-Scale pretrains a vision-language-action robotic policy using continuous latent actions learned from human videos, improving real-robot success from 63.3% to 85.0% as annotated human video scales from 1K to 5K hours.

**Relevance:** 10
Back to [[topic](#topic-1)] [[top](#topics)]

<a id="1014-smolwam-a-latent-world-action-model-for-robotic-manipulation-on-edge-devices"></a>

### 1014\. [SmolWAM: A Latent World-Action Model for Robotic Manipulation on Edge Devices](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13740)
**ArXiv:** 2610.13740 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13740)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.13740.pdf)]

**Authors:** Timi Owolabi, Hikmah Olawore, Frances Adelakun, Ayotomiwa Oyewumi

**Abstract:** World-action models (WAMs) predict a robot's actions together with the observations those actions will produce. However, the strongest models rely on video diffusion transformers that are far too computationally expensive for resource-constrained edge devices. We present SmolWAM, a latent WAM that we derive from the 2B-parameter Cosmos Policy by keeping its pretrained trunk and rebuilding its inference interface: single-frame visual latents, cached text features that preserve the trunk's attention sinks, 12 of 28 transformer blocks, and 1,008 tokens from which actions and future visual latents are read out in one rectified-flow step. Trained in 3.6 GPU-hours, SmolWAM succeeds in 95.5% of 400 LIBERO episodes at 57 ms and 2.3 TFLOP per 16-action chunk with 2.4 GiB of GPU memory, 11x faster than its teacher with 18.5x fewer FLOPs and 3.2x less memory, and its world head still forecasts scene motion.

**Comment:** Criterion 1: SmolWAM jointly predicts robot actions and future visual latents in one rectified-flow step, achieving 95.5% success over 400 LIBERO episodes with 11× faster inference than its teacher.

**Relevance:** 10
Back to [[topic](#topic-1)] [[top](#topics)]

<a id="1015-dream-to-remember-continual-steering-of-generalist-robot-policies-with-world-models"></a>

### 1015\. [Dream to Remember: Continual Steering of Generalist Robot Policies with World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13816)
**ArXiv:** 2610.13816 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13816)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.13816.pdf)]

**Authors:** Bahey Tharwat, Iman Nematollahi, Marvin Alles, ..., Akshay L Chandra, Abhinav Valada

**Abstract:** Generalist robot policies are trained with imitation learning and are bounded by the quality and coverage of their demonstrations. Reinforcement learning can improve and extend them, either by fine-tuning the policy weights or by training a small module to steer the input noise of a frozen generative policy. When tasks arrive sequentially, both approaches suffer from catastrophic forgetting. Fine-tuning on a task degrades prior tasks, and steering only shifts forgetting to the shared steering module. We propose Dream to Remember (D2R), which continually steers a frozen generalist policy toward new tasks, while retaining its earlier skills. As the steering module learns a new task, a frozen pretrained world model rehearses prior tasks through imagined rollouts, anchoring the module to its earlier behavior without a replay buffer or new data. When the world model is faithful enough, D2R learns the new task entirely in imagination. Otherwise, it learns the task in the environment while using the world model only to rehearse prior tasks. On CALVIN, D2R improves eight tasks in sequence entirely in imagination, raising average success from 63.7% to 74.8%, while LoRA fine-tuning on the same sequence drops it to 11.7%. With a frozen off-the-shelf generalist policy and world model, $π_0$ and A2World, D2R learns unseen LIBERO-90 tasks in the environment while keeping $π_0$'s performance on the LIBERO suites it was fine-tuned on, even though the world model is not accurate enough for policy optimization. Finally, on a real Franka robot, D2R improves three tasks in sequence entirely in imagination while retaining the earlier improvements.

**Comment:** Criterion 1: D2R uses a frozen world model to rehearse manipulation skills through imagined rollouts while continually steering a generative robot policy, improving average success across eight sequential CALVIN tasks from 63.7% to 74.8% and demonstrating skill retention on a real Franka robot.

**Relevance:** 10
Back to [[topic](#topic-1)] [[top](#topics)]

<a id="1016-axiom-0-hierarchical-self-refinement-for-vision-language-action-policies"></a>

### 1016\. [Axiom-0: Hierarchical Self-Refinement for Vision-Language-Action Policies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14155)
**ArXiv:** 2610.14155 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14155)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.14155.pdf)]

**Authors:** Renbin Shu, Jing Yang, Xiangyuan You, ..., Feng Wu, Keke Tang

**Abstract:** Reliable manipulation requires policies that generalize across tasks, layouts, and object interactions. Vision-language-action (VLA) models combine pretrained multimodal understanding with learned control, but their performance remains limited on unfamiliar task compositions. We present Axiom-0, a VLA policy that addresses both the quality of its representations and their use in action generation through hierarchical self-refinement. Intra-VLM Representation Refinement (IVRR) strengthens selected intermediate features through consistency supervision with learned feature projections during training. VLM-Action Interface Refinement (VAIR) recursively updates the resulting conditioning before action generation. Joint optimization of the supervised VLM layers, VAIR, and the action generator connects the two components. On RoboCasa365, Axiom-0 achieves 62.32% overall success in our evaluation, exceeding the highest published leaderboard score (Paimon-0, 58.1%) by 4.22 percentage points and the Xiaomi-Robotics-1 reference by 4.92 points. Under the evaluated profiling setup, mean action-chunk generation latency increases by 1.7% over Xiaomi-Robotics-1. These results demonstrate improved task completion with modest inference overhead.

**Comment:** Criterion 1: Axiom-0 improves vision-language-action manipulation policies through intermediate VLM representation refinement and recursive action-conditioning refinement, achieving 62.32% success on RoboCasa365 versus the cited leaderboard best of 58.1%.

**Relevance:** 10
Back to [[topic](#topic-1)] [[top](#topics)]

<a id="1018-chronos-policy-learning-robot-policies-on-physical-time"></a>

### 1018\. [Chronos Policy: Learning Robot Policies on Physical Time](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13929)
**ArXiv:** 2610.13929 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13929)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.13929.pdf)]

**Authors:** Changhao Wang, Jaden Clark, Shuran Song

**Abstract:** Robot policies often convert sensing and control into fixed-rate observation-action sequences, leaving physical time implicit in sequence position. This assumption breaks when observation rates or delays vary, or when inference latency changes. We introduce Chronos Policy, which makes physical time explicit by conditioning on observation capture times and desired action execution times. We study two ways to incorporate time into a diffusion transformer: adding timestamp embeddings to token features, or encoding physical time in positional encoding through time-aware rotary positional encodings (time-aware RoPE). We find that time-aware RoPE is more robust, allowing attention to depend directly on physical time while keeping token content unchanged. During training, we temporally randomize which observations are sampled and when the action chunk begins, exposing the policy to a wider range of sensing and execution schedules that may occur during deployments. This also enables learning from asynchronous, multi-rate data without forcing all measurements onto a fixed temporal grid. Across simulation and real-world experiments, Chronos Policy improves performance on dynamic tasks, where observation-to-action timing is critical, and on multisensory tasks, where the relative timing between observations matters. On standard quasi-static tasks, it preserves nominal performance while improving robustness to changes in sensing and inference timing.

**Comment:** Criterion 1: Chronos Policy introduces physical-time conditioning through time-aware RoPE in a diffusion-transformer robot policy, improving dynamic and multisensory task performance and robustness to sensing and inference timing in simulation and real-world experiments.

**Relevance:** 10
Back to [[topic](#topic-1)] [[top](#topics)]

<a id="1019-noise-space-learning-for-vla-adaptation"></a>

### 1019\. [Noise Space Learning for VLA Adaptation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13943)
**ArXiv:** 2610.13943 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13943)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.13943.pdf)]

**Authors:** Junjie Lu, Chuheng Zhang, Yucen Wang, ..., Min Xu, Li Zhao

**Abstract:** Pretrained vision-language-action (VLA) models encode broad manipulation priors, yet adapting them to specific tasks requires balancing limited robot interaction, computational resources, and safety constraints. Noise space learning (NSL) adapts flow-matching-based VLAs by learning a context-conditioned distribution over noise while keeping the pretrained VLA fixed. Building on prior noise-steering methods, we formulate NSL as an interface for VLA adaptation that accommodates different learning algorithms and instantiate it with PPO, SAC, and AWR. Theoretically, we provide an information-theoretic analysis of how NSL enables efficient adaptation. Empirically, we study these NSL algorithms on LIBERO, RoboTwin, and four real-robot manipulation tasks under interaction, computation, and execution constraints. Across these settings, we develop a suite of effective NSL variants and derive practical recommendations for choosing among them under different resource and deployment regimes.

**Comment:** Criterion 1: Noise Space Learning adapts frozen flow-matching VLA policies through context-conditioned noise distributions learned with PPO, SAC, or AWR, evaluated on LIBERO, RoboTwin, and four real-robot manipulation tasks.

**Relevance:** 10
Back to [[topic](#topic-1)] [[top](#topics)]

<a id="1020-splat2act-from-captured-scenes-to-personalized-vla-manipulation"></a>

### 1020\. [Splat2Act: From Captured Scenes to Personalized VLA Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13945)
**ArXiv:** 2610.13945 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13945)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.13945.pdf)]

**Authors:** Xiaomeng Ye, Krishna Jaganathan, Gyubeum Lim, ..., Muhammad Zubair Irshad, Zsolt Kira

**Abstract:** A user who brings a generalist manipulation policy into their home or office wants it to pick up their objects from their shelves, counters, and tables. Yet even a strong generalist like $π_{0.5}$ achieves 0% pickup success in our captured deployment scenes without adaptation, and collecting real demonstrations in every user's environment is prohibitively expensive. We present Splat2Act, an automated real-to-sim-to-real pipeline that personalizes a pretrained vision-language-action (VLA) policy to a specific scene and object set using only a handheld phone scan and images of the user's objects. Unlike prior real-to-sim work that reconstructs individual tabletops, Splat2Act operates on building-scale indoor environments: it builds a photorealistic digital twin with 3D Gaussian Splatting and collision geometry, automatically discovers tabletop and non-tabletop receptacles, constructs feasible pickup tasks, and generates successful demonstrations with a privileged simulation oracle, with only scene and object capture required from a human. Applied to 101 scanned campus environments with a median navigable area of 104.87 m$^2$ and 7 receptacles, the pipeline yields 852 receptacles and over 68,000 pickup demonstrations. We fine-tune $π_{0.5}$ in two stages: Stage 1 adapts to hundreds of public object assets, drawn either from the deployment scene alone or from all 101 scenes; Stage 2 personalizes to the user's own objects on the deployment scene's receptacles. Our strongest personalized policy reaches 62.5% aggregate pickup success in the target scene. Deployed on a real Stretch 3, the same policy achieves 63.9% success on a 12-task subset, closely tracking its 66.7% simulated success on those tasks. These results suggest that automated real-to-sim-to-real training can personalize VLA manipulation policies using only simulation-oracle data.

**Comment:** Criteria 1 and 7: Splat2Act builds Gaussian-Splatting digital twins and automatically generates over 68,000 simulated pickup demonstrations to personalize a VLA manipulation policy, achieving 63.9% success on a real Stretch 3 task subset.

**Relevance:** 10
Back to [[topic](#topic-1)] [[top](#topics)]

<a id="1021-clothcode-generative-modeling-for-garment-state-estimation-and-dynamics-prediction"></a>

### 1021\. [ClothCode: Generative Modeling for Garment State Estimation and Dynamics Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14200)
**ArXiv:** 2610.14200 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14200)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.14200.pdf)]

**Authors:** Yingdong Ru, Lipeng Zhuang, Shiyu Fan, ..., Florent P. Audonnet, Gerardo Aragon Camarasa

**Abstract:** We introduce ClothCode, a unified generative framework for garment state estimation and action-conditioned dynamics prediction in a shared discrete configuration space. A material-aligned tokenizer encodes garment configuration as residual vector-quantized patch tokens, while task-specific Masked Generative Transformers predict these tokens through parallel confidence-guided refinement. Across three garment templates, ClothCode reduces mean displacement error by 26.6% for state estimation and 37.0% for dynamics prediction relative to the strongest baselines. Dynamics prediction requires only 16.7 ms per window, compared with 556.2 ms for diffusion. Integrated into the same model-predictive controller, ClothCode improves real-world folding and flattening success from 56.7% to 78.9%. Further results and videos are available at: https://anonymous.4open.science/r/ClothCode-DE32

**Comment:** Criterion 1: ClothCode uses masked generative Transformers over discrete garment configurations as an action-conditioned dynamics model for model-predictive control, improving real-world folding and flattening success from 56.7% to 78.9%.

**Relevance:** 10
Back to [[topic](#topic-1)] [[top](#topics)]

<a id="1022-learning-transferable-world-action-models-from-task-paired-human-videos"></a>

### 1022\. [Learning Transferable World-Action Models from Task-Paired Human Videos](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14351)
**ArXiv:** 2610.14351 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14351)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.14351.pdf)]

**Authors:** Jeongeun Park, Taekyung Kim, Dongyoon Han, Sangdoo Yun

**Abstract:** World-action models inherit broad visual and physical priors from video pre-training. However, standard within-video next-frame prediction allows them to rely on appearance shared between the context and future, rather than learning interactions that transfer across scenes and embodiments. We introduce WATCH, a cross-video pre-training that learns from task-paired human demonstrations collected across diverse environments. Conditioned on the video and actions of one demonstration, the model jointly predicts another demonstration's actions and future frames from its current observation. Predicting one demonstration from another encourages the model to learn task-relevant interactions that transfer across scenes and demonstrators. Our pre-training shows that these capabilities can be learned from readily scalable human-human pairs alone, without requiring matched human-robot demonstrations. Analyses before robot post-training show improved motion alignment across embodiments and better action and video prediction on unseen robots, with representations closer to those learned from robot pairs. After robot post-training, WATCH improves MimicDroid in-context success from 40.9% to 68.3% with Cosmos Policy and from 59.8% to 77.7% with Cosmos3, with the largest gains on the unseen levels. Gains persist under context-free post-training across robot embodiments. WATCH outperforms within-video pre-training and co-training baselines, supporting cross-video prediction as a scalable approach to learning transferable world-action representations.

**Comment:** Criterion 1: WATCH learns transferable world-action models through cross-video prediction of actions and future frames from task-paired human demonstrations, improving MimicDroid robot success from 40.9% to 68.3% with Cosmos Policy after robot post-training.

**Relevance:** 10
Back to [[topic](#topic-1)] [[top](#topics)]

<a id="1023-memory-without-curation-learning-multi-stream-latent-memory-for-robot-policies"></a>

### 1023\. [Memory Without Curation: Learning Multi-Stream Latent Memory for Robot Policies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14406)
**ArXiv:** 2610.14406 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14406)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.14406.pdf)]

**Authors:** Yilin Wu, Rutav Shah, Shuaiyi Huang, ..., Max Simchowitz, Guanya Shi

**Abstract:** It is widely appreciated that robotic policies require long-term memory to perform complex tasks. However, directly conditioning policies on all past observations leaves policy learning susceptible to learning spurious shortcuts, and introduces large latency at policy deployment. As such, today's memory-augmented policies curate what to remember, either by retaining only recent frames, selected keyframes, or textual summaries, or by learning latent representations of the visual stream alone. In this work, we propose an alternative approach IMPRINT, a policy-agnostic latent memory framework that augments vision-language-action (VLA) models with multi-stream memory without explicitly constraining what the policy should remember. IMPRINT compresses different streams into a latent memory that is updated incrementally at inference, enabling low latency. To avoid shortcut learning, IMPRINT adopts a two-stage training procedure, first supervising memory to reconstruct multiple streams (observations, actions, proprioception) of past information, as well as estimate current task progress, followed by end-to-end finetuning, jointly with the policy, on action prediction. Across both simulation and real-world tasks demanding memory, IMPRINT improves average success over the base VLA by at least 26%, outperforming popular alternatives, and maintaining lowest inference latency among memory-augmented VLAs. Through careful ablations, we demonstrate the utility of incorporating diverse information streams into robotic memory, validating the advantages of foregoing history curation. Project website: https://yilin-wu98.github.io/imprint_website/.

**Comment:** Matches criteria 1 and 7: IMPRINT augments VLA robot policies with incrementally updated multi-stream latent memory and two-stage training, improving average success by at least 26% over the base VLA on memory-demanding simulation and real-world tasks.

**Relevance:** 10
Back to [[topic](#topic-1)] [[top](#topics)]

<a id="1024-red-teaming-of-vision-language-action-models-via-quality-diversity-environment-generation"></a>

### 1024\. [Red-Teaming of Vision-Language-Action Models via Quality Diversity Environment Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14415)
**ArXiv:** 2610.14415 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14415)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.14415.pdf)]

**Authors:** Shihan Zhao, Henry Chen, Ya-Chuan Hsu, ..., Stefanos Nikolaidis, Daniel Seita

**Abstract:** A long-standing goal in robotics is to develop generalist robots that can perform tasks across a wide range of settings, flexibly specified with images and text. Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models offer a promising path toward this goal by training on large collections of robot demonstrations. However, while VLAs perform well in-distribution, they often fail under minor test-time shifts, such as changes in object positions, lighting, textures, and camera poses. To address this, we propose Quality Diversity for Diverse Environment Generation (Q-DEG), a method for improving VLA robustness by generating adversarial environments and fine-tuning the VLA on its own successful rollouts. Q-DEG uses Quality Diversity (QD) optimization to find a set of high-failure environments, while promoting diversity using policy embeddings extracted from the VLA. These environments expose vulnerabilities that can be mitigated through self-imitation fine-tuning, thereby improving the VLA's robustness. In simulation, we conduct experiments across three representative VLA models, and our results indicate that Q-DEG improves robustness to out-of-distribution (OOD) adversarial environments compared to the base VLA and VLAs fine-tuned using data collected from two red-teaming baselines. Furthermore, we demonstrate that improving VLA robustness through Q-DEG facilitates sim-to-real transfer. Our anonymous project website is at: https://qd-env.github.io/qdeg/.

**Comment:** Matches criteria 1 and 7: Q-DEG uses quality-diversity optimization and VLA policy embeddings to generate adversarial robot environments, then improves robustness through self-imitation fine-tuning, with evaluation across three VLA models and demonstrated sim-to-real transfer.

**Relevance:** 10
Back to [[topic](#topic-1)] [[top](#topics)]

<a id="1025-flow-support-weighted-proposals-for-test-time-verified-vla-policies"></a>

### 1025\. [Flow-Support-Weighted Proposals for Test-Time Verified VLA Policies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14588)
**ArXiv:** 2610.14588 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14588)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.14588.pdf)]

**Authors:** Chen Zhao, Zipeng Ye, Haitao Shen, ..., Yuchen Xie, Jing Zhang

**Abstract:** Test-time sampling promises to turn one action prediction into a set of distinct executable candidates that a verifier can choose among. Downstream fine-tuning works against that promise. A vision-language-action model trained to solve one task distribution concentrates its flow-matching decoder around the dominant demonstrated action, so repeated samples differ only marginally and the verifier receives many copies of one decision. We call this proposal contraction and identify it as a key constraint on test-time scaling: repeated samples can remain highly redundant as the candidate budget grows. Action Dynamic Sampling (ADS) alleviates the constraint on the proposal side, during training. At each training step, ADS integrates $K$ additional trajectories from the current policy under fresh base noise, scores each by the flow support along its path with a Hutchinson trace estimator, and corrects the policy's local action distribution toward this weighted set through a detached finite-set KL term. Training cost rises; deployment cost is unchanged. Across simulation and four physical-robot tasks, ADS raises proposal spread without the success penalty that Gaussian perturbation incurs, lifts oracle best-of-8 success on RoboCasa from 57.6% to 63.0%, improves fixed verifiers by 2.4 to 4.9 points in simulation, and raises TokenPB by up to 13.0 points on real-world manipulation.

**Comment:** Criterion 1: Action Dynamic Sampling improves flow-matching vision-language-action policies for robotic manipulation through flow-support-weighted proposal training, raising RoboCasa oracle best-of-8 success from 57.6% to 63.0% and real-world TokenPB performance by up to 13.0 points.

**Relevance:** 10
Back to [[topic](#topic-1)] [[top](#topics)]

<a id="1026-pilot-guiding-long-horizon-world-model-planning-for-robot-manipulation-with-visual-subgoals-and-policy-priors"></a>

### 1026\. [PILOT: Guiding Long-Horizon World-Model Planning for Robot Manipulation with Visual Subgoals and Policy Priors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14335)
**ArXiv:** 2610.14335 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14335)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.14335.pdf)]

**Authors:** Yang Liu, Hanchen Cui, Karthik Desingh

**Abstract:** Latent world-model planning has shown promising results in goal-conditioned robotic manipulation, but remains challenging for long-horizon planning due to limited temporal guidance and inefficient action search. A distant visual goal provides little guidance for preparatory actions, while uninformed action sampling makes local optimization inefficient. We propose PILOT (Policy-Initialized Local Optimization with Temporal subgoals), which uses a video model to generate intermediate visual subgoals and a learned policy to initialize the cross-entropy method (CEM). CEM refines candidate action sequences toward each subgoal using an action-conditioned world model in a receding-horizon control loop. In our primary real-robot evaluation across four UR5e manipulation tasks, PILOT achieves 68.8% success, compared with 55.0% without policy initialization, 13.8% for policy-only execution, and 0.0% for direct final-goal planning. Simulation results show that these gains persist over longer horizons: PILOT exceeds direct execution of the same policy by 30 percentage points on long Push-T tasks and by 22 percentage points on LIBERO-10. Policy initialization also accelerates local optimization: on Push-T, CEM matches the cost reached by generic initialization after 10 iterations in only 4 iterations and reduces the final planning cost by 24.1% under the same sampling budget. These results show that combining temporal subgoals with policy-guided action search improves both long-horizon task execution and local planning efficiency. Videos are available on the webpage: https://pilot-planner.github.io/.

**Comment:** Criterion 1: PILOT combines video-generated visual subgoals, policy-initialized CEM, and an action-conditioned world model for robotic manipulation, achieving 68.8% success across four real-robot UR5e tasks.

**Relevance:** 10
Back to [[topic](#topic-1)] [[top](#topics)]

<a id="1027-dexcast-learning-real-world-dexterous-control-from-human-videos"></a>

### 1027\. [DexCast: Learning Real-World Dexterous Control from Human Videos](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14691)
**ArXiv:** 2610.14691 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14691)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.14691.pdf)]

**Authors:** Zihan Gao, Yuhong Zhang, Mengzhe Wang, ..., Zhifang Liu, Haoqian Wang

**Abstract:** Recent advances in 3D hand-object interaction (HOI) perception have opened new opportunities for learning dexterous robotic skills from human videos. However, existing dexterous imitation learning methods often rely on costly and manually teleoperated robot demonstrations, requiring substantial human effort. Therefore, we build DEXCAST, a framework that learns dexterous robotic hand control directly from monocular RGB-D videos by leveraging joint HOI reconstructions, without requiring any teleoperation. Furthermore, how to model high-dimensional robotic hand motion and extract temporal context to inform future action generation remains a key challenge. To this end, we propose a DexHand Motion Perception Module (DMPM) to capture inter-joint coordination and temporal dependencies in compact latent representations that condition our diffusion policy. Extensive real-world experiments demonstrate that DEXCAST achieves exceptional performance across diverse dexterous manipulation tasks.

**Comment:** Criterion 1: DexCast conditions a diffusion policy on compact hand-motion representations extracted from human RGB-D videos, demonstrating real-world dexterous manipulation without teleoperated robot demonstrations.

**Relevance:** 10
Back to [[topic](#topic-1)] [[top](#topics)]

<a id="1028-bridgewam-bridging-video-and-action-experts-via-latent-bridge-queries"></a>

### 1028\. [BridgeWAM: Bridging Video and Action Experts via Latent Bridge Queries](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14746)
**ArXiv:** 2610.14746 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14746)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.14746.pdf)]

**Authors:** Jiaxin Fang, Ruitong Liu, Zeyu Lai, ..., Mengdi Xu, Yuxiang Gao

**Abstract:** World Action Models (WAMs) commonly couple video and action experts through dense, layer-wise Mixture-of-Transformers (MoT) attention, exposing action prediction to irrelevant visual features while enforcing matched expert depths. This coupling hinders selective visual-feature use and introduces redundant computation. We introduce Bridge-of-Experts (BoE), instantiated as BridgeWAM, which aggregates visual, language, and proprioceptive priors through 32 Latent Bridge Queries (LBQs) into a shared, limited latent space. Joint video-action supervision lets action gradients guide the Video Expert toward representations better suited for action prediction, while the Action Expert learns to selectively retrieve task-relevant information through cross-attention. Decoupling expert depths and reusing LBQs during action denoising yield a 5.06B model with a 30-layer Video Expert and a two-layer Action Expert, achieving 98.25% success on LIBERO and 72.24% pooled success on LIBERO-Plus, demonstrating effective task execution and strong generalization with an ultralight action head. Ablations support joint representation adaptation and unified LBQ conditioning. Relative to Fast-WAM, BridgeWAM reduces single-sample inference FLOPs by 28.72% in simulation profiling and real-robot latency to 77.1 ms (a 2.47-fold speedup). Qualitative experimental results further indicate preferential attention to target objects and task destinations across perturbations.

**Comment:** Criterion 1: BridgeWAM uses 32 Latent Bridge Queries and joint video–action supervision to connect video and action experts for robotic manipulation, achieving 98.25% success on LIBERO and 72.24% on LIBERO-Plus.

**Relevance:** 10
Back to [[topic](#topic-1)] [[top](#topics)]

<a id="1029-deft-vla-stage-localized-force-specialization-for-contact-rich-robotic-manipulation"></a>

### 1029\. [DEFT-VLA: Stage-Localized Force Specialization for Contact-Rich Robotic Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14854)
**ArXiv:** 2610.14854 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14854)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.14854.pdf)]

**Authors:** Zan Huang, Yibo Qiu, Kan Ding, ..., Ronald X Xu, Mingzhai Sun

**Abstract:** Contact-rich manipulation poses a stage-dependent sensory conflict: vision and proprioception are essential for localization and approach, when force is largely uninformative and potentially distracting, whereas force becomes critical after contact, when irrelevant visual details and correlated robot states may become shortcuts. We propose DEFT-VLA (Dual-Expert Force-specialized Training for Vision-Language-Action Manipulation), which retains the task-adapted base expert for vision-dominated non-contact behavior and introduces a force-aware contact expert for critical contact stages, localizing force-conditioned adaptation to where it is needed. We further introduce a contact-localized force-attending curriculum that applies force-attending post-training only to the contact expert, progressively restoring vision and masking state tokens to suppress shortcuts. Real-robot experiments on pipette tip attachment, bimanual pipette aspiration, and whiteboard wiping show that DEFT-VLA effectively exploits force feedback to improve contact-stage performance while maintaining strong non-contact behavior and robustness to held-out grasp heights. It improves overall task success by 20-40 percentage points over the strongest baseline on each task.

**Comment:** Criterion 1: DEFT-VLA specializes a vision-language-action policy with a force-aware contact expert and contact-localized training curriculum, improving real-robot manipulation success by 20–40 percentage points over the strongest baseline.

**Relevance:** 10
Back to [[topic](#topic-1)] [[top](#topics)]

<a id="1030-prism-precision-forcing-for-generalist-vision-language-action-policies"></a>

### 1030\. [PRISM: Precision Forcing for Generalist Vision-Language-Action Policies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14704)
**ArXiv:** 2610.14704 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14704)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.14704.pdf)]

**Authors:** Chenghao Yue, Peiyan Li, Hao Wang, ..., Yu Luo, Yuanlong Zhang

**Abstract:** Coupling a pretrained vision-language model (VLM) with a diffusion or flow-matching action expert has become the mainstream vision-language-action (VLA) paradigm for embodied policies, yet they remain unreliable once a task demands precise manipulation, e.g., submillimeter cable insertion. We attribute this weakness to a training objective that averages flow-matching errors across contexts and flow times, allocating little supervision to the few decisive moments determining task success. Along the generative path, inference integrates a locally supervised velocity field, so equally penalized errors at different flow times can affect the executed action unequally. Consequently, precision-task performance deteriorates as physical tolerance tightens by orders of magnitude. We call this supervision imbalance across contexts and flow times Flow-Time Starvation. The remedy is to jointly align supervision allocation and representation learning with context-dependent precision demand, enabling the policy to recognize when greater precision is needed. We instantiate this precision-forcing strategy as Precision-aware Reweighting and Internalization for Skilled Manipulation (PRISM), which scores precision demand offline, strengthens flow supervision where errors are consequential and action signals are visible, and internalizes that demand in the VLM condition representation. Simulation and real-robot experiments show that PRISM preserves or improves general-task performance while raising mean end-to-end success from 38.3% to 86.7% across drive connector insertion, bimanual connector mating, and screw tightening, substantially extending the precision boundary of existing embodied policies without new sensors or observation modalities.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 1: PRISM improves VLM-conditioned generative robot policies through precision-aware flow supervision and representation learning, raising mean success from 38.3% to 86.7% across three precision-manipulation tasks.

**Relevance:** 10
Back to [[topic](#topic-1)] [[top](#topics)]

<a id="1031-cag-wam-causal-action-gain-world-action-models-for-robot-manipulation"></a>

### 1031\. [CAG-WAM: Causal Action-Gain World Action Models for Robot Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14720)
**ArXiv:** 2610.14720 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14720)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.14720.pdf)]

**Authors:** Peizheng Guo, Cheng Zhang, Xingyu Zhang, Changwen Zheng, Wenwen Qiang

**Abstract:** World Action Models (WAMs) combine action generation with future prediction, allowing predicted futures to support action evaluation and robot decision making. However, the future associated with a candidate action may contain action-induced and environment-driven changes. Directly evaluating the predicted future may not fully characterize the future change associated with the candidate action. For example, a candidate action that worsens a favorable environmental future may still yield a favorable outcome, while one that improves an unfavorable environmental future may still yield an unfavorable outcome. To analyze this issue, we formulate a Structural Causal Model (SCM) for action evaluation in WAMs. The SCM formalizes how action-induced and environment-driven changes jointly shape future evaluation, and reveals a backdoor path through which environmental evolution can also affect the future evaluation associated with a candidate action. Intervention and backdoor adjustment account for this path, providing a causal basis for characterizing the future change associated with each candidate action. Based on this analysis, we propose Causal Action-Gain World Action Model (CAG-WAM). For each candidate action, CAG-WAM predicts an action-conditioned future and an environmental future under the same current observation, and defines their evaluation difference as causal action gain (CAG) for characterizing the future change associated with the candidate action. During training, we incorporate CAG into Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) to guide robot policy learning. During inference, we evaluate the initial action with CAG and, when needed, revise it using predicted future information. Experiments on multiple benchmarks support the effectiveness of CAG-WAM.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 1: CAG-WAM improves world-action models for robot manipulation by comparing action-conditioned and environmental futures to compute causal action gain, which guides GRPO policy learning and inference-time action revision.

**Relevance:** 10
Back to [[topic](#topic-1)] [[top](#topics)]

<a id="1032-verify-act-distill-turning-test-time-compute-into-test-time-adaptation-for-vision-language-action-models"></a>

### 1032\. [Verify, Act, Distill: Turning Test-Time Compute into Test-Time Adaptation for Vision-Language-Action Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14885)
**ArXiv:** 2610.14885 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14885)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.14885.pdf)]

**Authors:** Kaneyoshi Hiratsuka, Satoshi Yamamori, Jun Morimoto

**Abstract:** Test-time compute (TTC), which scores multiple candidate actions at inference time, is a powerful way to improve the execution performance of vision-language-action (VLA) models, but existing methods keep the policy frozen, so the gain must be recomputed at every execution. We propose Verify, Act, Distill, a framework that consolidates the gains of TTC into the VLA itself. A fixed verifier scores candidates generated by a flow-matching VLA, the weighted aggregate is executed, and the observations and aggregated chunks from successful adaptation episodes are distilled directly into LoRA parameters with the standard flow-matching loss. This reuses supervision that TTC has already produced during execution, without additional reinforcement learning or rollouts dedicated to teacher generation. On the 40 LIBERO tasks with a SmolVLA 5k checkpoint, distilling from only 10 episodes per task raises the mean success rate of verifier-free single-candidate generation from 54.1% to 70.6%, retaining about 74% of the improvement obtained by aggregating eight candidates. Applying the same aggregation to the distilled policy further raises the success rate from 76.4% to 82.3%, with a particularly large gain on LIBERO-Long (44.0% to 57.8%), and the single-candidate policy also improves on LIBERO-plus, which contains distribution shifts. These results show that using verification as a learning signal, rather than only as an execution-time selector, both retains the improvements of TTC and re-amplifies the effect of subsequent TTC.

**Comment:** Criterion 1: Verify, Act, Distill improves a flow-matching vision-language-action policy by distilling verifier-guided successful executions into LoRA parameters, raising verifier-free success on 40 LIBERO manipulation tasks from 54.1% to 70.6%.

**Relevance:** 10
Back to [[topic](#topic-1)] [[top](#topics)]

<a id="1034-cfg-policy-unified-diffusion-policy-learning-framework-via-classifier-free-guidance"></a>

### 1034\. [CFG-Policy: Unified Diffusion Policy Learning Framework via Classifier-free Guidance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14936)
**ArXiv:** 2610.14936 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14936)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.14936.pdf)]

**Authors:** Shutong Ding, Zhaoyang Liu, Yimiao Zhou, ..., Jiayuan Gu, Ye Shi

**Abstract:** Diffusion policies are increasingly used in robotic control and manipulation, vision-language-action models, and world-model-based agents. Their ability to represent multimodal action distributions offers rich opportunities for exploration in reinforcement learning (RL). However, optimizing diffusion policies with RL can reduce the behavioral diversity and further limit their exploration capability. In RL post-training, directly fine-tuning a pretrained policy may also disrupt its acquired capabilities, while action-space residuals may lack the expressive power for multimodal exploration. Together, these issues reveal a common challenge across online and offline-to-online diffusion RL: improving task performance while retaining the behavioral diversity and capabilities needed for continued adaptation. We introduce CFG-Policy, a unified, plug-and-play framework that separates exploration and exploitation into two generative policies and combines their score or velocity fields during sampling through classifier-free guidance. For online RL, a frozen exploration branch sustains behavioral diversity while an exploitation branch learns from task rewards; for offline-to-online RL, a pretrained exploitation branch remains frozen while a trainable branch steers the composed behavior. CFG-Policy preserves the underlying RL objectives and improves performance with QSM and QVPO on 5 MuJoCo tasks and with Flow-SDE+PPO on 4 RoboTwin manipulation tasks. Project page is at https://zhy-liu.github.io/cfg-policy-page.

**Comment:** Criterion 1: CFG-Policy combines exploration and exploitation diffusion policies through classifier-free guidance, improving Flow-SDE+PPO on four RoboTwin manipulation tasks.

**Relevance:** 10
Back to [[topic](#topic-1)] [[top](#topics)]

<a id="1035-compositional-average-velocity-modeling-for-efficient-diffusion-offline-reinforcement-learning"></a>

### 1035\. [Compositional Average Velocity Modeling for Efficient Diffusion Offline Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14941)
**ArXiv:** 2610.14941 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14941)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.14941.pdf)]

**Authors:** Thanh Nguyen, Abu Hanif Muhammad Syarubany, Hwanhee Kim, Chang D. Yoo

**Abstract:** Diffusion-based policies have recently emerged as a powerful paradigm for offline reinforcement learning, but their reliance on multi-step denoising introduces substantial computational overhead and requires backpropagation through time during training, limiting scalability and practical deployment. In this work, we propose \textbf{Instant Flow Q-Learning (InsQL)}, a single-step generative policy learning framework that retains the expressive capacity of diffusion models while eliminating iterative sampling and BPTT. InsQL builds on the flow-matching perspective of diffusion policies, but replaces marginal velocity parameterization with \emph{average-velocity} modeling and introduces a principled compositional mechanism, yielding a theoretically grounded objective that learns average velocity consistently across scales. This formulation provides a direct noise-to-action mapping and enables single-step action generation. InsQL further unifies compositional average-velocity modeling with behavior-regularized actor--critic optimization in a single framework, requiring only a single policy network and avoiding distillation, multi-stage procedures, and Jacobian computations during training. At inference time, actions are generated in a single forward pass and can be refined via value-guided candidate selection to improve performance. Extensive experiments on standard offline RL benchmarks and real-world robotic manipulation tasks show that InsQL matches or surpasses state-of-the-art diffusion-based methods while substantially reducing both training and inference time. Overall, these results establish compositional average-velocity modeling as an efficient and scalable alternative to multi-step generative policies for offline RL.

**Comment:** Criterion 1: InsQL uses compositional average-velocity modeling for single-step generative policy learning, with experiments including real-world robotic manipulation showing competitive performance and reduced training and inference time.

**Relevance:** 10
Back to [[topic](#topic-1)] [[top](#topics)]

<a id="1037-cola-multi-vla-coordination-via-learned-adapters"></a>

### 1037\. [CoLA: Multi-VLA Coordination via Learned Adapters](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15337)
**ArXiv:** 2610.15337 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15337)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.15337.pdf)]

**Authors:** Ahaskar Kashyap, Jiawen Zong, Boyu Ma, ..., Stefano V. Albrecht, Jianfei Yang

**Abstract:** Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models are typically deployed as isolated single-agent policies. When several such agents share a workspace, they can coordinate implicitly, by observing one another, or explicitly, by exchanging messages, but it remains unclear when explicit communication becomes necessary. We introduce CoLA (Coordination via Learned Adapters), which keeps each agent's VLA backbone frozen and trains a lightweight message channel and a per-arm diffusion action head on top, with fully decentralised execution. Across three tasks that differ in how much each agent can observe, the channel matters only when information is private. On an occluded tray handover, where the box's colour marker is visible to one agent only, CoLA reaches 76.0% success versus 1.3% with messages zeroed throughout training. On two-arm and three-arm handover tasks, removing messages has no measurable effect with a shared overhead camera (90.6% vs. 88.3% at two arms; 63.5% vs. 60.0% at three), but lowers success on the two-arm task's wrist-only variant (74.8% vs. 64.3%). This architecture generalises across different VLA backbones as well. With a frozen $π_{0.5}$ backbone on the same occluded tray task, CoLA matches the overall success rate of a LoRA-fine-tuned baseline (37.3% vs. 34.7%) using a fraction of the trainable parameters. When CoLA places the box, it selects the correct tray 71.8% of the time, whereas the independently fine-tuned baseline, whose placing arm never sees the marker, selects it at chance level (34.7% against 33.3%).

**Comment:** Criterion 1: CoLA combines frozen VLA backbones with learned communication adapters and per-arm diffusion action heads for multi-robot manipulation, achieving 76.0% success on an occluded tray handover versus 1.3% with messages zeroed throughout training.

**Relevance:** 10
Back to [[topic](#topic-1)] [[top](#topics)]

<a id="1038-proworld-multi-view-world-model-with-proprioceptive-feedback-for-closed-loop-policy-evaluation"></a>

### 1038\. [ProWorld: Multi-View World Model with Proprioceptive Feedback for Closed-Loop Policy Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15294)
**ArXiv:** 2610.15294 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15294)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.15294.pdf)]

**Authors:** Zijun Wang, Tianyi Yan, Haokun Lin, ..., Hanhui Li, Xiaodan Liang

**Abstract:** Evaluating robot manipulation policies through repeated physical trials is costly. We study whether an action-conditioned world model can serve as a closed-loop test environment whose policy success rates track those on real robots. Beyond realistic video, the model must return the camera views and proprioceptive state needed for the policy's next action and preserve failed-action outcomes to avoid overestimating policy capability. We introduce ProWorld, a world model that jointly predicts multi-view video and robot state, then feeds both back to a frozen policy for repeated interaction. ProWorld comprises a shared transformer for joint video-state flow prediction and Shared-Time View Encoding to distinguish cameras while preserving temporal synchronization. To reduce interaction hallucinations that turn missed grasps into successful lifts, we augment training with failed interactions collected in high-fidelity simulation, reducing inflated success rates in the generated environment. ProWorld achieves the highest overall TriWorldBench score among the compared methods. Across three policies and four local manipulation tasks, it attains a Pearson correlation of 0.95 and a Spearman correlation of 0.94 between generated and real success rates. These results support joint visual-proprioceptive feedback and failure-aware training as a basis for world-model-based policy evaluation.

**Comment:** Matches criteria 1 and 7 by introducing a world model with joint multi-view video and proprioceptive feedback for closed-loop manipulation-policy evaluation, achieving Pearson 0.95 and Spearman 0.94 correlations with real-robot success rates.

**Relevance:** 10
Back to [[topic](#topic-1)] [[top](#topics)]

<a id="1039-rm-wam-reliable-memory-augmented-world-action-models-for-long-horizon-robot-manipulation"></a>

### 1039\. [RM-WAM: Reliable Memory-Augmented World Action Models for Long-Horizon Robot Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15305)
**ArXiv:** 2610.15305 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15305)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.15305.pdf)]

**Authors:** Cui Miao, Junhao Yu, Ping Liu, ..., Xiaodong Wang, Tao Zhang

**Abstract:** World Action Models (WAMs) improve robotic manipulation by jointly predicting future visual states and actions, yet most existing methods make decisions primarily from the current observation. This limits their reliability in long-horizon tasks, where visually similar states may correspond to different interaction histories and task stages. Moreover, directly incorporating historical observations can introduce stale, stage-mismatched, or error-contaminated information, leading to inaccurate world prediction and accumulated closed-loop errors. We propose RM-WAM, a reliable memory-augmented World Action Model that treats memory as a context-dependent predictive resource rather than universally useful historical context. RM-WAM represents history using complementary short-term visual state memory and latent event memory, which respectively capture recent interaction dynamics and longer-term state transitions. A counterfactual memory learning scheme varies the accessible history while keeping the current inputs and prediction targets fixed, enabling the model to learn a context-dependent utility estimate for historical memory. Based on this estimate, a utility-aware gating mechanism adaptively controls memory injection into the world-action backbone. Experiments demonstrate that RM-WAM highlights the importance of reliable and selective memory utilization in long-horizon tasks.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 1 through a World Action Model for long-horizon robot manipulation that uses counterfactual memory learning and utility-aware gating to selectively incorporate short-term visual state memory and latent event memory.

**Relevance:** 10
Back to [[topic](#topic-1)] [[top](#topics)]

<a id="1042-harnessing-vlm-in-embodied-rl-from-reward-regressor-to-success-detector-via-exploration-augmented-key-observation-gating"></a>

### 1042\. [Harnessing VLM in Embodied RL: From Reward Regressor to Success Detector via Exploration-Augmented Key-Observation Gating](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15401)
**ArXiv:** 2610.15401 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15401)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.15401.pdf)]

**Authors:** Xin Liu, Yutong Jiang, Jian Su, ..., Haoran Li, Dongbin Zhao

**Abstract:** VLMs provide promising solutions to embodied reward design. Most current methods that train VLMs as reward regressors not only require extensive annotations but are also primarily designed for finetuning pretrained policies, and perform less well in the more challenging from-scratch RL setting. This issue stems from a fundamental mismatch: regressing precise rewards is misaligned with what VLMs naturally excel at. We posit that it is more aligned with VLMs' inherent multi-modal VQA capabilities for the models to function as success detectors, and propose VLM success reward via Exploration-Augmented Key-Observation Gating (VLMeg) to well harness VLM for embodied reward design. As the core of VLMeg, the proposed exploration-augmented key-observation gating first determines whether the current RL episode is judgment-worthy based on its similarity to a generated success trajectory. Then, the key observation with the lowest confusion from the judgment-worthy episode will be selected for VLM evaluation as episodic reward. This gating mechanism not only avoids redundant calls but also enables higher detection accuracy of VLM. Considering the sparsity of judgment-worthy episodes, the advanced exploration incentives based on the generated trajectory are introduced, enabling more efficient exploration toward judgment-worthy trajectories for more VLM reward triggers and thus gaining higher RL efficiency. Extensive experiments in MetaWorld demonstrate VLMeg achieves state-of-the-art from-scratch RL, outperforming advanced VLM-based baselines on 12 challenging reward-free tasks. Furthermore, VLMeg also enables effective RL without any ground-truth reward in 3 challenging real-world experiments. Code will be open after acceptance at https://github.com/Raina915/VLMeg.

**Comment:** Criterion 1: VLMeg uses exploration-augmented key-observation gating to turn VLM success judgments into rewards for from-scratch robotic manipulation learning, outperforming VLM-based baselines on 12 MetaWorld tasks and enabling learning without ground-truth rewards in three real-world experiments.

**Relevance:** 10
Back to [[topic](#topic-1)] [[top](#topics)]

<a id="1043-look-before-you-move-agent-interface-aligned-supervision-for-open-world-mobile-manipulation"></a>

### 1043\. [Look Before You Move: Agent-Interface-Aligned Supervision for Open-World Mobile Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15572)
**ArXiv:** 2610.15572 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15572)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.15572.pdf)]

**Authors:** Ronghe Qiu, Teli Ma, Jiaming Zhou, ..., Qiang Huang, Junwei Liang

**Abstract:** Open-world mobile manipulation requires a robot to find an object, approach it, and move it to a target receptacle from a language instruction. Training vision-language model (VLM) agents for these tasks requires demonstrations of both action selection and active vision. Existing pipelines often convert planned trajectories into high-level action labels, but shortest-path progress provides limited supervision for deciding where to look. We introduce Agent-Interface Data Alignment (AIDA), a data collection pipeline in which a teacher directly selects and executes actions through the deployed agent's skill interface. For mobile manipulation, we design teachers that maintain target visibility, recover lost targets, and approach for picking or placing. We add template-based spatial reasoning supervision and use a shared harness to organize observations, history, and outputs during training and inference. The resulting agent, AIDA-OWMM, achieves 71.76% full-task success on 170 held-out OWMM episodes. Teacher sensitivity analysis shows that active-vision demonstrations improve student success.
