The simulation-trained agent achieves 38% end-to-end success on a real mobile robot without additional training. Applying AIDA to a navigation subset of EmbodiedBench further demonstrates the pipeline's utility for navigation tasks.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 1: AIDA trains VLM agents for language-instructed mobile manipulation using agent-interface-aligned demonstrations of action selection and active vision, achieving 71.76% held-out OWMM success and 38% real-robot end-to-end success without additional training.

**Relevance:** 10
Back to [[topic](#topic-1)] [[top](#topics)]

<a id="1045-recovering-low-bit-failures-in-vision-language-action-policies-via-quantization-conditioned-reinforcement-learning"></a>

### 1045\. [Recovering Low-Bit Failures in Vision-Language-Action Policies via Quantization-Conditioned Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15675)
**ArXiv:** 2610.15675 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15675)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.15675.pdf)]

**Authors:** Seongmin Park, Jaewon Lee, Yujun Heo, ..., Hyoung-rock Kim, Jungwook Choi

**Abstract:** Quantization lowers deployment costs of vision-language-action (VLA) policies but can compromise task completion. Across five VLA models and 4,050 real-world trials, we systematically reveal that quantization-aware training (QAT) generally improves on post-training quantization (PTQ) but leaves substantial gaps to full precision (FP) on long-horizon tasks and under visual and semantic shifts, while its imitation-based objective does not explicitly use rollout success or partial completion. To exploit these outcome signals, we introduce Quantization-Conditioned Reinforcement Learning (QCRL), an offline RL method coupling progress-based advantage estimation with quantization-aware source conditioning. FP rollouts supply reference experience beyond action-level distillation, progress-scaled terminal penalty distinguishes partial completion, and quantization-aware conditioning selects advantageous reference behavior within the quantized policy. We evaluate weight-only INT4 W4A16 on $π_{0.5}$ and GR00T N1.5 and joint weight-activation NVFP4 W4A4 on $π_{0.5}$. Using only in-distribution data, QCRL reaches 99.3% of FP's mean score across three visual-shift conditions for $π_{0.5}$ with INT4 W4A16, alongside 42.9% model-memory savings. With NVFP4 W4A4, it reaches 100.7% of the same FP baseline, alongside a $1.73\times$ speedup using NVFP4/FP8 deployment. Hardware gains are relative to FP16 on Jetson Thor.

**Comment:** Criterion 1: QCRL improves quantized VLA robot policies using progress-based offline reinforcement learning and quantization-aware source conditioning, recovering 99.3% of the full-precision mean score for INT4 π₀.₅ across three visual-shift conditions.

**Relevance:** 10
Back to [[topic](#topic-1)] [[top](#topics)]

<a id="1046-streamingwam-streaming-world-action-modeling-via-chunk-wise-staircase-flow-matching"></a>

### 1046\. [StreamingWAM: Streaming World Action Modeling via Chunk-Wise Staircase Flow Matching](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15585)
**ArXiv:** 2610.15585 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15585)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.15585.pdf)]

**Authors:** Shaojie Ren, Jingwen Sun, Zezhi Liu, ..., Wei Zou, Zhibo Chen

**Abstract:** World Action Models (WAMs), often built upon pretrained video-generation world models, have emerged as a promising framework for general-purpose and open-ended robot control. However, their real-world deployment often suffers from discontinuous and jerky execution. Existing methods typically address this issue by either improving inference efficiency or enhancing action continuity across prediction chunks, without jointly addressing both challenges. To bridge this gap, we propose StreamingWAM, a streaming-diffusion framework for WAM-based robot control. StreamingWAM combines a noise ladder for chunk-wise action prediction, a prefix rollout module for cross-chunk continuity, and dense video-history memory for temporally consistent conditioning. Experiments on LIBERO, LIBERO-Plus, RoboTwin 2.0, and real-world robotic tasks demonstrate that StreamingWAM improves action continuity across static and dynamic settings, achieving competitive performance while reducing steady-state replanning latency to 72.9 ms in simulation and 57.3 ms on the real robot. The method also produces smoother action transitions, while the streaming staircase reduces sensitivity to the execution horizon K.

**Comment:** Criterion 1: StreamingWAM applies streaming diffusion to world-action-model robot control, combining chunk-wise prediction and prefix rollouts to improve action continuity while achieving 57.3 ms steady-state replanning latency on a real robot.

**Relevance:** 10
Back to [[topic](#topic-1)] [[top](#topics)]

<a id="1047-1step-wam-your-world-action-model-but-faster-using-1-step-action-generation"></a>

### 1047\. [1Step-WAM: Your World Action Model but Faster using 1-Step Action Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15625)
**ArXiv:** 2610.15625 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15625)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.15625.pdf)]

**Authors:** Rundong Su, Pengtao Chen, Haohan Geng, ..., Hao Huang, Tao Chen

**Abstract:** World Action Models (WAMs) unify visual world modeling and action generation, yet their real-world deployment is heavily bottlenecked by iterative action denoising and the sequential execution of visual prefilling and action prediction. We demonstrate that the action-denoising trajectories in WAMs are approximately straight, rendering the first-step velocity a strong estimate of the final action direction. Building on this observation, we introduce 1Step-WAM, a framework that transforms multi-step action denoising into one single forward. Specifically, we employ a lightweight calibrator to directly correct the first step velocity, and use bandwidth-constrained action decoding to further reduce errors in the calibrated one-step actions. Compressing action generation into a single step enables it to execute in parallel with visual prefilling, completely breaking the sequential bottleneck of the traditional imagination-then-action paradigm. Evaluations on RoboTwin and LIBERO show that 1Step-WAM largely preserves multi-step task success with only about 13.4M trainable parameters, while achieving inference speedups of $7.44\times$, $5.98\times$, and $24.84\times$ for FastWAM, ImageWAM and Kairos, respectively. Furthermore, real-world robotic deployments using the FastWAM backbone confirm a consistent 7.44$\times$ speedup while maintaining close success rate to the multi-step baseline. Code is avaliable at https://github.com/changdong1687/1Step-WAM.

**Comment:** Criterion 1: 1Step-WAM accelerates robotic world action models through calibrated single-step action generation, largely preserving task success on RoboTwin and LIBERO and demonstrating a 7.44× speedup in real-world FastWAM deployments.

**Relevance:** 10
Back to [[topic](#topic-1)] [[top](#topics)]

<a id="1048-surgiclaw-an-interpretable-atomic-skill-agent-for-precision-robotic-micromanipulation"></a>

### 1048\. [SurgiCLAW: An Interpretable Atomic-Skill Agent for Precision Robotic Micromanipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15766)
**ArXiv:** 2610.15766 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15766)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.15766.pdf)]

**Authors:** Angen Ye, junjie xie, Yujia Song, Junkai Huang, Dapeng Zhang

**Abstract:** Recent multimodal large language models (MLLMs) and vision--language--action models have provided robots with new capabilities for long-horizon task understanding and planning. In surgical robotics, however, end-to-end prediction of low-level actions remains limited by insufficient precision, opaque decisions, and scarce data. This paper presents SurgiCLAW, a hierarchical skill-based framework for precision surgical manipulation. SurgiCLAW confines an MLLM to intraoperative visual understanding, structured explanation, and atomic surgical-skill selection, while delegating physical execution to verifiable deterministic control programs. For brain--machine-interface implantation, a representative high-precision surgical task, we construct a state-to-skill invocation dataset and expand it through visual and state perturbations. The model first learns structured skill invocation through supervised fine-tuning and is then optimized using three rewards for action correctness, operation prerequisites, and format validity, improving action selection, operational safety, and output parseability. We evaluate subtask success, offline model comparison, real-robot performance, and ablations. On a fixed test set, SurgiCLAW achieves 94.6% skill-selection accuracy and 93.0% macro-averaged F1 (Macro-F1). Across 50 closed-loop real-robot implantation trials, it achieves an 88.0% task success rate.

**Comment:** Criteria 1 and 7: SurgiCLAW combines MLLM-based visual understanding and atomic-skill selection with deterministic robotic execution, achieving 88.0% success across 50 closed-loop real-robot implantation trials.

**Relevance:** 10
Back to [[topic](#topic-1)] [[top](#topics)]

<a id="1049-flow-matching-for-robot-foundation-models"></a>

### 1049\. [$Δ$ Flow Matching for Robot Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15794)
**ArXiv:** 2610.15794 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15794)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.15794.pdf)]

**Authors:** Kuangji Zuo, Tuo An, Xinying Guo, ..., Jindou Jia, Jianfei Yang

**Abstract:** Flow matching is a common generative recipe for robot foundation models. Vision-language-action models (VLAs) generate action chunks, while world-action models (WAMs) additionally generate future visual states. Both start generation from a fixed Gaussian prior, despite temporal continuity between past and future states and actions. This requires costly training and iterative sampling, especially for WAMs with an additional visual branch. We introduce $Δ$-flow, which constructs the source from recent history with mild noise rather than from a fixed Gaussian prior. This moves the source closer to the target, reducing transport to a small residual $Δ$ that can be learned efficiently and enables single-step inference. We integrate $Δ$-flow into the VLA policy $π_{0.5}$ and the WAM policies ImageWAM and Fast-WAM. On LIBERO, ImageWAM reaches 90% success in one epoch rather than six, and Fast-WAM reaches 95% in two epochs rather than eight. With LoRA fine-tuning, $π_{0.5}$ exceeds its baseline's five-epoch performance after one epoch. Single-step inference gives $1.54\times$, $2.49\times$, and $4.45\times$ speedups on $π_{0.5}$, ImageWAM, and Fast-WAM, respectively. It preserves action smoothness, achieving a $4.5\times$ reduction in action jerk compared with one-step Gaussian sampling. $Δ$-flow reaches state-of-the-art generalization performance on LIBERO-Plus. Action-history sources can be applied to pretrained WAM policies without retraining. Replacing the Gaussian action source at inference largely preserves task success with a single sampling step and can slightly improve it. We further study how action representation and visual information in action learning affect history-to-future prediction in $Δ$-flow. These results show that $Δ$-flow is simple yet effective, improving the efficiency and generalization of robot foundation models.

**Comment:** Criterion 1: Δ-flow initializes VLA and world-action model generation from lightly noised recent history, enabling single-step robotic policies and reducing ImageWAM training to reach 90% LIBERO success from six epochs to one.

**Relevance:** 10
Back to [[topic](#topic-1)] [[top](#topics)]

<a id="1050-flowvla-enabling-world-dynamics-understanding-through-pluggable-3d-flow-learning"></a>

### 1050\. [FlowVLA: Enabling World Dynamics Understanding through Pluggable 3D Flow Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16160)
**ArXiv:** 2610.16160 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16160)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.16160.pdf)]

**Authors:** Junhyeong Go, Mingyu Jung, Woobin Im, Jongbin Ryu

**Abstract:** Recent advances in 3D reconstruction enable the extraction of temporally consistent 3D motion from RGB observations; however, current Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models still predominantly rely on 2D image-text priors. Therefore, they do not accurately represent the fundamental dynamics of the 3D world associated with physical actions. This over-reliance on static 2D visual cues limits the capacity of VLA models in achieving robust robot manipulation. To address these limitations, we introduce FlowVLA, a reconstruction-guided, pluggable 3D flow learning framework aimed at empowering VLA models with an inherent understanding of action-relevant dynamics. Instead of requiring explicit 3D sensor inputs or altering the inference-time architecture, our method is designed to utilize temporally aligned 3D flow only during training. This allows the model to learn the dynamic evolution of the physical scene while preserving the computational efficiency of conventional 2D VLA backbones. To bridge scene-level dynamics with robotic manipulation, we propose a modular 3D flow learning framework with dual-level flow supervision. Specifically, point-level flow guides the model to forecast the future displacements of task-critical 3D points, while TCP-level flow aggregates these motions to align with the robot's TCP trajectories. This dual-level flow supervision encourages the model to focus on semantically meaningful dynamic regions, effectively coupling scene-level motion with precise control signals. Extensive evaluations across simulation and real-world experiments demonstrate that FlowVLA consistently improves manipulation performance, zero-shot robustness, and optimizes data efficiency. FlowVLA adds only lightweight auxiliary heads during training and discards them at inference. Therefore, FlowVLA introduces no additional 3D sensor inputs, architectural modifications, or inference-time computation.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 1: FlowVLA improves vision-language-action models for robot manipulation using training-only point-level and TCP-level 3D flow supervision, with gains in manipulation performance, zero-shot robustness, and data efficiency without additional inference-time computation.

**Relevance:** 10
Back to [[topic](#topic-1)] [[top](#topics)]

<a id="1052-listen-before-act-audio-conditioned-robot-manipulation-with-listenvla"></a>

### 1052\. [Listen Before Act: Audio-Conditioned Robot Manipulation with ListenVLA](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16313)
**ArXiv:** 2610.16313 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16313)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.16313.pdf)]

**Authors:** Jiaqi Fan, Rui Xu, Jiakun Xia, ..., Yili Fu, Xu Li

**Abstract:** Vision-language-action (VLA) models have advanced robotic manipulation by connecting visual perception and language instructions to action generation. However, many manipulation tasks depend on auditory cues that cannot be reliably inferred from visual observations and may need to be elicited through physical interaction. To address this gap, we present ListenVLA, an audio-conditioned VLA policy built on GR00T N1.7 for sound-guided manipulation. ListenVLA combines semantic audio embeddings with waveform-derived acoustic cues over a causal audio history and conditions continuous action generation through audio tokens and feature modulation. Moreover, we introduce an audio-action binding objective that uses context-matched demonstrations to link auditory differences to demonstrated action differences. The objective supervises short-horizon differences between flow-velocity predictions under different audio conditions, with shared pure-noise action inputs and fixed non-audio observations, alongside full-chunk action supervision. Furthermore, we develop LIBERO-Audio, an eight-task benchmark covering sound-based target selection, responses to auditory events, and physical interactions that reveal acoustic evidence. Experiments on LIBERO-Audio and three real-world manipulation tasks show excellent performance. Ablation studies support the contributions of binding supervision and audio modulation, while audio swap tests show that sound influences action predictions. Our code and videos are available at https://anonymous.4open.science/r/listenvla-F18D

**Comment:** Criterion 1: ListenVLA extends the GR00T N1.7 vision-language-action policy with audio-conditioned action generation and an audio-action binding objective for robotic manipulation, evaluated on the eight-task LIBERO-Audio benchmark and three real-world tasks.

**Relevance:** 10
Back to [[topic](#topic-1)] [[top](#topics)]

<a id="1053-pint-a-unified-model-with-shared-predictive-intent-for-robot-manipulation"></a>

### 1053\. [PINT: A Unified Model with Shared Predictive Intent for Robot Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16355)
**ArXiv:** 2610.16355 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16355)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.16355.pdf)]

**Authors:** Xinyu Guo, Chao Jing, Li Yi, Xingyu Chen, Zhengxing Wu

**Abstract:** Generalist robot manipulation can benefit from pretrained vision-language models (VLMs) for semantic reasoning and video generation models (VGMs) for world modeling. However, existing unified models that directly fuse them through shared attention at multiple layers can make it harder to draw on their specialized capabilities. We introduce PINT, a unified model that coordinates semantic reasoning, world modeling, and action generation through a shared chunk-level Predictive Intent while preserving expert-specific pathways. PINT inserts learnable Intent Tokens into the VLM sequence to form this representation, conditioning both the VGM and an Action Expert (AE). It replaces only the VGM's prompt conditioning and retains the VGM at inference, where the AE reads its representations through one-way attention. Rather than prescribing what Predictive Intent should encode, PINT learns it jointly from the native prediction objectives of the VLM, VGM, and AE, which provide complementary views of the same upcoming task execution. Training uses two stages: Stage 1 freezes the VGM so that future video prediction shapes Predictive Intent with task-relevant future dynamics, while Stage 2 fixes the learned Intent and adapts the VGM and AE. Without embodied pretraining, PINT achieves strong in-distribution (ID) performance and substantially improves out-of-distribution (OOD) generalization, reaching 15.5% on RoboTwin 2.0-Hard compared with 1.8% for Fast-WAM and 3.2% for StarVLA, and 79.5% on LIBERO-Plus compared with 51.5% and 75.0%, respectively. Across five real-world bimanual tasks, PINT reaches 68% ID and 24% OOD success, compared with 35%/9% for pretrained Motus and 67%/35% for pretrained $π_{0.5}$.

**Comment:** Criterion 1: PINT coordinates a VLM, a video-generation world model, and an action expert through shared Predictive Intent tokens for robot manipulation, achieving 79.5% success on LIBERO-Plus and 15.5% on RoboTwin 2.0-Hard.

**Relevance:** 10
Back to [[topic](#topic-1)] [[top](#topics)]

<a id="1054-trust-calibrated-vla-policy-post-training-with-evolving-imagination"></a>

### 1054\. [Trust-Calibrated VLA Policy Post-Training with Evolving Imagination](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16517)
**ArXiv:** 2610.16517 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16517)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.16517.pdf)]

**Authors:** Wenzhan Li, Yiran Qin, Heng Zhou, ..., Yulan Guo, Ruimao Zhang

**Abstract:** Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models are often pretrained via imitation learning, making reinforcement learning (RL) post-training important when deployment moves beyond demonstration coverage. Existing refinement paradigms expose a reliability--efficiency tension: model-free refinement avoids simulator bias but is interaction-intensive, whereas model-based refinement can exploit unreliable imagination even as its simulator adapts. We propose Model-Evolving Reinforcement Learning (MERL), a trust-calibrated framework that couples policy optimization with an online-updated simulator comprising a world model and a reward proxy head. Across stages, simulator reliability controls the imagined-data ratio and rollout horizon; within rollouts, chunk-level trust controls replay selection and optimization weight. For recursive imagination without exact grounded continuations, a residual predictor estimates trust without querying future ground truth. On perturbed LIBERO-PRO tasks, MERL reaches 79.7% average success, improves high-threshold learning speed by 10.9% over the matched model-free control, and outperforms same-protocol external baselines across three training seeds. Fixed-data adaptation from 50 demonstrations per task achieves 80.0% average success on two physical-robot tasks.

**Comment:** Criterion 1: MERL post-trains vision-language-action robot policies using an online-updated world model and trust-calibrated imagined rollouts, achieving 79.7% average success on perturbed LIBERO-PRO and 80.0% on two physical-robot tasks.

**Relevance:** 10
Back to [[topic](#topic-1)] [[top](#topics)]

<a id="1055-what-limits-cross-task-generalization-in-world-action-models"></a>

### 1055\. [What Limits Cross-Task Generalization in World Action Models?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16641)
**ArXiv:** 2610.16641 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16641)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.16641.pdf)]

**Authors:** Chen Zhao, Zipeng Ye, Yihan Lin, Yuchen Xie, Jing Zhang

**Abstract:** Cross-task generalization remains difficult for world action models (WAMs), even when their predicted futures depict plausible manipulation. This gap can be attributed directly: we make this gap directly testable by decoupling a WAM into a world model and an inverse-dynamics model (IDM), then holding the IDM fixed while replacing the source of future images. We introduce Align Once, Evaluate Everywhere (AoE), a camera-space inverse-dynamics model pretrained on large-scale robot data, which provides this recovery interface for different future sources across tasks and environments. Through it, we find that actions recovered from recorded futures of unseen tasks execute almost as well as replayed demonstrations, whereas futures predicted by world models collapse across benchmarks. The failures are not generic: they concentrate on the gripper and interaction geometry while generation capacity is spent on task-irrelevant background. This diagnosis suggests that supervision should target exactly these regions. We therefore introduce the vision-to-action (VITA) loss, which passes generated futures through the frozen AoE and penalizes discrepancies in the recovered actions, redirecting generation toward action-relevant detail. Across two world models and three manipulation benchmarks, this simple change raises the execution success of generated futures from near zero to 47.0%. The same loss also transfers to WAMs and yields consistent gains in native control.

**Comment:** Criterion 1: Introduces the VITA loss, which uses a frozen AoE inverse-dynamics model to improve action-relevant future generation for robotic manipulation, raising execution success from near zero to 47.0% across two world models and three manipulation benchmarks.

**Relevance:** 10
Back to [[topic](#topic-1)] [[top](#topics)]

<a id="1057-mosaic-mode-and-support-aware-inference-time-control-of-robot-diffusion-models"></a>

### 1057\. [MOSAIC: Mode- and Support-Aware Inference-Time Control of Robot Diffusion Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17001)
**ArXiv:** 2610.17001 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17001)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.17001.pdf)]

**Authors:** Sabrina Bodmer, Rahel Rickenbach, René Zurbrügg, Melanie N. Zeilinger, Colin Jones

**Abstract:** Generative policies such as diffusion models provide expressive data-driven priors for robot behavior, yet the situations a robot faces at execution time rarely match its training data and impose constraints and objectives the policy never learned. Existing inference-time guidance methods either rely on gradients that can push samples away from the learned prior, or on sampling procedures that can be inefficient, collapse modes, or average them into out-of-distribution predictions. We introduce MOSAIC, a mode- and support-aware framework for gradient-free inference-time control of robotic diffusion models. MOSAIC treats the initial noise and all stochastic reverse-step perturbations as a joint input to the pretrained denoising process and adapts a distribution over this input using importance weights computed from fully denoised outputs. Physical feasibility and consistency with the learned support are balanced using adaptive penalties, while mode-wise importance-weighted updates preserve modes and avoid destructive averaging. The same formulation can jointly steer multiple independently trained diffusion models through shared coupling costs, enabling coordinated behavior without requiring a joint model. We evaluate MOSAIC on navigation, dexterous grasping, motion planning with unseen geometry, and collaborative box manipulation with independently trained robot priors. Across these settings, MOSAIC adapts and composes frozen diffusion priors under new deployment constraints without retraining.

**Comment:** Criterion 1: MOSAIC introduces gradient-free, mode- and support-aware inference-time control of frozen robot diffusion models, evaluated on dexterous grasping and collaborative box manipulation under new constraints without retraining.

**Relevance:** 10
Back to [[topic](#topic-1)] [[top](#topics)]

<a id="1059-vla-gauge-systematically-evaluating-generalization-and-robustness-in-vision-language-action-models"></a>

### 1059\. [VLA-GAUGE: Systematically Evaluating Generalization and Robustness in Vision-Language-Action Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16567)
**ArXiv:** 2610.16567 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16567)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.16567.pdf)]

**Authors:** Jun Xiao, Rui Ye, Dong Cao, ..., Ying Wang, Zhiliang Zhu

**Abstract:** Vision-Language-Action models (VLAs) have achieved high success rates on familiar manipulation tasks, yet it remains unclear whether such scores reflect genuine zero-shot generalization and perturbation robustness. Existing evaluation frameworks provide limited capability-level diagnosis and combinatorial perturbation coverage, often without connecting diagnosis to targeted model refinement. We introduce VLA-GAUGE, a diagnostic framework that unifies both evaluations under a shared executable task representation. It combines (1) paired in-distribution and out-of-distribution tasks across six capability suites, (2) single-factor sensitivity analysis with combinatorial perturbation screening and evaluation, and (3) diagnosis-driven refinement through targeted data augmentation and model fine-tuning. We evaluate 12 VLAs spanning different model sizes and action-modeling paradigms under a unified protocol. We find that (1) The mean Zero-Shot Score on VLA-GAUGE is only 22.3%, with SimpleVLA-RL scoring 8.0% and ranking second to last among the evaluated models. (2) Under three representative combinatorial perturbation configurations, the mean R-Score is 9.3. (3) Diagnosis-driven fine-tuning improves $π_{0.5}$'s zero-shot generalization and robustness under a selected perturbation combination on held-out scenarios by 72.4 and 31.8 scoring points, respectively; the latter exceeds the 10.9-point gain from LIBERO-Plus single-factor fine-tuning despite fewer successful training trajectories. These results show that high reported success rates on familiar manipulation tasks do not guarantee strong zero-shot generalization to unseen tasks or robustness to combinatorial perturbations, while diagnostic evaluation provides actionable signals for targeted model improvement.

**Comment:** Criteria 1 and 7: VLA-GAUGE benchmarks 12 vision-language-action models on robotic manipulation generalization and combinatorial perturbations, with diagnosis-driven fine-tuning improving π₀.₅’s held-out generalization and robustness scores by 72.4 and 31.8 points, respectively.

**Relevance:** 10
Back to [[topic](#topic-1)] [[top](#topics)]

<a id="1061-mars-systematic-multi-level-acceleration-for-world-action-models"></a>

### 1061\. [MARS: Systematic Multi-Level Acceleration for World Action Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17141)
**ArXiv:** 2610.17141 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17141)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.17141.pdf)]

**Authors:** Zengyuan Zuo, Yongchang Zhang, Guangyu Zhao, ..., Meng Fang, Yiwu Zhong

**Abstract:** World Action Models (WAMs) have emerged as a promising alternative for embodied intelligence by jointly predicting future world states and robot actions. However, their heavy visual encoding and iterative generative inference incur substantial latency, limiting their applicability to high-frequency closed-loop control. Existing training-free acceleration methods typically optimize a single stage, yielding limited speedups or degraded control under aggressive reuse. In this work, we systematically profile the inference overhead of WAMs and observe abundant redundancy across Transformer computation, diffusion denoising, and consecutive policy queries. However, the safe removal of such redundancy depends strongly on its impact on action prediction and control sensitivity. Based on these observations, we introduce \textbf{MARS}, a training-free and plug-and-play framework for multi-level WAM acceleration. MARS selectively removes low-impact attention computation, adaptively accelerates diffusion inference according to motion granularity, and speculatively reuses predicted future visual representations across consecutive queries with online verification. Together, these complementary mechanisms provide remarkable acceleration while limiting approximation error. Extensive experiments across LIBERO, LIBERO-PRO, and RoboCasa show that MARS achieves up to $4.00\times$, $3.94\times$, and $2.10\times$ end-to-end speedups, respectively, while maintaining task success rate close to the original policy. Moreover, MARS generalizes across different model backbones and across simulation and real-robot experiments, substantially improving the efficiency--performance trade-off of WAM inference. Our code is available at https://github.com/PKU-VaLuE-Lab/MARS.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 1 by accelerating diffusion-based World Action Models for robotic control through selective attention computation, adaptive denoising, and verified visual-representation reuse, achieving up to 4.00× speedup on LIBERO with near-baseline task success.

**Relevance:** 10
Back to [[topic](#topic-1)] [[top](#topics)]

<a id="1063-can-a-frontier-vlm-serve-as-a-robot-policy-for-memory-dependent-manipulation"></a>

### 1063\. [Can a Frontier VLM Serve as a Robot Policy for Memory-Dependent Manipulation?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17350)
**ArXiv:** 2610.17350 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17350)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.17350.pdf)]

**Authors:** Jack B. Jedlicki, Amish Sethi, Heng Yang

**Abstract:** We use a frozen frontier vision-language model, GPT-6-Astra, directly as the robot policy on RoboMME, a benchmark of 16 memory-dependent manipulation tasks spanning temporal, spatial, object, and procedural memory. The model keeps the whole episode in context and outputs every end-effector waypoint and gripper command, with no RoboMME-specific training. On the 800 test episodes it succeeds in 92.1%, above the previously reported human score of 90.5%, which was obtained with an oracle skill interface, and far ahead of the best leaderboard entry (60.8%) and of a hierarchical system that pairs the same model with a RoboMME-trained policy (79.1%). It matches or exceeds the human score on 13 of the 16 tasks. Its 63 failures concentrate in two tasks and are mostly premature completions and mistimed or unintended button presses. These results blur the usual division between long-horizon planning, memory and end-effector control, and raise the question of how much of this stack a single general-purpose model can take over in embodied systems. Project website: https://embodied-astra.github.io. Code, prompts and all episode logs: https://github.com/jackbjed/embodied-astra-robomme.

**Comment:** Criterion 1: Uses a frozen frontier VLM directly as a manipulation policy that outputs end-effector waypoints and gripper commands, achieving 92.1% success across 800 RoboMME episodes covering 16 memory-dependent tasks.

**Relevance:** 10
Back to [[topic](#topic-1)] [[top](#topics)]

<a id="1065-box-wam-box-trajectories-as-compact-state-representations-for-world-action-models"></a>

### 1065\. [Box-WAM: Box Trajectories as Compact State Representations for World Action Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17526)
**ArXiv:** 2610.17526 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17526)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.17526.pdf)]

**Authors:** Qinxi Yu, Wenzhou Ding, Gloria X. Wang, Albert J. Zhai, Shenlong Wang

**Abstract:** World action models (WAMs) are a category of policy models that ex- plicitly predict both actions and future world states. They have been shown to outperform action-only models across various robotic manipulation tasks. How- ever, representing the future with full-frame video prediction can be computation- ally expensive during both training and inference. This raises a central question: what compact state representation preserves the planning and supervision bene- fits of future prediction? In this paper, we propose BoxWAM, which replaces dense future frames with oriented bounding box trajectories of task-relevant ob- jects. By explicitly imagining object-centric future motion, our method provides a lightweight prediction target that captures the key physical changes induced by robot actions. We show that box trajectory prediction substantially reduces com- putational cost while improving action prediction performance, suggesting that object-centric compact states can serve as an efficient alternative to video-based world action modeling for robotic manipulation. Result videos can be found on https://box-wam.github.io

**Comment:** Criterion 1: BoxWAM replaces full-frame future prediction with oriented object-box trajectories in a robotic manipulation world action model, reducing computational cost while improving action prediction.

**Relevance:** 10
Back to [[topic](#topic-1)] [[top](#topics)]

<a id="1066-acting-through-registers"></a>

### 1066\. [Acting Through Registers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17638)
**ArXiv:** 2610.17638 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17638)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.17638.pdf)]

**Authors:** Yiwei Zhang, Yantai Yang, Jin Gao, ..., Weiming Hu, Zhipeng Zhang

**Abstract:** World Action Models (WAMs) use generative visual representations to condition robot actions, yet how these representations organize action-relevant information remains poorly understood. We identify persistent high-norm spatial outliers in WAM representations and find that their action relevance persists even after normalization and K/V projection attenuate their extreme magnitude. Motivated by this observation, we introduce RegisterWAM, which adds learnable non-spatial registers to the world model and uses them directly for action conditioning. Registers alone do not fully eliminate spatial outliers; pairing them with observation flow-matching (FM) loss supervision suppresses spatial outliers while concentrating high-norm responses in registers. Beyond this reorganization, observation FM loss supervision also improves action learning without registers, motivating an additional noised-observation FM objective that learns current-scene recovery from corrupted inputs. The resulting model achieves 81.64\% success on LIBERO-Plus without additional embodied pretraining data or foundation models, reaches 93.0\% average success on RoboTwin 2.0, and improves real-world manipulation performance.

**Comment:** Criterion 1: RegisterWAM uses learnable non-spatial registers and observation flow-matching supervision to condition robot actions through a generative world model, achieving 81.64% success on LIBERO-Plus, 93.0% on RoboTwin 2.0, and improved real-world manipulation performance.

**Relevance:** 10
Back to [[topic](#topic-1)] [[top](#topics)]

<a id="1069-beyond-the-end-effector-learning-whole-arm-contact-manipulation-from-human-demonstrations"></a>

### 1069\. [Beyond the End-Effector: Learning Whole-Arm Contact Manipulation from Human Demonstrations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17802)
**ArXiv:** 2610.17802 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17802)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.17802.pdf)]

**Authors:** Bo-Ruei Huang, Daniel Seita, Erdem Bıyık

**Abstract:** Robot manipulation has traditionally relied on end-effector-centric interactions. In contrast, humans routinely exploit whole-arm contacts to improve efficiency, robustness, and adaptability, especially when the hands are occupied or when precise grasping is unnecessary. Enabling similar capabilities in robots remains challenging: the robot must select where on its arm to make contact, generate effective non-prehensile interactions, and satisfy task-specific pose or safety constraints on the end-effector. We propose Whole-Arm Contact Learning (WACL), a hierarchical framework that describes each whole-arm contact task with three robot-agnostic representations and hands them to a quasi-static surface-contact optimizer: semantic keypoints that summarize the scene, a reference contact trajectory that specifies where and how the arm presses on the object, and safety-aware path constraints that a vision-language model generates from the task description to protect the end-effector's task. The robot's kinematic chain enters only in this optimizer, which grounds these representations onto the arm by jointly searching over which link makes contact, where on that link, and the joint configuration that realizes it. This decomposition lets WACL learn the contact trajectory from 10 bare-hand human demonstrations per task collected without teleoperation, while new end-effector constraints change only the optimizer's input rather than any trained component. Real-world experiments on a 7-DoF arm across four whole-arm tasks show that WACL sustains contact under end-effector constraints where a strong link-pose baseline fails (78% vs. 33% success) and generalizes to new constraints without retraining. Website: https://wacl-robot.github.io

**Comment:** Criterion 1: WACL uses a vision-language model to generate safety-aware path constraints that a whole-arm contact optimizer grounds into robot motions, achieving 78% success versus 33% for a link-pose baseline.

**Relevance:** 10
Back to [[topic](#topic-1)] [[top](#topics)]

<a id="1070-learning-robot-policies-for-whole-body-mobility-assistance-via-dynamic-human-motion-generation"></a>

### 1070\. [Learning Robot Policies for Whole-Body Mobility Assistance via Dynamic Human Motion Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17750)
**ArXiv:** 2610.17750 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17750)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.17750.pdf)]

**Authors:** Shikun Ban, Shohei Nagai, Qingyang Song, ..., Thomas Wang, Zackory Erickson

**Abstract:** Robotic assistance for human mobility could benefit care recipients and reduce caregiver burden during physically demanding tasks such as Sit-to-Stand (STS) and Lift-and-Transfer (L&T). Learning robot policies for these whole-body assistive tasks is challenging: real-world data collection for human-robot interaction is costly and safety-critical, making simulation an attractive alternative. Prior work, however, typically treats the human as static or minimally responsive and thus fails to capture the cooperative nature of mobility assistance. We present a simulation-based framework for learning whole-body assistive robot policies. At its core is a human policy trained with an adversarial motion prior that produces realistic care-recipient motion in response to the robot's actions. A human-adaptive scripted controller interacts with this policy to collect diverse robot demonstrations at scale. From these demonstrations, we train 3D diffusion policies that map segmented human point clouds and robot joint positions to whole-body actions for a mobile manipulator, without relying on privileged human state at deployment. The resulting robot policies successfully perform both STS and L&T assistance in simulation. Preliminary experiments further demonstrate sim-to-real transfer for STS after fine-tuning on a small amount of real-world data. Videos are available on our project page: https://rchi-lab.github.io/human-gen-mobility-assistance/

**Comment:** Criterion 1: Trains 3D diffusion policies on demonstrations collected through responsive human-motion simulation for whole-body assistive manipulation, demonstrating sit-to-stand and lift-and-transfer in simulation and preliminary sit-to-stand sim-to-real transfer after limited real-data fine-tuning.

**Relevance:** 10
Back to [[topic](#topic-1)] [[top](#topics)]

<a id="1071-foretac-predictive-contact-guidance-for-generative-robot-policies"></a>

### 1071\. [ForeTac: Predictive Contact Guidance for Generative Robot Policies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18118)
**ArXiv:** 2610.18118 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18118)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.18118.pdf)]

**Authors:** Zhiyuan Chen, Shuai Chen, Haoran Zhou, ..., Diyun Xiang, Yangwei You

**Abstract:** Visually similar actions can produce contact loss, jamming, slippage, or irreversible damage, while tactile feedback may arrive after a critical transition. Contact-rich manipulation therefore requires generative policies to anticipate and evaluate whether candidate actions will produce desirable contact outcomes, then adjust action generation before execution. We present ForeTac, a predictive contact-guidance framework organized as a represent-predict-evaluate-guide loop. ForeTac encodes the current tactile state, predicts the tactile consequences of a candidate action sequence, and evaluates the predicted trajectory using a contact-quality objective learned from successful and failed physical interactions. At deployment, its gradient propagates through the tactile predictor to guide action generation while the pretrained model parameters remain frozen. We evaluate ForeTac on four real-robot tasks (planar and curved-surface wiping, card swiping, and fragile-object grasping) with diffusion and flow-matching policies. Across these tasks, ForeTac raises the average task success rate from 36.3% to 71.3% and the average contact success rate (CSR) from 20.0% to 66.3% compared with vision-only Diffusion Policy. Component analyses support the tactile representation and action-conditioned foresight, and show that predicted touch preserves contact-quality ordering. Project page: https://foretac.github.io/.

**Comment:** Criterion 1: ForeTac guides diffusion and flow-matching robot manipulation policies using predicted tactile outcomes and contact-quality gradients, raising average task success from 36.3% to 71.3% over vision-only Diffusion Policy across four real-robot tasks.

**Relevance:** 10
Back to [[topic](#topic-1)] [[top](#topics)]

<a id="1072-data-free-continual-learning-for-world-action-model-with-rectified-generative-replay"></a>

### 1072\. [Data-Free Continual Learning for World Action Model with Rectified Generative Replay](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18038)
**ArXiv:** 2610.18038 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18038)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.18038.pdf)]

**Authors:** Kaipeng Fang, Hangxu Liu, Chaojian Shi, ..., Jingkuan Song, Lianli Gao

**Abstract:** World action models (WAMs) built on pretrained video generation models adapt quickly to new robotic tasks, but they forget previously learned tasks under sequential fine-tuning. Experience replay (ER) mitigates such forgetting by rehearsing data of previous tasks, yet this data is often unavailable in robotics, where a policy is deployed as weights only and its training data is not shared along with it. In this work, we ask whether a WAM can achieve data-free continual learning by regenerating its own past. Our key observation is that a WAM can indeed autoregressively regenerate complete rollouts of previously learned tasks and replay them to mitigate forgetting. However, replaying such raw rollouts still lags far behind replaying real data, which we attribute to two issues. First, the WAM is unable to recognize task completion and keeps generating purposeless motion after the task is done. Second, under open-loop generation, the generated actions gradually drift away from the generated observations. To address both issues, we present rectified generative replay (R$^2$-WAM), which truncates generated rollouts at task completion with an agentic evaluator and filters out rollouts whose actions are physically inconsistent with the observations. On the four LIBERO suites and five real-world tasks, R$^2$-WAM reduces forgetting by over 90% relative to sequential fine-tuning and nearly matches ER without accessing any data from previous tasks. Our analysis further reveals that forgetting in WAMs concentrates in inverse dynamics modeling: a forgotten WAM still produces visually plausible observations of previous tasks while its generated actions fail.

**Comment:** Criterion 1: R²-WAM enables continual learning for robotic world action models through generative replay with task-completion evaluation and physical-consistency filtering, reducing forgetting by over 90% across four LIBERO suites and five real-world tasks without accessing previous-task data.

**Relevance:** 10
Back to [[topic](#topic-1)] [[top](#topics)]

<a id="1074-wovr-world-model-as-reliable-simulator-for-versatile-vla-policy-optimization-with-rl"></a>

### 1074\. [WoVR+: World Model as Reliable Simulator for Versatile VLA Policy Optimization with RL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17922)
**ArXiv:** 2610.17922 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17922)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.17922.pdf)]

**Authors:** Shangqing Zhou, Zhennan Jiang, Ning Ma, ..., Haoran Li, Dongbin Zhao

**Abstract:** Action-conditioned world models can reduce costly real-robot interaction in reinforcement learning for Vision-Language-Action models (VLA-RL), but hallucinations can mislead policy optimization. Extending them to richer VLA interfaces and long-horizon tasks raises challenges in spatiotemporal and cross-modal consistency, exploration-reliability balance in RL, and compounding rollout errors. We present WoVR+, extending WoVR's hallucination-aware WM-RL framework by redesigning the learned environment and imagined interaction protocol: A VLA-interface-aligned world model provides multi-view prediction and proprioceptive updates while mitigating hallucinations. Chunk-wise noise preserves flow-based policy exploration while limiting prediction-degrading action jitter. For long-horizon tasks, stage-local imagined training combines stage-entry resets, stage-specific rewards, local horizons, and controllable sampling to shorten generated prefixes and balance training across stages. WoVR+ improves success over SFT by 21.09% and 16.7% on RoboTwin and 6 real-robot tasks. Long-horizon phone-packaging success rises from 30% to 75%. Videos are available at https://youmo445.github.io/WoVR_Plus.github.io/.

**Comment:** Criterion 1: WoVR+ uses an action-conditioned world model for VLA reinforcement learning, introducing hallucination mitigation and stage-local imagined training that raises long-horizon phone-packaging success from 30% to 75%.

**Relevance:** 10
Back to [[topic](#topic-1)] [[top](#topics)]

<a id="1076-forkcast-predictive-recovery-for-vision-language-action-policies-via-counterfactual-futures"></a>

### 1076\. [ForkCast: Predictive Recovery for Vision-Language-Action Policies via Counterfactual Futures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18650)
**ArXiv:** 2610.18650 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18650)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.18650.pdf)]

**Authors:** Chenru Wen, Huajian Zeng, Haokai Ding, ..., Dezhen Song, Xingxing Zuo

**Abstract:** Vision-language-action (VLA) policies perform manipulation tasks using camera images and language instructions, but object slip or visual occlusion can disrupt execution. Recovery is typically learned during policy training or delegated to a monitor that triggers a scripted repair after detecting a failure. We introduce ForkCast, a predictive recovery framework that compares counterfactual futures to determine whether and how to intervene in a frozen VLA policy. At each decision point, ForkCast evaluates three candidates: executing the policy's nominal action chunk (Commit), adjusting the camera viewpoint (Reobserve), and replanning the arm trajectory (Replan). A candidate-conditioned world model forecasts the latent outcome of each candidate at a common future horizon, and a shared task-progress head scores the predicted outcomes. By evaluating the policy's own continuation alongside the repair candidates, a single comparison based on task progress determines both whether and how to intervene, without a failure detector, an intervention threshold, or updates to the VLA backbone. On five simulated RoboTwin 2.0 tasks with injected occlusion and object slip, ForkCast increases pooled success from 13.3% for the frozen $π_{0.5}$ policy to 30.0% and transfers to two additional VLA backbones without updating ForkCast. After adaptation on real-robot candidate rollouts, ForkCast also improves success over the base policy under both clean and visibility-limited conditions. Code will be released to benefit the research community.

**Comment:** Matches criteria 1 and 7: ForkCast uses a candidate-conditioned world model to select continuation, camera reobservation, or arm replanning for a frozen manipulation VLA, increasing pooled success on five RoboTwin 2.0 tasks from 13.3% to 30.0%.

**Relevance:** 10
Back to [[topic](#topic-1)] [[top](#topics)]

<a id="1078-wave-0-bridging-vla-policies-and-wams-with-lightweight-latent-future-prediction"></a>

### 1078\. [WAVE-0: Bridging VLA Policies and WAMs with Lightweight Latent Future Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18568)
**ArXiv:** 2610.18568 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18568)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.18568.pdf)]

**Authors:** Haomin Wang, Anqiong Ji, Ke Wang, ..., Ran Zheng, Zhipeng Zhang

**Abstract:** Vision-language-action (VLA) policies excel at generalist robotic manipulation but generate actions without explicitly modeling their environmental consequences. World action models (WAMs) incorporate future prediction, yet most rely on costly video generation, while latent alternatives either use predicted futures only as auxiliary targets or infer latent actions from suboptimal visual transitions through costly large-scale video pretraining. We introduce WAVE-0 (World-model-Augmented VLA Execution), a framework that bridges VLAs and WAMs through a lightweight latent world model. By connecting the semantic understanding of pretrained VLAs with explicit future prediction, WAVE-0 allows anticipated action consequences to directly guide action generation. Built on a pretrained VLA, its 257M world model translates VLM-generated latent action plans into predicted future representations that condition the action expert, unifying latent planning, future prediction, and action generation. The world model is trained directly on ground-truth robot actions with a counterfactual ranking objective, requiring neither pixel reconstruction nor large-scale video pretraining. WAVE-0 achieves success rates of 91.47% on RoboTwin 2.0 and 98.7% on LIBERO, along with 88.0%, 61.4%, and 75.0% on LIBERO-Plus, LIBERO-Pro, and LIBERO-Para, respectively. On real-world tasks, it improves the success rate of $π_{0.5}$ from 38.3% to 73.3%. These results demonstrate strong manipulation performance and generalization across diverse distribution shifts.

**Comment:** Criterion 1: WAVE-0 conditions a VLA action expert on future representations predicted by a lightweight latent world model, improving real-world manipulation success from 38.3% to 73.3%.

**Relevance:** 10
Back to [[topic](#topic-1)] [[top](#topics)]

<a id="1079-model-based-online-decision-making-via-generative-trajectory-planning"></a>

### 1079\. [Model-Based Online Decision Making via Generative Trajectory Planning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18677)
**ArXiv:** 2610.18677 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18677)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.18677.pdf)]

**Authors:** Haldun Balim, Yilun Du, Na Li

**Abstract:** Online decision-making faces a tension between efficient inference and long-horizon reasoning. Value-based methods rely on short-horizon bootstrapping that limits the propagation of long-term information, while trajectory optimization methods plan explicitly but must solve a high-dimensional optimization problem at every decision step. We propose Generative Trajectory Planning (GTP), a model-based framework that addresses this tension by learning a generative model as a proposal distribution over action sequences. Instead of optimizing sequences from scratch, GTP samples candidate sequences from a diffusion prior and refines them using learned dynamics, reward, and value models, enabling efficient trajectory-level reasoning. Across continuous control and long-horizon manipulation tasks, GTP matches or exceeds state-of-the-art model-free, diffusion-policy, and model-based baselines, and remains stable in regimes where strong baselines degrade. Our results suggest that generative models are best understood not as policies, but as structured proposal distributions for planning, offering a new perspective on how to integrate generative modeling with model-based decision-making.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 1: Generative Trajectory Planning samples action sequences from a diffusion prior and refines them using learned dynamics, reward, and value models, matching or exceeding strong baselines on long-horizon manipulation tasks.

**Relevance:** 10
Back to [[topic](#topic-1)] [[top](#topics)]

<a id="1080-uniwam-a-unified-mixture-of-experts-framework-for-world-and-world-action-models"></a>

### 1080\. [UniW(A)M: A Unified Mixture-of-Experts Framework for World and World-Action Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18697)
**ArXiv:** 2610.18697 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18697)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.18697.pdf)]

**Authors:** Jingbo Xu, Jianjie Fang, Zhuohang Li, ..., Wei Wu, Yong Li

**Abstract:** World models (WMs) and world-action models (WAMs) focus on future world prediction and executable action generation, respectively. Although these models learn shared physical dynamics and interaction semantics from embodied trajectories, they are typically trained in isolation, which restricts knowledge reuse between prediction and control. The central challenge is therefore to develop a unified learning framework that shares these dynamics while preserving the distinct modeling capabilities required by each paradigm. We propose UniW(A)M, a mixture-of-experts world-(action) model built upon diffusion Transformers that unifies action-conditioned world modeling and diverse WAM variants. Shared experts capture common dynamics, while atomic capability experts are reused across interfaces so that each interface keeps its own conditional pathway. At deployment, a plug-in adaptive interface router (PAIR) learns from a small subset of target-task chunk scenarios to select a suitable WAM interface for each execution chunk, balancing task success and execution efficiency while keeping the backbone frozen. We evaluate UniW(A)M on LIBERO, RoboTwin 2.0, and three real-world manipulation tasks, where it achieves 88.3% average real-world success versus 76.7% for the strongest baseline. On WorldArena, it leads action-conditioned world modeling on all three tasks. AC-WM co-training contributes to action generation on every action interface and PAIR raises OOD success from 63.3% to 76.7%.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 1: UniW(A)M uses diffusion Transformers with shared and capability-specific experts to unify world prediction and robotic action generation, achieving 88.3% average real-world manipulation success versus 76.7% for the strongest baseline.

**Relevance:** 10
Back to [[topic](#topic-1)] [[top](#topics)]

<a id="1081-steertuning-policies-from-a-few-human-videos"></a>

### 1081\. [Steertuning Policies from a Few Human Videos](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18711)
**ArXiv:** 2610.18711 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18711)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.18711.pdf)]

**Authors:** Billy Yan, Isaac Tu, Kelly Lee, ..., Lerrel Pinto, Irmak Guzey

**Abstract:** Current recipes for robot learning require massive amounts of robot demonstrations to obtain robust and adaptive policies. In this work, we introduce Steertuning, a system that enables robots to learn new tasks in new environments from 5 human demonstrations in under 5 Steertuning consists of two parts. (1) We pretrain a 400M-parameter diffusion policy on our egocentric human dataset, which consists of 100 hours of densely annotated random interactions. This pretraining makes Steertuning robust to background differences and enables spatial and object generalization when deployed on new tasks. (2) Given 5 demonstrations of a new task, we finetune our model for only 2 minutes (2000 training updates) and steer the policy's actions during reverse sampling using the demonstration whose observation is closest to that of the deployment environment. Together with a carefully designed data processing pipeline for embodiment transfer, this enables Steertuning to achieve a 71% success rate across 12 tasks in 12 new environments, ranging from lab settings to kitchens in new homes. The entire process, from data collection for a new task to robot policy deployment, takes less than 5 minutes. Our full dataset, model weights, codebase, and robot videos will be made available at our website: https://steertuning.github.io.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 1: Steertuning pretrains a 400M-parameter diffusion policy on egocentric human interactions and adapts it using five human demonstrations plus reverse-sampling action steering, achieving 71% robot task success across 12 tasks in 12 new environments.

**Relevance:** 10
Back to [[topic](#topic-1)] [[top](#topics)]

<a id="1083-forcewam-decoupling-future-and-response-learning-from-demo-and-play-data-for-contact-rich-manipulation"></a>

### 1083\. [ForceWAM: Decoupling Future and Response Learning from Demo and Play Data for Contact-Rich Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18852)
**ArXiv:** 2610.18852 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18852)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.18852.pdf)]

**Authors:** Zijun Xu, Wendi Chen, Xinkai Wang, ..., Cewu Lu, Lixin Yang

**Abstract:** Expert demonstrations show how to complete contact-rich tasks, but provide limited coverage of how interaction forces respond to different actions. Task-agnostic play provides broader experience of contact dynamics without specifying task goals. To fully leverage these two types of data, we introduce ForceWAM, a force world action model that decouples knowledge of task-compatible interaction outcomes from knowledge of how local actions change contact. A world model learns from successful demonstrations to jointly predict future video and six-axis force/torque trajectories. Together, these futures describe the expected evolution of motion and contact during the task. A separate inverse dynamics model (IDM) learns from both task and play, mapping current observations and recorded visual-wrench futures to their associated robot actions. Play data thus provide knowledge of force responses for goal-directed execution. At deployment, the IDM reactively translates generated futures into actions, reusing them with fresh wrench feedback between world-model updates. Across real-robot force-regulated sliding, box flipping with and without disturbances, and ring placement, ForceWAM achieves $85$-$95\%$ mean success, surpassing world-modeling and reactive baselines. Our experiments further show that adding task-agnostic play data improves success by $15$-$45$ percentage points, demonstrating its value for learning contact-rich execution. Project page and code are available at https://force-wam.github.io/.

**Comment:** Criterion 1: ForceWAM generates future video and six-axis force/torque trajectories and translates them into robot actions using an inverse dynamics model trained on demonstration and play data, achieving 85–95% mean success on real-robot contact-rich manipulation tasks.

**Relevance:** 10
Back to [[topic](#topic-1)] [[top](#topics)]

<a id="1084-interaction-latents-as-actions-for-contact-rich-manipulation"></a>

### 1084\. [Interaction Latents as Actions for Contact-Rich Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18855)
**ArXiv:** 2610.18855 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18855)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.18855.pdf)]

**Authors:** Yuhang Zheng, Yujie Zang, Yuchen Liu, ..., Zhongpu Xia, Shuicheng Yan

**Abstract:** Contact-rich manipulation requires robots to coordinate not only where to move, but also how contact and force should evolve throughout an interaction. Yet most robot policies represent actions primarily as motion trajectories, using tactile signals as inputs or auxiliary prediction targets without explicitly encoding contact dynamics in the action representation. We propose ILA (Interaction Latents as Actions), which uses decodable interaction latents that jointly capture the evolution of motion, contact, and force application as policy action units, providing a shared representation for action generation, evaluation, and offline improvement. We learn these latents through visually conditioned joint action-tactile reconstruction with a time-conditioned implicit decoder. Using them as flow-matching generation targets supports post-training of pretrained vision-language-action models (VLAs) and world action models (WAMs), as well as training flow-matching policies from scratch. A shared decoder maps generated latents to executable actions. For offline improvement, we learn a tactile-conditioned expert reference model from interaction latents encoded from expert demonstrations. The resulting expert-consistency rewards guide weighted learning from successful rollouts, while observation-compatible expert references guide correction of low-reward predictions from failed rollouts. Across three policy families, ILA improves success rates averaged over four real-world tasks by 21.0-36.5 percentage points relative to tactile-conditioned direct-action baselines. Ablations highlight the benefit of encoding contact evolution in action targets beyond tactile conditioning, while the same latent space supports expert-guided offline improvement. Project website: https://mrsecant.github.io/ILA/.

**Comment:** Criterion 1: ILA introduces interaction latents encoding motion, contact, and force as flow-matching targets for post-training vision-language-action and world action models, improving average success across four real-world manipulation tasks by 21.0–36.5 percentage points across three policy families.

**Relevance:** 10
Back to [[topic](#topic-1)] [[top](#topics)]

<a id="1085-greenwam-a-world-foundation-model-for-robotics"></a>

### 1085\. [GreenWAM: A World Foundation Model for Robotics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18774)
**ArXiv:** 2610.18774 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18774)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.18774.pdf)]

**Authors:** Gennady Sidorov, Valerii Kornilov, Denis Grankin, Polina Fedotova, Aleksey Postnikov

**Abstract:** A robot needs a world model that predicts how its actions will change the scene and a policy that chooses those actions. Models are usually built and evaluated for only one of these roles, and every robot records its actions in its own format. We ask whether one robot world model can serve both roles across different bodies, and what makes this work. We present GreenWAM, a world foundation model for robotics, together with the recipe that produces it: (1) a shared anatomical action space that describes 20 robot families, from bimanual arms to dexterous humanoids, in common joint coordinates; (2) joint training on these families in visual, action-conditioned and world-action modes; and (3) policies that split an action expert off the generator. As a world model, GreenWAM attains top-tier trajectory, depth and 3D accuracy on WorldArena 1.0, ahead of every world-action model and video generator in our comparison. As a policy, it scores among the top entries of the RoboTwin 2.0 leaderboard, reaches the highest zero-shot LIBERO-plus success among the compared methods and is competitive with dedicated vision-language-action and world-action policies on CALVIN and LIBERO. We further adapt GreenWAM to a real humanoid, where it succeeds more often than policies adapted from $π_{0.5}$ and from Cosmos 3 Edge, and distill it into a single-step policy for real-time control. Controlled experiments yield three findings: robot specialization improves world prediction and control together; an action expert helps when split off at the start of adaptation; and keeping world-model modes during policy adaptation preserves robustness to scene changes.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 1 by jointly training a generative robot world model and manipulation policies across 20 robot families, achieving the highest zero-shot LIBERO-plus success among compared methods and demonstrating real-humanoid control.

**Relevance:** 10
Back to [[topic](#topic-1)] [[top](#topics)]

<a id="1086-eqc-equilibrium-energy-control-for-introspective-lifelong-robot-learning"></a>

### 1086\. [EqC: Equilibrium Energy Control for Introspective Lifelong Robot Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18790)
**ArXiv:** 2610.18790 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18790)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.18790.pdf)]

**Authors:** Haodong Yang, Runhan Huang, Bowen Zhang, ..., Yicheng Liu, Hang Zhao

**Abstract:** Long-term robot autonomy depends on more than effective action generation: a policy must recognize when its behavior is no longer sufficiently supported and use assistance to improve. We introduce Equilibrium Energy Control (EqC), a framework for introspective lifelong robot learning built around an explicit conditional energy landscape. EqC casts generative action refinement as equilibrium-seeking optimization, while the geometry of the refinement process reveals whether the generated behavior remains consistent with the learned landscape. This competence signal enables Energy-Gated DAgger, where persistent energy-consistency violations trigger expert takeover and the resulting recovery trajectories are aggregated for subsequent policy updates. EqC integrates naturally into both Diffusion Transformer policies and flow-matching-based vision-language-action (VLA) policies. Across simulation and real-world bimanual manipulation, EqC matches or improves control performance and robustness while enabling timely intervention near the policy's competence boundary. A real-world study further shows how these interventions progressively expand the range of situations handled autonomously, providing a practical path from static generative controllers toward self-improving robot systems.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 1 by integrating equilibrium-based action refinement and Energy-Gated DAgger into Diffusion Transformer and flow-matching VLA policies, improving robustness and enabling progressive learning in simulated and real-world bimanual manipulation.

**Relevance:** 10
Back to [[topic](#topic-1)] [[top](#topics)]

<a id="1089-loopwam-closed-loop-generalist-policy-improvement-from-deployment-rollouts"></a>

### 1089\. [LoopWAM: Closed-Loop Generalist Policy Improvement from Deployment Rollouts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18873)
**ArXiv:** 2610.18873 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18873)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.18873.pdf)]

**Authors:** Wenxuan Guo, Yingping Liang, Zhaoguo Wang, ..., Erjin Zhou, Jiwen Lu

**Abstract:** Scaling expert demonstrations alone does not enable generalist robot policies to improve from deployment. Real-world rollouts contain successes, suboptimal behavior, and failures: imitating every recorded action can reinforce poor behavior, while filtering out failures discards evidence of physical interactions and their consequences. We introduce \textbf{LoopWAM}, a closed-loop world-action model that learns directly from this full quality spectrum, using rollouts from both itself and other deployed policies. Source-dependent noise sampling unifies behavior learning and action-conditioned world modeling, while quality steering and quality-weighted supervision retain full video supervision across qualities and regulate action imitation. Video and action tokens interact throughout parallel denoising, allowing learned consequences to inform action generation directly. On RoboChallenge Table30-V2, three deployment-and-training rounds raise success from 23.67\% to 43.33\%, a 19.7-percentage-point gain, with a final score of 56.80. LoopWAM also achieves 94.5\% average success on RoboTwin~2.0 and 49.5\% overall success on RoboCasa365 without additional embodied pretraining, obtaining the best overall results among the compared methods across all three benchmarks. On challenging shirt folding task, two rounds of deployment-driven improvement after single-task post-training raise success from 13.20\% to 55.56\%. These results demonstrate closed-loop improvement from mixed-quality experience under both multi-task learning and single-task post-training. \href{https://loop-wam.github.io/}{\textcolor{orange}{Project Page}}

**Comment:** Criterion 1: LoopWAM jointly denoises video and action tokens in a robot world-action model, learning from mixed-quality deployment rollouts to raise RoboChallenge Table30-V2 success from 23.67% to 43.33%.

**Relevance:** 10
Back to [[topic](#topic-1)] [[top](#topics)]

<a id="1091-decoupled-drift-diffusion-world-models-for-consistent-future-frame-prediction"></a>

### 1091\. [Decoupled Drift-Diffusion World Models for Consistent Future Frame Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19212)
**ArXiv:** 2610.19212 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19212)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.19212.pdf)]

**Authors:** Anton Arapin, Aditya Singh, Yixin Wang, Jun Gao, Bernadette Bucher

**Abstract:** World models of visual information predict future image frames conditioned on given action sequences. This predicted scene evolution is driven by deterministic changes such as controlled motion on a robot platform, as well as stochastic changes such as rendering of manipulated objects or previously occluded regions. Our key insight is that the spatial location of these stochastic changes is itself learnable. We leverage this insight by jointly regressing both the deterministic next-state prediction and the spatial uncertainty map, then using the uncertainty map to guide a conditional diffusion process. We propose Decoupled Drift-Diffusion World Model (D3WM), which uses a transformer trained with a Gaussian negative-log-likelihood objective to produce both the deterministic next-state prediction and a spatial map of heteroscedastic aleatoric uncertainty. The learned uncertainty map then provides a structured spatial gate for a conditional diffusion model: deterministic regions are anchored to the predicted state, while uncertain regions, such as disocclusions or dynamic textures, are sampled by the diffusion process. We frame this decomposition under a variance-gated stochastic differential equation that separates deterministic drift from stochastic diffusion. D3WM shows improved results over competitive baselines in two distinct types of action spaces: robotic tabletop manipulation and camera motion.

**Comment:** Criterion 1: D3WM is an action-conditioned diffusion world model evaluated on robotic tabletop manipulation, using learned spatial uncertainty maps to gate stochastic generation and improve future-frame prediction over competitive baselines.

**Relevance:** 10
Back to [[topic](#topic-1)] [[top](#topics)]

<a id="1092-pacer-process-aware-vla-policy-learning-from-human-corrections-for-robotic-disassembly"></a>

### 1092\. [PACER: Process-Aware VLA Policy Learning from Human Corrections for Robotic Disassembly](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19248)
**ArXiv:** 2610.19248 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19248)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.19248.pdf)]

**Authors:** Chang Liu, Sibo Tian, Litian Gong, ..., Xiao Liang, Minghui Zheng

**Abstract:** Robotic disassembly of end-of-life (EoL) products requires robots to accommodate product variation, follow prerequisite sequences, and approach components precisely for contact-rich manipulation. Supervised fine-tuning (SFT) provides a practical path for adaptation, but limited and costly disassembly data cannot capture the distribution shifts arising from changing environments and product uncertainties. The policy's own rollouts record its approach attempts, and human corrections demonstrate recovery from its errors, but turning these experiences into better policy performance remains challenging. We introduce PACER, a Process-Aware Correction and Evidence Reweighting framework that learns from these experiences through SFT, using progress toward the target and each trace's source and outcome to weight valid action chunks in the native supervised action loss. PACER trains one policy per weighting setting and selects one of the resulting policies through held-out validation. We evaluate PACER on UR5e motherboard disassembly across five component classes, where it achieves 87/100 successful approach-and-handoff trials on the held-out benchmark, compared with 54/100 for vanilla post-SFT.

**Comment:** Criterion 1: PACER improves vision-language-action policy learning for robotic disassembly by reweighting supervised action chunks using progress, trace source, and outcome, achieving 87/100 successful UR5e approach-and-handoff trials versus 54/100 for vanilla post-SFT.

**Relevance:** 10
Back to [[topic](#topic-1)] [[top](#topics)]

<a id="1093-paraphrase-consensus-is-not-self-consistency-instruction-reliance-and-robustness-in-vla-policies"></a>

### 1093\. [Paraphrase Consensus Is Not Self-Consistency: Instruction Reliance and Robustness in VLA Policies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19438)
**ArXiv:** 2610.19438 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19438)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.19438.pdf)]

**Authors:** Hanlong Li, Sihan Tan, Takeshi Ashizawa, Benjamin Yen, Kazuhiro Nakadai

**Abstract:** Vision-language-action (VLA) policies fine-tuned on LIBERO are trained and evaluated on the same ten instruction strings, and rewording the instruction changes base OpenVLA's success by -38.0 points. Test-time prompt ensembling, which improves language models, does not recover this loss in any setting we ran. Paraphrase consensus (the medoid action over K paraphrases) costs base OpenVLA 16.8 points when the training instruction is a member. Without it, the medoid of the paraphrases scores -32.4 points relative to the training instruction, and confidence-based selection scores -28.0. The same medoid over samples of one paraphrase improves on greedy decoding but stays -27.6 points below the training instruction, consistent with aggregation reducing variance without removing the bias the paraphrases share. A wrong-instruction probe measures how much of a policy's success depends on the instruction. Across three policies and four suites, paraphrases cost a policy only where this share is high, and the stronger policies lose a much smaller fraction of it than base OpenVLA. LoRA post-training on paraphrased instructions reduces the one-paraphrase effect to +2.0 points, against -32.0 for a matched control, reproduces across two seeds and on OpenVLA-OFT, transfers to withheld phrasings, and leaves the policy's reliance on the instruction unchanged.

**Comment:** Criterion 1: LoRA post-training on paraphrased instructions improves instruction-following robustness of OpenVLA manipulation policies on LIBERO, yielding a +2.0-point paraphrase effect versus −32.0 points for a matched control.

**Relevance:** 10
Back to [[topic](#topic-1)] [[top](#topics)]

<a id="1096-back-on-track-deployment-time-policy-improvement-via-world-model-planning"></a>

### 1096\. [Back on Track: Deployment-time Policy Improvement via World Model Planning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19579)
**ArXiv:** 2610.19579 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19579)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.19579.pdf)]

**Authors:** Colton Crosby, Hao Wang, Mac Schwager, Somil Bansal

**Abstract:** State-of-the-art visuomotor policies are increasingly capable of executing complex tasks, yet can fail during deployment in ways they cannot predict or recover from on their own. Improving their performance and robustness typically requires collecting more data, retraining, or modifying the policy architecture, all of which are time- and resource-intensive in practice. We instead propose an offline-trained monitoring-and-intervention framework that improves a policy during deployment using only black-box query access. From a limited set of labeled on-policy rollouts, we learn a policy-conditioned liveness value that measures the nominal policy's progress toward task completion; we additionally train a compact latent world model using these rollouts together with play data. At deployment, the liveness value continuously monitors execution. When it detects a loss of progress, a short-horizon planner searches the world model for corrective actions using the same value as its planning objective, after which control returns to the nominal policy. We evaluate our method on the LIBERO-Goal task suite and on a Franka Panda manipulator, using the vision-language-action model $π_{0.5}$ and a diffusion policy as nominal policies, and demonstrate significant improvements in task success rates, while supporting real-time control. Additional results and videos are available on the project website (https://policy-improvement-wm.pages.dev/).

**Comment:** Criteria 1 and 7: A learned liveness monitor triggers corrective planning in a latent world model, improving deployed diffusion and vision-language-action manipulation policies on LIBERO-Goal and a real Franka Panda without retraining the nominal policies.

**Relevance:** 10
Back to [[topic](#topic-1)] [[top](#topics)]

<a id="1099-one-shot-human-video-guided-adaptation-for-vision-language-action-models"></a>

### 1099\. [One-shot Human Video-Guided Adaptation for Vision-Language-Action Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19746)
**ArXiv:** 2610.19746 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19746)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.19746.pdf)]

**Authors:** Qi Chen, Yuwei Wu, Quan Chen, ..., Zhi Gao, Yunde Jia

**Abstract:** Vision-language-action (VLA) models have recently achieved remarkable progress by leveraging large-scale robot datasets and foundation models for general-purpose robot manipulation. However, their ability to rapidly adapt to novel manipulation tasks beyond the training distribution remains limited. Existing approaches often require task-specific robot demonstrations and additional training, which are labor- and compute-intensive, limiting the scalability of VLA adaptation to open-world scenarios. To reduce this dependence on target-task robot data, we propose CapPro, a framework that enables a VLA policy to adapt to previously unseen manipulation tasks using only a single human demonstration video. CapPro consists of two components.
