First, a phase-aware human video representation module constructs a compact video representation through learned visual token selection and learns task-relevant query representations under phase-caption supervision. Second, a video-conditioned adaptation module jointly incorporates the compact video and query-derived representations into the action-conditioning context of the pretrained VLA policy. % This design introduces task-specific guidance while largely preserving the original VLA action-generation backbone. In this way, the human demonstration provides task-specific execution guidance rather than an embodiment-aligned trajectory to imitate, without requiring target-task robot demonstrations or target-task fine-tuning. Experiments across source and unseen target tasks show that CapPro improves average performance across unseen target groups while maintaining comparable performance on the source tasks. These results demonstrate the effectiveness of human-video guidance for cross-task VLA adaptation.

**Comment:** Criterion 1: CapPro adapts a pretrained VLA manipulation policy to unseen tasks from a single human demonstration video using phase-aware visual token selection and video-conditioned action guidance, without target-task robot demonstrations or target-task fine-tuning.

**Relevance:** 10
Back to [[topic](#topic-1)] [[top](#topics)]

<a id="1100-coordi-pi-coordination-aware-dual-hand-action-representation-for-bimanual-dexterous-manipulation"></a>

### 1100\. [Coordi-pi: Coordination-Aware Dual-Hand Action Representation for Bimanual Dexterous Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19753)
**ArXiv:** 2610.19753 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19753)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.19753.pdf)]

**Authors:** Yiming Meng, Yanming Shao, Weijie Shen, ..., Ping Luo, Yao Mu

**Abstract:** Vision-language-action (VLA) models have shown strong potential for general-purpose robotic manipulation, but extending them to bimanual dexterous manipulation introduces a high-dimensional and structured action space. Existing bimanual VLA policies commonly concatenate right- and left-side arm--hand actions at each control timestep, leaving cross-hand interactions implicit within a single action vector. We introduce Coordi-$π$, a structured action representation for bimanual dexterous manipulation. Coordi-$π$ represents the right- and left-side arm--hand actions as individual action tokens and organizes them into an interleaved sequence, explicitly exposing the two sides within the action sequence. To explicitly encode hand identity and temporal synchronization in this interleaved representation, a learnable hand-identity embedding distinguishes the two sides, while sequence--control-timestep factorized rotary positional encoding (RoPE) separates sequence position from control timestep, allowing actions at the same control timestep to share a temporal coordinate while retaining distinct sequence positions. Across all five bimanual dexterous manipulation tasks in DexJoCo, Coordi-$π$ achieves state-of-the-art performance under both \textit{rand-obj} and \textit{rand-full}, improving the average success rate over $π_{0.5}$ from 31.9\% to 51.3\% and from 18.3\% to 32.3\%, respectively. Coordi-$π$ also consistently outperforms $π_{0.5}$ across four real-world bimanual dexterous manipulation tasks.

**Comment:** Criterion 1: Coordi-π improves VLA-based bimanual dexterous manipulation through interleaved per-hand action tokens and factorized rotary positional encoding, raising average DexJoCo success under rand-obj from 31.9% to 51.3% and outperforming π₀.₅ on four real-world tasks.

**Relevance:** 10
Back to [[topic](#topic-1)] [[top](#topics)]

<a id="1101-what-matters-for-world-modeling-in-vision-language-action-models"></a>

### 1101\. [What Matters for World Modeling in Vision-Language-Action Models?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19825)
**ArXiv:** 2610.19825 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19825)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.19825.pdf)]

**Authors:** Qisen Ma, Yixiang Chen, Yuan Xu, ..., Yan Huang, Liang Wang

**Abstract:** Integrating world models into Vision-Language-Action models (VLAs) has emerged as a promising direction, enabling policies to anticipate future dynamics beyond reactive observation-to-action mapping. However, existing approaches vary simultaneously in their world representations, integration paradigms, and policy backbones, making it difficult to determine what truly matters for effective world model integration. To disentangle these factors, we categorize existing efforts into four representative paradigms, namely Implicit Future Alignment, Explicit Future Query, MoT Future Expert, and External World Predictor. We instantiate each within a unified framework and systematically evaluate these paradigms with diverse world representations and policy backbones across in-domain, out-of-domain, long-horizon, dynamic, few-shot, and real-world settings. Our study yields four main findings: 1) world representation effectiveness is paradigm-dependent, whereas 2D semantic representations offer robust, cross-paradigm gains; 2) no single paradigm is universally optimal, yet the decoupled MoT Future Expert offers competitive overall performance, particularly in OOD robustness; 3) world modeling consistently benefits policies built on standard VLMs, while decoupled designs exhibit greater compatibility with pretrained VLAs; and 4) world modeling improves performance over action-only baselines across multiple settings, with notable gains on long-horizon and dynamic tasks. Project page and video demonstrations are available at https://dylanmqs.github.io/WM-in-VLAs/.

**Comment:** Criterion 1: A unified evaluation of world-model integration into vision-language-action policies finds strong OOD robustness for MoT Future Expert and improvements over action-only baselines on long-horizon and dynamic tasks.

**Relevance:** 10
Back to [[topic](#topic-1)] [[top](#topics)]

<a id="1103-evidence-guided-counterfactual-learning-for-long-tailed-robotic-policies"></a>

### 1103\. [Evidence-Guided Counterfactual Learning for Long-Tailed Robotic Policies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12861)
**ArXiv:** 2610.12861 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12861)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.12861.pdf)]

**Authors:** Weiwei Chen, Chenghao Xu, Meiling Yu, Xu Yang, Cheng Deng

**Abstract:** In the practical deployment of vision--language--action policies, handling long-tailed task distributions is a common challenge due to the highly imbalanced composition of robot demonstration datasets. To investigate this issue, we study the head-task action bias in shared robot policies, particularly focusing on its relationship with task-specific visual evidence. Our experimental analysis shows that, when the visual evidence required by a tail task is insufficient, the policy increasingly falls back on action patterns reinforced by data-rich head tasks, resulting in degraded performance on under-represented tasks. Motivated by this observation, we propose Evidence-Guided Counterfactual Learning, a training framework for long-tailed robot policies. Our method identifies decision-relevant visual evidence using instruction-related and action-related signals, and constructs counterfactual observations by removing the selected evidence. The resulting counterfactual predictions are used to strengthen the association among task instructions, visual evidence, and actions, with the supervision adaptively adjusted according to the number of demonstrations available for each task. The proposed framework requires neither additional training data nor region-level annotations and incurs no additional computation during deployment. Extensive experiments across simulated benchmarks, different VLA architectures, and real-world robot platforms demonstrate that our method robustly and consistently improves long-tailed robot policy learning, particularly for tail tasks, while maintaining reliable performance on head tasks.

**Comment:** Criterion 1: Evidence-Guided Counterfactual Learning strengthens instruction–visual evidence–action associations in vision-language-action robot policies, improving long-tailed task performance across simulated benchmarks, VLA architectures, and real-world robots without additional training data.

**Relevance:** 9
Back to [[topic](#topic-1)] [[top](#topics)]

<a id="1104-human2dex-dexterous-robot-learning-from-wrist-centric-human-demonstrations"></a>

### 1104\. [Human2Dex: Dexterous Robot Learning from Wrist-Centric Human Demonstrations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13119)
**ArXiv:** 2610.13119 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13119)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.13119.pdf)]

**Authors:** Jiachen Zhang, Yuanyuan Li, Shaqi Luo

**Abstract:** Collecting dexterous manipulation demonstrations for each robot hand requires repeated task-specific data acquisition. We ask whether one shared corpus of natural human demonstrations can instead serve as a reusable data interface for different dexterous hands, without collecting task demonstrations on each target robot. We present Human2Dex, a wrist-centric human demonstration interface that separates shared human observation and motion semantics from embodiment-specific execution. It aligns observations through local hand-appearance adaptation and a shared 21-point hand representation with consistent structural semantics across human and robot observations. It constructs executable supervision by retargeting fused human hand motion to each target hand, and introduces the Grasp-Pocket Interaction Representation (GPIR) to express tracked objects relative to a hand-centered Functional Grasp Pocket. The corpus is converted into separate training sets for Linker O6 and Wuji, while action labels, normalization, training, and diffusion-policy checkpoints remain hand-specific. Across six real-world tasks, Human2Dex achieves 109/120 (90.8%) successes on Linker O6 and 97/120 (80.8%) on Wuji. Across both hands, Human-Raw achieves 63/240 (26.3%) successes, whereas the complete Human2Dex configuration achieves 206/240 (85.8%). These results support the use of wrist-centric human demonstrations as a reusable interface across calibrated dexterous hands while retaining embodiment-specific policy learning. The project website is publicly available at https://human2dex.pages.dev/.

**Comment:** Criterion 1: Human2Dex trains hand-specific diffusion policies using retargeted wrist-centric human demonstrations, achieving 85.8% combined success across two dexterous robot hands on six real-world manipulation tasks.

**Relevance:** 9
Back to [[topic](#topic-1)] [[top](#topics)]

<a id="1105-sampling-based-kinodynamic-planning-with-world-models"></a>

### 1105\. [Sampling-based Kinodynamic Planning with World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12910)
**ArXiv:** 2610.12910 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12910)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.12910.pdf)]

**Authors:** Yitian Gao, Hanlan Yang, Zachary Kingston

**Abstract:** Visual world models predict the consequences of actions from images, but planning with them is typically done by optimizers such as CEM and MPPI. However, these optimizers stall on long-horizon tasks where progress requires motion the objective does not reward, such as moving around a block before pushing it. This paper revisits kinodynamic sampling-based motion planning for world models, using the learned model as the propagator of a tree search in latent space. Our key insight is that the sampling strategies devised for high-dimensional search in configuration space apply directly to the latent space of a world model, where they rapidly expand through intermediate states that myopic optimizers never sample. We present two exploration strategies for the high-dimensional latent space, nearest-neighbor-guided RRT and projection-based KPIECE, with GPU-native batched expansion for efficient device utilization. We evaluate with an off-the-shelf DINO-WM on four simulated domains and a real-world block-pushing task. Our approaches match or exceed the success rate of CEM and MPPI at up to 13.8x lower planning time in simulation, and improve the success rate by 20% on the real robot.

**Comment:** Criterion 1: Uses the learned DINO-WM world model for latent-space robotic planning with RRT and KPIECE, improving real-world block-pushing success by 20% over CEM and MPPI.

**Relevance:** 9
Back to [[topic](#topic-1)] [[top](#topics)]

<a id="1109-disentangling-task-relevant-latent-dynamics-for-reward-modeling"></a>

### 1109\. [Disentangling Task-Relevant Latent Dynamics for Reward Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14159)
**ArXiv:** 2610.14159 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14159)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.14159.pdf)]

**Authors:** Dayou Li, Jionghao Wang, Peihao Wang, ..., Wenping Wang, Zhiwen Fan

**Abstract:** Reward design remains a major bottleneck for reinforcement learning in robotic manipulation. Handcrafted dense rewards can compensate for the limited guidance provided by sparse rewards, but require substantial task-specific reward engineering. Learning rewards from videos provides a scalable, data-driven alternative to manual reward design. However, existing video-based rewards are often computed in entangled visual spaces where task-relevant state changes are confounded with extraneous visual variation. As a result, the learned reward may respond to visual distractors instead of actual task execution. To address this limitation, we propose DiTaR (Disentangling Task-Relevant Latent Dynamics for Reward Modeling), a framework for learning dense rewards from heterogeneous videos. DiTaR learns a continuous task-relevant representation from multi-frame video latents through an asymmetric two-stage procedure that suppresses task-irrelevant visual variation. In the task-relevant latent space, we train a history- and language-conditioned rectified-flow model to capture task dynamics and derive a directional dense reward from temporal changes in dynamics compatibility. Together, these components enable reward modeling directly over task-relevant dynamics rather than generic visual variation. DiTaR substantially improves policy learning on MetaWorld-v3 and ManiSkill over video-based reward baselines, and transfers to real-world offline RL through reward relabeling. Reward analyses further show that DiTaR provides more reliable rewards.

**Comment:** Criterion 1: DiTaR uses a history- and language-conditioned rectified-flow model of task dynamics to derive dense robotic-manipulation rewards, improving policy learning on MetaWorld-v3 and ManiSkill and transferring to real-world offline RL.

**Relevance:** 9
Back to [[topic](#topic-1)] [[top](#topics)]

<a id="1114-remembering-what-matters-for-long-horizon-robotic-world-models"></a>

### 1114\. [Remembering What Matters for Long-Horizon Robotic World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14583)
**ArXiv:** 2610.14583 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14583)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.14583.pdf)]

**Authors:** Hao Wu, Tianyu Shao, Shengju Qian, ..., Ming-Hsuan Yang, Yuxuan Liang

**Abstract:** Autoregressive robot video world models based on discrete visual tokens remain unreliable over long rollout horizons. Retaining the entire generated history does not necessarily improve prediction quality. We investigate a possible memory-role conflict in Full Context decoding: persistent scene information, recent visual-action states, and stale generated history compete for attention despite their different predictive roles. This perspective motivates ReCAP (Receding Context with Anchor Preservation), a training-free context-scheduling method for long-horizon robot video world models. ReCAP keeps the pretrained tokenizer, world model, and visual decoder frozen. It preserves the initial scene anchor and a contiguous window of recent complete visual-action blocks while evicting older generated history. The schedule preserves within-block structure and temporal order, requires no additional modules or parameter updates, and bounds active-context growth independently of rollout length. We evaluate ReCAP on RT-1, BridgeV2, LIBERO-90, and CALVIN using frozen autoregressive world models. Across evaluated environments and horizons, ReCAP improves visual fidelity, action-conditioned consistency, and robot structure preservation relative to Full Context. On BridgeV2, it reduces LPIPS by 63.3%. In the system benchmark at H=64, it reduces the logical KV footprint by 73.3% and p50 latency by 6.51%. Attention analyses show increased attention to retained recent states, while history interventions associate larger amounts of stale context with higher prediction error. These observations are consistent with the memory-role-conflict hypothesis, although they do not isolate it from context-length and position-handling effects. Our results support inference-time context scheduling as a practical approach to improving long-horizon robot video prediction and computational efficiency without retraining.

**Comment:** Criterion 1: ReCAP improves long-horizon generative robot world models through training-free, anchor-preserving context scheduling, reducing LPIPS by 63.3% on BridgeV2 with additional evaluations on RT-1, LIBERO-90, and CALVIN.

**Relevance:** 9
Back to [[topic](#topic-1)] [[top](#topics)]

<a id="1119-barrier-constrained-guidance-for-real-time-asynchronous-vla-execution"></a>

### 1119\. [Barrier-Constrained Guidance For Real-time Asynchronous VLA Execution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14997)
**ArXiv:** 2610.14997 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14997)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.14997.pdf)]

**Authors:** Wenbo Yu, Jifan Li, Wenke Xia, ..., Zhengping Che, Di hu

**Abstract:** While asynchronous execution enables VLA models to operate in real-time environments by predicting the next chunk during execution, it inevitably introduces boundary misalignment with the committed action stream. Existing delay-aware solutions address this at the prohibitive cost of extensive retraining, architectural modifications, or fixed-prefix inpainting that inflates computation when low latency is vital. To address these problems, we propose FreeBar, a training-free real-time inference plugin that enables robust asynchronous execution with only marginal additional inference cost by casting chunk alignment in flow-based VLAs as barrier-guided action-space constraint. Specifically, FreeBar introduces a lightweight Control Barrier Function (CBF)-inspired consistency constraint between consecutive action chunks and solves the resulting correction in closed form, avoiding costly backpropagation through the VLA action head. By progressively tightening the consistency bound during generation, FreeBar aligns generated chunks with the committed execution stream while preserving early-stage generative and within-chunk future action flexibility,. Across Kinetix, RoboCasa, and diverse real-world robotic platforms with multiple VLA backbones, FreeBar improves success rates over naive asynchronous execution by 10%--15% on average, with larger gains in highly dynamic scenarios. It also preserves real-time computational efficiency, decreasing inference latency per action chunk over 45% than RTC. Additionally, as a universal plugin, FreeBar remains robust across distinct latency environments, base policies, and hyperparameter settings.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 1: FreeBar introduces training-free, closed-form barrier-guided action alignment for flow-based VLAs, improving robotic execution success by 10–15% on average across Kinetix, RoboCasa, and real-world platforms while reducing per-chunk inference latency by over 45% relative to RTC.

**Relevance:** 9
Back to [[topic](#topic-1)] [[top](#topics)]

<a id="1124-same-goal-different-words-spatial-instruction-sensitivity-in-world-action-models"></a>

### 1124\. [Same Goal, Different Words: Spatial Instruction Sensitivity in World-Action Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16242)
**ArXiv:** 2610.16242 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16242)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.16242.pdf)]

**Authors:** Ali-Adeeb Abbas, Esmaeil Seraj, Behrad Toghi, Taşkın Padır

**Abstract:** World-action models should preserve the intended manipulation across equivalent descriptions of a spatial goal. We evaluate this consistency in 864 rollouts from three models across four RoboLab scenes, holding initial states and task goals fixed. Describing relations through the reference object rather than the object-to-move lowers the rate of reaching a stable placement by 12.2 percentage points on average. The effect of instruction rephrasing varies across spatial relations, so aggregate scores can hide sensitivity to wording. Standalone tests of the policies' language understanding identify relation reversals and show that adding images can disrupt otherwise correct text-only interpretations. Rollout analysis also distinguishes spatial placement from instruction following: moving the wrong object can satisfy the requested relation, and rates of stable placement fall from 43.2% at any time to 21.8% at episode end. These findings motivate a more thorough evaluation of world-action models' steerability, language understanding, and grounding. Evaluations should test consistency across equivalent instructions, verify that the intended object moved, and check whether the requested arrangement persists to the end of the rollout.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 1 by evaluating instruction grounding in three world-action models across 864 RoboLab manipulation rollouts, finding that equivalent spatial rephrasings reduce stable-placement success by 12.2 percentage points on average.

**Relevance:** 9
Back to [[topic](#topic-1)] [[top](#topics)]

<a id="1126-unilap-a-unified-latent-action-policy-for-cross-embodiment-manipulation"></a>

### 1126\. [UniLAP: A Unified Latent Action Policy for Cross-Embodiment Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16969)
**ArXiv:** 2610.16969 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16969)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.16969.pdf)]

**Authors:** Lei Yao, Qiao Feng, Mutian Tong, ..., Lap-Pui Chau, Lingjie Liu

**Abstract:** Latent Action Models (LAMs) can capture manipulation priors from action-free videos, but discrete latents usually impose a granularity bottleneck, limiting their ability to represent fine-grained action. Recent continuous alternatives mitigate this, but they are typically used as proxies for world modeling and require a further planning step to produce executable actions. We thus introduce UniLAP, a unified continuous latent-action policy that models the latent space via flow matching and bypasses reliance on future frames during inference. By distilling a LAM teacher with a flow-adaptive objective, UniLAP inherits the teacher's action representation while retaining the multimodal distribution of feasible behaviors. To decouple latent representations from task-irrelevant appearance, we extract a label-free motion target to isolate relevant kinematic cues from spurious visual dynamics. With the established embodiment-agnostic latent manifold, UniLAP can be easily adapted to novel platforms. Pretrained on multi-source videos at a compact 1.1B parameter scale, UniLAP outperforms state-of-the-art latent-action and Vision-Language-Action (VLA) baselines across hand pose forecasting, single-arm and humanoid manipulation, and real-world deployment.

**Comment:** Criterion 1: UniLAP uses flow matching and flow-adaptive distillation of a latent-action teacher to learn generative manipulation policies from action-free videos, outperforming latent-action and VLA baselines across single-arm manipulation, humanoid manipulation, and real-world deployment.

**Relevance:** 9
Back to [[topic](#topic-1)] [[top](#topics)]

<a id="1130-prt-flow-probability-tractable-flow-matching-for-reinforcement-learning"></a>

### 1130\. [PrT-Flow: Probability-Tractable Flow Matching for Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17667)
**ArXiv:** 2610.17667 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17667)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.17667.pdf)]

**Authors:** Congzhong Sun, Ning Tan

**Abstract:** Diffusion models and Flow Matching can represent flexible distributions over robot actions, but many reinforcement learning algorithms also require the probability of each action. We introduce PrT-Flow (Probability-Tractable Flow Matching), which computes probability density while retaining multimodal, multi-step action generation. Its Jacobian-Tractable Transformer (JTT) makes each sampling step invertible and enables efficient computation of its Jacobian determinant. We train these finite steps with Conditional Euler-step Training (CET), which supervises their displacement over one step during sampling rather than their instantaneous velocity. We prove that, under certain assumptions, CET has the same minimizers as fitting the Euler-solver updates of Flow Matching. PrT-Flow achieves 96% success on Robomimic Lift after 300 epochs of imitation learning, matching the diffusion policy reference, and 96% success on OGBench Cube-Triple after 600k online steps. These results demonstrate that invertible networks with tractable probability densities can learn effective robot policies and support SAC-style actor-critic training. Our code is available at https://github.com/Sunlight-zero/PrT-Flow .

**Comment:** Criterion 1: PrT-Flow applies probability-tractable flow matching to robotic manipulation policy learning, achieving 96% success on Robomimic Lift while enabling SAC-style actor–critic training through tractable action densities.

**Relevance:** 9
Back to [[topic](#topic-1)] [[top](#topics)]

<a id="1132-the-human-vla-communication-gap-what-humans-leave-implicit-and-what-policies-cannot-recover"></a>

### 1132\. [The Human--VLA Communication Gap: What Humans Leave Implicit and What Policies Cannot Recover](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17746)
**ArXiv:** 2610.17746 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17746)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.17746.pdf)]

**Authors:** Yiqing Xu, Jiajun Wu

**Abstract:** A robot may know how to perform a task yet fail when a human asks for it naturally. We study this human--VLA communication gap by separating an \emph{information gap}, where task semantics, grounding, or event structure remain unresolved, from a residual \emph{execution gap}, where even a fully specified task depends on how it is organized and invoked. To do this, we characterize how people naturally express demonstrated objectives and evaluate controlled variants of the same physical tasks, with detailed analysis on $π_{0.5}$ and consistent trends on $π_0$-FAST and Cosmos3-Edge. Our study reveals that humans increasingly compress instructions as tasks grow, relying more on contextual grounding and implicit execution structure. Current policies are misaligned with this behavior: they tolerate grounding dependence relatively well, but struggle when task semantics or event structure must be reconstructed. Even after the information gap is resolved, execution remains sensitive to command granularity and invocation state. These findings identify concrete targets for human-centered policy learning and interfaces that better bridge natural human objectives to robot behavior. Project website: https://commgap.github.io/.

**Comment:** Criterion 1: Studies instruction following and task grounding in robotic VLA policies, using controlled evaluations of π0.5, π0-FAST, and Cosmos3-Edge to reveal failures caused by implicit task semantics, event structure, and command granularity.

**Relevance:** 9
Back to [[topic](#topic-1)] [[top](#topics)]

<a id="1133-accrue-continual-off-policy-reinforcement-learning-for-robot-foundation-models"></a>

### 1133\. [ACCRUE: Continual Off-Policy Reinforcement Learning for Robot Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18596)
**ArXiv:** 2610.18596 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18596)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.18596.pdf)]

**Authors:** Changyeon Kim, Kimin Lee, Yashraj Narang, Yijie Guo

**Abstract:** Reinforcement learning (RL) post-training improves robot foundation models (RFMs) beyond their demonstrations, but real-world deployment demands more: learning new tasks efficiently while retaining previously acquired behaviors. Existing continual RL methods on top of VLAs mitigate forgetting but rely on on-policy updates with limited forward transfer, whereas off-policy methods improve single-task sample efficiency without addressing learner persistence across tasks. We propose ACCRUE, a continual off-policy RL framework that carries lightweight actor and critic networks across tasks, allowing each stage to reuse behavioral and value knowledge acquired through prior RL. The inherited critic adapts only on current-task transitions and guides current-task actor learning, while prior behavior is preserved by distilling the actor toward cached targets on a small number of retained episodes. Fixed visual features keep the inherited learner and its replay compatible with base-policy updates. Across contact-rich assembly sequences in simulation and on real-robot hardware, ACCRUE accelerates subsequent-task learning with near-zero forgetting and significantly reduces real-robot training time by 31.0\% to 68.5\%.

**Comment:** Criterion 1: ACCRUE improves VLA-based robot policy learning through continual off-policy RL with inherited actor–critic networks and cached-target distillation, reducing real-robot training time by 31.0–68.5% on contact-rich assembly tasks.

**Relevance:** 9
Back to [[topic](#topic-1)] [[top](#topics)]

<a id="1134-worldoracle-benchmarking-action-conditioned-world-models-against-real-world-executions"></a>

### 1134\. [WorldOracle: Benchmarking Action-Conditioned World Models against Real-World Executions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18826)
**ArXiv:** 2610.18826 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18826)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.18826.pdf)]

**Authors:** Haoyi Duan, Liliang Chen, Nan Wang, ..., Maoqing Yao, Guanghui Ren

**Abstract:** We introduce WorldOracle, a benchmark specifically designed to evaluate action-conditioned world models (ACWMs) against paired real-world executions. Given the same initial observation and low-level action sequence, WorldOracle asks whether a predicted rollout reproduces what happens in the real world, including physical consequences and task-state transitions. We evaluate models along four complementary dimensions: physical state correctness, interaction spatial alignment, key interaction area fidelity, and multi-view action coherence. To support this evaluation, we curate real-world training and evaluation suites that cover different interaction dynamics and embodiments. The evaluation suite contains long, multi-segment episodes with both successful and failed executions, enabling evaluation across varied action outcomes and behavior distributions. Experiments on seven representative ACWMs reveal distinct model strengths and failure modes. WorldOracle provides a systematic foundation for measuring and advancing action-conditioned world modeling for manipulation. We will release the datasets, evaluation code, and leaderboard.

**Comment:** Criterion 1: WorldOracle benchmarks seven action-conditioned world models for robotic manipulation against paired real-world executions, evaluating physical state correctness, interaction spatial alignment, key interaction area fidelity, and multi-view action coherence.

**Relevance:** 9

<a id="1136-what-matters-in-quality-aware-vla-pretraining-a-benchmark-and-empirical-study"></a>

### 1136\. [What Matters in Quality-Aware VLA Pretraining: A Benchmark and Empirical Study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18869)
**ArXiv:** 2610.18869 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18869)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.18869.pdf)]

**Authors:** Mingxuan Yan, Litian Gong, Dihong Huang, ..., Jianfei Yang, Jiachen Li

**Abstract:** How should demonstration quality be used during vision-language-action (VLA) pretraining to improve performance after target-task fine-tuning? We compare weighting, filtering, and conditioning across reward definitions and label granularities using 5,000 naturally suboptimal human demonstrations and 24 target tasks. Within each VLA, we match demonstrations and training budgets. Additional comparisons examine pretraining data size and the roles of quality tags during fine-tuning and inference. Across three VLAs, we find that approach-aware episode conditioning improves mean success over plain behavioral cloning; conditioning and filtering favor episode and chunk labels, respectively, on average, with model-dependent reversals; and constant-tag fine-tuning improves mean success after conditioned pretraining with either tagged or untagged inference. We introduce AXIS-Bench to support these controlled comparisons. Built on crowdsourced simulation demonstrations, it provides recorded object and contact states for recomputing quality labels on shared trajectories. Replayable trajectories support workspace-aligned rendering and user-defined real-world target tasks. In our real-world experiment, simulation pretraining raises success from 9.6\% to 31.7\%. Project page: https://axis-bench.github.io/.

**Comment:** Criterion 1: This study develops quality-aware VLA policy pretraining through approach-aware episode conditioning and controlled comparisons on AXIS-Bench, with simulation pretraining raising real-world task success from 9.6% to 31.7%.

<a id="1138-fact-a-multimodal-force-action-track-foundation-model-for-human-to-robot-dexterity"></a>

### 1138\. [FACT: A Multimodal Force-Action-Track Foundation Model for Human-to-Robot Dexterity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19155)
**ArXiv:** 2610.19155 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19155)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.19155.pdf)]

**Authors:** Zhi Cao, Yining Hong, Jiaolong Yang, ..., Jiajun Wu, Huang Huang

**Abstract:** Human demonstrations offer a scalable alternative to costly robot data collection for dexterous manipulation, but most human datasets lack force measurements. Dexterous manipulation requires not only visual and proprioceptive understanding but also the ability to anticipate and regulate interaction forces. To scale force-aware human-robot co-training, we introduce EgoForce, a large-scale human tactile dataset capturing force-rich manipulation experience, and combine it with robot demonstrations for pretraining. We present FACT, a robot foundation model that cascades from prospective visual planning to force-aware refinement for dexterous manipulation. FACT comprises two jointly trained components. A Track-Guided Coarse Planner jointly predicts actions, future video, and visual tracks. Future video and visual tracks also provide a shared task representation that facilitates cross-embodiment knowledge transfer and coarse action generation. Building on this coarse plan, a Force-Action Joint Refiner jointly predicts future force and refines the coarse action, allowing anticipated force and action to shape each other during refinement. We evaluate the model on ten tasks requiring fine-grained coordination and force feedback, including tool use, musical instruments, and precision laboratory procedures. Using as few as 10 robot trajectories with 90 human trajectories for fine-tuning, FACT outperforms both baselines, with success-rate gains of up to 60 percentage points over T-Rex. Ablations show that force supervision in human pretraining data is crucial for downstream robot performance after fine-tuning, while visual track prediction and training with future-force prediction improve dexterous execution. Experiments varying the human-robot data ratio during fine-tuning further suggest that FACT learns from human demonstrations nearly as effectively as from robot demonstrations.

**Comment:** Criterion 1 (robot world/action models): FACT’s Track-Guided Coarse Planner jointly predicts future video, visual tracks, and robot actions for force-aware dexterous manipulation, achieving success-rate gains of up to 60 percentage points over T-Rex.

**Relevance:** 9

<a id="1141-robospan-learning-modular-coordination-policies-for-high-dof-whole-body-mobile-manipulation-with-teleoperation"></a>

### 1141\. [RoboSpan: Learning Modular Coordination Policies for High-DoF Whole-Body Mobile Manipulation with Teleoperation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19594)
**ArXiv:** 2610.19594 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19594)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.19594.pdf)]

**Authors:** Keqiang Ren, Xuecheng Ye, Hanyu Liu, ..., Xinran Li, Zhigong Song

**Abstract:** Whole-body mobile manipulation suffers from two fundamental challenges: inherent motion heterogeneity across locomotion, arm, and hand modules, and progressive error accumulation over long-horizon multi-stage tasks. Existing teleoperation platforms also lack integrated dexterous tactile sensing to support coordinated whole-body decision-making. This paper presents RoboSpan, a high-DoF whole-body teleoperation and demonstration platform, and the Hierarchical Modular Framework (HMF) for learning modular coordination policies. HMF functionally decouples the 29-DoF action space into fine hand manipulation, arm pose adjustment, and mobile-base spatial configuration branches, and introduces the Coordinated Modular Embodied Transformer (COMET) with inter-modular cross-attention to enable dynamic information exchange and action coordination. Vision-language model driven hierarchical task decomposition structurally suppresses temporal error propagation, while a full-palm 2D tactile representation encodes distributed contact signals for cross-module tactile empowerment. Experiments on a physical 29-DoF robot demonstrate that the proposed method achieves over 20\% higher task success rate than mainstream baselines across a range of long-horizon whole-body tasks, markedly improving stability and robustness in contact-rich manipulation.

**Comment:** Criterion 1: RoboSpan uses VLM-driven hierarchical task decomposition and COMET inter-modular cross-attention for coordinated whole-body manipulation, reporting over 20% higher task success than mainstream baselines on a physical 29-DoF robot.

**Relevance:** 9

<a id="1142-forcebench-benchmarking-force-integration-for-robotic-manipulation"></a>

### 1142\. [ForceBench: Benchmarking Force Integration for Robotic Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19604)
**ArXiv:** 2610.19604 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19604)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.19604.pdf)]

**Authors:** Xuan Han, Zinuo Jin, Tianlin Li, ..., Ruihai Wu, Zhibo Pang

**Abstract:** Force feedback provides vision-language-action (VLA) policies with physical observations that can guide action selection and execution. However, differences in tasks, demonstrations, and control settings across existing systems hinder reliable comparisons of force integration designs. We introduce ForceBench, a simulation benchmark for evaluating six-axis wrist-force integration under a unified protocol. ForceBench combines eight tasks spanning physical-property inference, resistance-dependent interaction, and contact-rich assembly with 1,600 synchronized demonstrations and task-specific success criteria. We compare one no-force baseline and six force integration designs on a shared $π_{0.5}$ backbone. Prompt Fusion achieves the highest observed mean simulation success of 35.31%, compared with 23.25% for the baseline, and improves success rates across all three task categories. Predictive and temporal gains in simulation concentrate on physical-property inference, while both temporal designs underperform the baseline on assembly. Real-world evaluation of eight systems across five tasks supports the main qualitative trends: Prompt Fusion and Dual-Path Fusion achieve 63.00% mean success, compared with 40.00% for the baseline, but only Prompt Fusion improves every task. These findings support ForceBench's practical value for identifying the benefits and limitations of force integration across manipulation demands. The project page is available at https://forcebench-platform.github.io/.

**Comment:** Criteria 1 and 7: ForceBench provides eight simulated manipulation tasks and 1,600 synchronized demonstrations for evaluating force integration into VLA policies, with Prompt Fusion increasing mean simulation success from 23.25% to 35.31%.

<a id="1143-accelerating-vla-inference-with-perturbation-informed-execution"></a>

### 1143\. [Accelerating VLA Inference with Perturbation Informed Execution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19656)
**ArXiv:** 2610.19656 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19656)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.19656.pdf)]

**Authors:** Jehun Lee, Hajun Lee, Jimin Ryu, ..., Jonghyun Choi, Jae-Joon Kim

**Abstract:** Action-chunking vision-language-action (VLA) models predict sequences of future robot actions. Under fixed-horizon execution, the robot executes a fixedlength prefix of each predicted chunk before invoking the policy again, leaving the remaining predictions unused. For a given number of executed actions, short execution horizons require more policy calls. Extending the execution horizon amortizes policy inference cost over more actions. However, uniformly applying long horizons across episodes can limit opportunities for precise corrections. To address this limitation, we propose PICES (Perturbation-Informed Chunk and Execution Horizon Selection), a training-free method that jointly selects an action chunk and the number of actions executed before the next policy call. Within a single policy call, the action expert generates a batch of action chunks from multiple initial noise samples and their locally perturbed counterparts, using the same conditioning features across all samples. PICES exploits differences between corresponding actions in each nominal chunk and its counterpart to determine candidate-specific execution horizons and select a nominal chunk for execution. Experiments on simulation benchmarks and real-world manipulation tasks demonstrate that PICES achieves speedup by up to 2.3x in wall-clock policy inference time on Jetson Orin and Thor relative to fixed horizon baselines while improving the success rate.

**Comment:** Criterion 1: PICES uses perturbation-informed action-chunk and execution-horizon selection for VLA manipulation policies, achieving up to 2.3× lower wall-clock policy inference time while improving success on simulated and real-world manipulation tasks.

**Relevance:** 9
Back to [[topic](#topic-1)] [[top](#topics)]

<a id="1145-refine-actions-if-needed-adaptive-action-chunk-refinement-for-robot-manipulation"></a>

### 1145\. [Refine Actions If Needed: Adaptive Action Chunk Refinement for Robot Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19765)
**ArXiv:** 2610.19765 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19765)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.19765.pdf)]

**Authors:** Hanlin Liu, Zichen Zhou, Jiacheng Zhang, ..., Gen Li, Fangqiang Ding

**Abstract:** Computationally lightweight manipulation policies predict executable actions with low inference latency, but prediction errors can limit task success. Iterative generative policies can achieve higher task success rates, but repeated network evaluations increase inference latency. Refining action proposals from computationally lightweight policies can combine these strengths, but the amount of refinement needed varies across states. We introduce RAIN (Refine Actions If Needed), a framework that predicts action errors at different refinement levels to decide whether and how much to refine each executable proposal, before performing any refinement. We train an ensemble to predict these errors by comparing action proposals and their refined versions with expert actions from offline demonstrations, without requiring explicit labels of refinement needs. Calibration balances uncertainty, computation, and temporal consistency. We evaluate RAIN on eight ManiSkill tasks and four real-robot tasks. In simulation, our policy achieves 66.6% success, approaching the best evaluated diffusion-policy success rate with 5.71 times lower policy latency. On the real robot, success increases from 55.0% with Action Chunking with Transformers (ACT) to 72.5%, with comparable average successful completion time. Matched controls show that dynamic routing maintains the strongest observed fixed-refinement success level with 26.6% fewer refiner evaluations. Project page: https://rain-policy.github.io/.

**Comment:** Criterion 1: RAIN adaptively allocates generative refinement to robot manipulation action chunks, achieving 66.6% success on eight ManiSkill tasks at 5.71× lower latency than the best evaluated diffusion policy and increasing real-robot success from 55.0% to 72.5%.

**Relevance:** 9

<a id="1158-surgcast-action-conditioned-future-skeletons-for-controllable-surgical-world-models"></a>

### 1158\. [SurgCast: Action-Conditioned Future Skeletons for Controllable Surgical World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15645)
**ArXiv:** 2610.15645 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15645)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.15645.pdf)]

**Authors:** Wanhao Liu, Rulin Zhou, Liangjing Shao, ..., Panshuo Li, Hongliang Ren

**Abstract:** Surgical world models must turn prospective robot actions into visually faithful, structurally consistent instrument motion. We introduce SurgCast, which predicts future instrument skeletons from current structure, robot state, and future actions, then uses the forecast to control video generation. Dual-path injection combines skeleton geometry with derived, instrument-specific motion descriptors through geometric residuals and conditioning modulation. Both paths complement native action conditioning and are retained in a four-step causal student. On SutureBot and SRTH-Porcine-Cholei, the teacher and student improve all seven reported quality and instrument-consistency metrics over their respective direct-action baselines; teacher FVD decreases by 37.6% and 67.3%. Ablations show that future actions improve skeleton prediction and that combining both injection paths performs best across all six metrics under predicted skeletons. These results support using predicted articulated structure and its motion semantics as a control interface for surgical video prediction. Project videos and examples are available at https://surgcast.netlify.app/.

**Comment:** Criterion 1: SurgCast develops an action-conditioned surgical robot world model using predicted instrument skeletons to guide video generation, reducing teacher FVD by 37.6% and 67.3% on SutureBot and SRTH-Porcine-Cholei.

**Relevance:** 8

<a id="1161-viewstruct-view-grounded-articulation-and-connectivity-inference"></a>

### 1161\. [ViewStruct: View-Grounded Articulation and Connectivity Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16793)
**ArXiv:** 2610.16793 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16793)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.16793.pdf)]

**Authors:** Wenjing Bian, Ranran Huang, Andreas Geiger

**Abstract:** Interacting with unfamiliar objects requires understanding how their parts connect and move. We introduce ViewStruct, a framework for jointly predicting connectivity and articulation in objects and scenes. Given an RGB image with aligned part segmentation and depth, optionally supplemented by images or video, ViewStruct predicts rigid-body groupings, attachments, and joint parameters. Its view-grounded representation links predictions to observed regions and expresses joint geometry in a gravity-aligned, view-relative frame, without requiring category-specific canonical orientations. A pretrained vision--language model is adapted to predict structure in seconds, without requiring observed articulation motion or per-instance optimisation. Experiments demonstrate improved structural prediction over evaluated baselines and transfer to unseen categories, asset sources, and real-world human-interaction videos, despite task-specific training exclusively on synthetic data. Simulated manipulation experiments further demonstrate the utility of predicted joints for generating robot motion trajectories.

**Comment:** Criterion 1: ViewStruct adapts a pretrained VLM to infer object connectivity and articulation, with simulated manipulation experiments using predicted joints to generate robot motion trajectories.

**Relevance:** 8
Back to [[topic](#topic-1)] [[top](#topics)]

<a id="1171-breaking-the-average-overcoming-convergent-defaults-in-robotic-manipulation-via-diagnostic-chain-of-thought-and-structured-guidance"></a>

### 1171\. [Breaking the Average: Overcoming Convergent Defaults in Robotic Manipulation via Diagnostic Chain-of-Thought and Structured Guidance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17983)
**ArXiv:** 2610.17983 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17983)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.17983.pdf)]

**Authors:** Mohammed Daba, Waleed Abdulrahman Saleh AlAkwa

**Abstract:** Recent Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models, such as $π_{0.5}$, have demonstrated impressive generalization capabilities by scaling behavior cloning across massive datasets. However, these models frequently suffer from distributional convergence, a phenomenon where the policy regresses to "safe" but suboptimal mean behaviors due to mode averaging in the training data. This often results in generic grasp strategies, Convergent Defaults, that fail in constrained or long-tail scenarios. In this work, we propose a novel framework to identify and correct these behaviors without parameter updates. We introduce a three-stage methodology: (1) Probing for Defaults, identifying task-specific mode collapse; (2) Diagnostic Root Cause Analysis, utilizing the VLA's latent reasoning capabilities to verbalize the logic behind suboptimal actions; and (3) Structured Guidance Injection, where concrete, distinct alternatives are encoded into reusable "Lens Prompts" that shift the sampling distribution. We validate this approach using $π_{0.5}$ in ManiSkill3 simulation environments. We hypothesize that our method outperforms vanilla $π_{0.5}$ and standard Chain-of-Thought prompting by effectively breaking convergent defaults in complex manipulation tasks.

**Comment:** Criterion 1: The framework uses diagnostic reasoning and reusable Lens Prompts to steer the π0.5 VLA's robotic manipulation behavior without parameter updates, with validation described in ManiSkill3.

**Relevance:** 8
Back to [[topic](#topic-1)] [[top](#topics)]

<a id="1173-imagining-goals-with-geometric-horizon-models-for-policy-generalization"></a>

### 1173\. [Imagining Goals with Geometric Horizon Models for Policy Generalization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19129)
**ArXiv:** 2610.19129 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19129)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.19129.pdf)]

**Authors:** Daniel Lawson, Dhruv Sreenivas, Adriana Hugessen, Khimya Khetarpal, Glen Berseth

**Abstract:** Training from offline data has allowed for substantial progress in domains such as robotics, leading to general-purpose policies that can be easily applied zero-shot or efficiently finetuned for downstream tasks. However, these policies can still have poor generalization, both due to the choice of modeling objective and from learning from a static dataset. In this work, we focus on the challenging task of zero-shot goal generalization, where a policy is evaluated on unseen tasks that require combining its existing knowledge (compositional generalization). An avenue for improving a policy's generalization is by generating new experience with world models; however, such generation has proven difficult for longer horizons. Thus, to alleviate this issue, we propose TD-Aug, which samples from a geometric horizon model and allows for directly imagining novel outcomes that can be achieved by composing existing knowledge. We demonstrate that training on these future outcomes as goals for goal-conditioned BC policies significantly improves generalization in stitching-based OGBench tasks.

**Comment:** Criterion 1: TD-Aug uses a geometric horizon world model to generate novel training goals for goal-conditioned behavioral cloning, significantly improving zero-shot compositional generalization on stitching-based OGBench tasks.

**Relevance:** 8
Back to [[topic](#topic-1)] [[top](#topics)]

<a id="1174-action-jets-learning-the-local-shape-of-the-action-conditioned-data-manifold"></a>

### 1174\. [Action Jets: Learning the Local Shape of the Action-conditioned Data Manifold](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19007)
**ArXiv:** 2610.19007 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19007)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.19007.pdf)]

**Authors:** Anton Arapin, Chahyon Ku, Bernadette Bucher

**Abstract:** World models are a powerful means of reasoning and planning, but many of these action-conditioned predictive models are high dimensional and computationally expensive. These challenges limit the number of action candidates that can be evaluated by a planner. We show that this cost is avoidable for planners over short horizons, which only require local information with respect to the current state. For a fixed state, the frames reachable by varying a low-dimensional action (a camera movement or an end-effector command) lie on a low-dimensional manifold. We estimate the local shape of that manifold directly via a network trained to estimate how each image region moves per unit of each action axis supervised by data collected from samples by the generator. A correction head fit to trajectory data estimates the shape of this manifold region up to a third-order Taylor polynomial. We call this network estimating the Taylor polynomial describing the manifold shape an Action Jet and compute its radius of validity in closed form. By traversing this approximation of the local manifold shape, we only have to perform inference once per state for any number of candidate actions. Scoring sixteen candidates from a single extraction is over 300x faster than with either source generator, and this gap widens with the number of candidates a planner evaluates. We measure against state-of-the-art action-conditioned generators, IRASim and WorldGym on BridgeData V2, a real 7-DoF manipulation benchmark, and FrameCrafter on DL3DV scenes, where we chart from the novel-view generators DepthSplat and LagerNVS. Because only the tangent comes from the generator and every other term from the data, the Action Jet also predicts held-out frames more accurately than IRASim, the generator that supplied its tangent, at short horizons and at over 100x less compute.

**Comment:** Criterion 1: Action Jets uses generative-model supervision to learn local Taylor approximations of action-conditioned visual dynamics on the BridgeData V2 manipulation benchmark, scoring 16 action candidates over 300× faster than its source generators.

**Relevance:** 8
Back to [[topic](#topic-1)] [[top](#topics)]

<a id="1175-sonoworld-towards-interactive-pose-controllable-ultrasound-world-models"></a>

### 1175\. [SonoWorld: Towards Interactive Pose-Controllable Ultrasound World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19327)
**ArXiv:** 2610.19327 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19327)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.19327.pdf)]

**Authors:** Zefan Yang, Zheyuan Zhang, Boris Maihe, ..., Terrence Chen, Shanhui Sun

**Abstract:** Robotic ultrasound can standardize image acquisition and reduce operator workload and variability, but policy learning remains limited by the lack of realistic, controllable simulation environments. While controllable world models have advanced rapidly in natural-image domains, ultrasound world modeling remains challenging because each frame captures only a cross-section of unobserved 3D anatomy, causing small probe motions to induce large appearance changes. Existing approaches rely on robot-tracked poses and latent-space prediction, but do not directly generate the observations required for interactive control. We propose SonoWorld, a causal ultrasound world model that learns video dynamics from unlabeled clinical videos and grounds them to 6-DoF control using a small set of freehand clinical scans tracked with an EM sensor. SonoWorld is trained in three stages: pretraining a latent-action-conditioned video diffusion model on around 6.9 million unlabeled multi-organ ultrasound frames; grounding the learned video dynamics to metric probe poses with a small EM-tracked thyroid dataset; and applying distribution matching distillation to enable causal, long-horizon autoregressive generation with selectively caching eviction tokens at inference time. We evaluate controllability through pose-following fidelity, round-trip consistency, and axis-specific control under synthetic actions on held-out tracked scans, and further validate it with a user study. In model-based action discrimination, SonoWorld correctly ranks the target-directed probe motion over an equal-magnitude opposite motion in up to 74.8% of trials. To our knowledge, SonoWorld is the first causal, pose-controllable ultrasound world model learned primarily from freehand clinical videos, providing a data-driven interactive environment for robotic ultrasound data acquisition and policy evaluation.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 1: SonoWorld grounds a video diffusion model pretrained on 6.9 million unlabeled ultrasound frames to 6-DoF probe control for robotic ultrasound simulation and policy evaluation, correctly ranking target-directed probe motions in up to 74.8% of trials.

**Relevance:** 8
Back to [[topic](#topic-1)] [[top](#topics)]
