<a id="2056-self-supervised-learning-of-semantically-consistent-visual-features-via-a-simple-3d-prior"></a>

### 2056\. [Self-Supervised Learning of Semantically Consistent Visual Features via a Simple 3D Prior](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16988)
**ArXiv:** 2610.16988 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16988)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.16988.pdf)]

**Authors:** Sebastian Day, Oisin Mac Aodha

**Abstract:** Visual features extracted from vision models like DINOv2 or Stable Diffusion enable high quality semantic correspondence between images. However, such features exhibit documented limitations that hinder accurate correspondence estimation such as the inability to disambiguate the left from right for categories exhibiting symmetries or repeated parts. Previous attempts to improve these features rely on using supervised data such as annotated keypoint pairs or camera pose information, or make restrictive topological assumptions, such as modelling the object of interest as a 3D sphere. We introduce a new self-supervised approach that allows us to learn features for topologically complex object categories using a simple prior. Instead of relying on an expensive instance-level analysis by synthesis pipeline, our approach makes use of contrastive learning and distribution matching at the global dataset-level to learn the coarse shape and appearance of a category. Combined with additional geometric losses, this enables us to replicate the global and local statistics of visual features which match those that are extracted from real images. Quantitative results are presented across several challenging object categories, demonstrating the effectiveness of our approach.

**Comment:** Criterion 2: proposes a self-supervised method combining contrastive learning, dataset-level distribution matching, and geometric losses with a simple 3D prior to improve visual features for semantic correspondence across challenging object categories.

**Relevance:** 10
Back to [[topic](#topic-2)] [[top](#topics)]

<a id="2058-growing-to-see-efficient-pretraining-of-self-supervised-vision-transformers"></a>

### 2058\. [Growing to See: Efficient Pretraining of Self-Supervised Vision Transformers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16817)
**ArXiv:** 2610.16817 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16817)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.16817.pdf)]

**Authors:** Amir Mohammad Karimi Mamaghan, Ferdinand Kapl, Andrea Dittadi, ..., Johannes von Oswald, Stefan Bauer

**Abstract:** Progressive depth growth builds large networks by starting with shallow models and inserting layers during training. Recent advances in large language models have renewed interest in this longstanding approach, demonstrating benefits for training efficiency and reasoning alongside changes in internal computations. However, how growing affects representation quality and computational patterns in self-supervised vision transformers remains unclear. In this work, we systematically investigate progressive depth growth in two popular self-supervised families, namely masked autoencoders (MAE) and DINOv3, and analyze how growing affects downstream performance, intermediate representations, and internal computations. Across global and dense tasks, we show that growing improves downstream performance while reducing training FLOPs by 13.9--24.8%, with ImageNet linear-probe accuracy gains of up to 3.36 percentage points. Growing also improves downstream performance at intermediate layers and reshapes internal computational patterns, introducing recurring alternations between global and localized attention in MAE and earlier local-to-global attention progression in self-distilled DINOv3. Together, these findings establish progressive depth growth as an effective and efficient pretraining strategy for improving downstream performance across global and dense visual tasks with less computation, and advance our understanding of how growing shapes visual computation.

**Comment:** Criterion 2: systematically investigates progressive depth growth in MAE and DINOv3 self-supervised pretraining, improving global and dense downstream performance while reducing training FLOPs by 13.9–24.8% and increasing ImageNet linear-probe accuracy by up to 3.36 percentage points.

**Relevance:** 10
Back to [[topic](#topic-2)] [[top](#topics)]

<a id="2108-event-based-action-recognition-by-pre-training-on-multi-frame-representations"></a>

### 2108\. [Event-based Action Recognition by Pre-Training on Multi-Frame Representations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13856)
**ArXiv:** 2610.13856 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13856)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.13856.pdf)]

**Authors:** Emma Norrby, Tomas Wilkinson, Per Nyblom, David Gustafsson

**Abstract:** Action recognition is a crucial task in domains like robotics, patient monitoring, or surveillance. Recently, event-based action recognition has attracted much attention due to the event camera's ability to capture fast motions and its improved privacy over conventional vision cameras. While frame-based event representations work well for short event sequences, their discriminative ability declines sharply with longer and more complex event sequences. In this work, we introduce a multi-frame representation that greatly improves recognition performance over its single-frame counterpart. We adopt a masked auto-encoding framework to pre-train a Vision Transformer using a sparsity-aware masking algorithm and token selection mechanism to improve downstream performance and control computational complexity. We also introduce multiple mask tokens for the decoder with a position-aware attention mechanism, leading to further improved performance. Our proposed method shows impressive performance for challenging long-duration and complex event sequences, yielding state-of-the-art performance on THU-EACT-50 CHL and DailyDVS-200.

**Comment:** Criterion 2: The paper advances masked-autoencoder pretraining for event-based video representations with sparsity-aware masking, token selection, and position-aware multiple decoder mask tokens, achieving state-of-the-art action recognition on THU-EACT-50 CHL and DailyDVS-200.

**Relevance:** 9
Back to [[topic](#topic-2)] [[top](#topics)]

<a id="2110-where-to-look-matters-rethinking-sub-volume-sampling-in-3d-medical-self-supervised-learning"></a>

### 2110\. [Where to Look Matters: Rethinking Sub-Volume Sampling in 3D Medical Self-supervised Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13915)
**ArXiv:** 2610.13915 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13915)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.13915.pdf)]

**Authors:** Junkai Liu, Le Zhang

**Abstract:** Self-supervised learning on 3D medical images commonly relies on random sub-volume sampling during pretraining. However, random cropping implicitly treats all spatial regions as equally worth observing, despite anatomical priors and spatial redundancy that make regions inherently unequal in their value for representation learning. To this end, we first show through controlled crop-quality manipulations that better sub-volumes lead to better downstream transfer, revealing the observation policy as an overlooked bottleneck in 3D medical SSL. Motivated by this finding, we revisit sub-volume sampling as a \emph{where-to-look} problem. We propose \textbf{\method}, a plug-and-play framework that replaces random cropping with an offline coarse-to-fine probe bank guided by \textit{anatomy-awareness}, \textit{informativeness}, and \textit{diversity}. We instantiate \method as a lightweight \lite variant using geometric and image-statistical cues, and a diagnostic \feat variant using frozen visual features, to examine whether lightweight volume-intrinsic priors suffice or richer semantic cues reliably provide additional value. Extensive experiments across multiple 3D medical SSL backbones and downstream tasks demonstrate that \method-\lite consistently improves transfer performance over random sampling. Mechanistic analyses further attribute these gains to more anatomically plausible and informative observations, reduced spatial redundancy, more efficient learning dynamics, and more structured representations.

**Comment:** Criterion 2: The paper replaces random sub-volume sampling with an anatomy-, informativeness-, and diversity-guided probe bank, consistently improving representation transfer across multiple 3D medical SSL backbones and downstream tasks.

**Relevance:** 9
Back to [[topic](#topic-2)] [[top](#topics)]

<a id="2112-variance-fingerprints-component-wise-identification-of-content-factors-by-augmentation-design"></a>

### 2112\. [Variance Fingerprints: Component-Wise Identification of Content Factors by Augmentation Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14260)
**ArXiv:** 2610.14260 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14260)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.14260.pdf)]

**Authors:** D. Hudson Smith

**Abstract:** Augmentation-based self-supervised learning can recover the content shared across views without separating its underlying factors into individual coordinates. We ask whether augmentations can be designed to make those factors identifiable. Our approach allows content factors to fluctuate across augmented views while preserving their mean for each source instance. Each instance receives a fixed, recorded set of augmentation strengths that controls how much its factors fluctuate. When factors respond differently to changes in these strengths, their patterns of variation provide information that distinguishes them. Under suitable decoder and support assumptions, we prove that these differences identify individual factors in a conditional Gaussian model, up to permutation, scaling, and translation. We then develop a learner that uses the recorded strengths to estimate these patterns jointly across instances, enabling learning from few views per instance. Synthetic experiments demonstrate near-perfect factor recovery through nonlinear mixing. Controlled dSprites experiments show improved coordinate alignment using augmentations that target known factors, even without telling the learner which augmentation corresponds to which representation coordinate. These results suggest a route to component identification in self-supervised learning: augmentation design can supply identifying information beyond shared content alone.

**Comment:** Criterion 2: This augmentation-based SSL method uses recorded augmentation strengths to identify individual content factors, with theoretical guarantees under a conditional Gaussian model and improved representation-coordinate alignment on controlled dSprites experiments.

**Relevance:** 9
Back to [[topic](#topic-2)] [[top](#topics)]

<a id="2118-more-learning-motion-oriented-registers-for-driving-world-models-and-planning"></a>

### 2118\. [MORE: Learning Motion-Oriented Registers for Driving World Models and Planning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14871)
**ArXiv:** 2610.14871 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14871)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.14871.pdf)]

**Authors:** Yingdong Gu, Haoyu Wang, Shaocheng Yan, ..., Jiayuan Li, Pai Peng

**Abstract:** By predicting future states, latent world models provide rich self-supervision for end-to-end driving, but often inherit dense features from pretrained visual encoders as their world-state interface. This creates a mismatch: visual pretraining produces information-rich, spatially dense features, but their adaptation for motion-conditioned world modeling and planning is deferred to downstream training. The dense grid also makes future prediction more computationally expensive as visual resolution increases. We therefore argue that video pretraining should directly learn state representations suited to downstream world modeling and planning, rather than relying on downstream training to adapt inherited visual features. We propose ***MORE***, a V-JEPA framework that learns **M**otion-**O**riented **RE**gisters during video pretraining as a shared state interface for driving world models and planning. MORE injects temporally aligned ego motion into a small set of registers and uses them as the sole context for masked patch prediction, channeling dense patch-level supervision through a compact state bottleneck. A complementary blockwise register-masking objective further shapes predictive structure in the register space. During downstream training, the same register space defines the future states predicted by the world model and provides shared scene memory for trajectory generation and scoring. On NAVSIM-v2, MORE outperforms the state of the art by 1.6 in EPDMS, while achieving superior efficiency with only 1/10 the inference latency of DriveLaW. These results support learning the state interface for world modeling and planning directly during video pretraining.

**Comment:** Criterion 2: MORE modifies V-JEPA video pretraining with ego-motion-conditioned registers, masked patch prediction, and blockwise register masking to learn compact predictive representations, outperforming the state of the art by 1.6 EPDMS on NAVSIM-v2.

**Relevance:** 9
Back to [[topic](#topic-2)] [[top](#topics)]

<a id="2121-world-modeling-through-spectral-alignment"></a>

### 2121\. [World Modeling through Spectral Alignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15429)
**ArXiv:** 2610.15429 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15429)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.15429.pdf)]

**Authors:** Holger Molin, William Peng, Marco Bagatella, Randall Balestriero

**Abstract:** How do we tell a world model what information to preserve? We study this question through the lens of spectral representation learning. We introduce SpecWM, a JEPA framework that explicitly specifies desired relationships between observations through a target similarity kernel and trains the encoder to reproduce those relationships in its embedding geometry. Different kernels provide different supervision, ranging from physical state information to a fully self-supervised temporal kernel requiring only the ordering of observations within trajectories. We characterize the global optima of the objective, establish conditions for linear recoverability and invariance to nuisance information, and connect temporal-kernel similarity to the behavioral successor measure under an idealized setting. Empirically, the choice of kernel strongly controls which physical quantities are recoverable from the representation. More importantly, temporal spectral alignment improves CEM planning success over LeWorldModel from 52% to 68% on OGBench Cube, 49% to 77% on OGBench Scene, and 29% to 51% on CALVIN. These results suggest that explicitly specifying representation geometry provides a simple way to steer what latent world models preserve for control.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 2 through SpecWM, a JEPA representation-learning method with a fully self-supervised temporal similarity kernel, theoretical guarantees on recoverability and nuisance invariance, and improved CALVIN planning success from 29% to 51%.

**Relevance:** 9
Back to [[topic](#topic-2)] [[top](#topics)]

<a id="2127-dime-scaling-facial-representation-learning-via-differential-masked-autoencoding"></a>

### 2127\. [DIME: Scaling Facial Representation Learning via Differential Masked Autoencoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17015)
**ArXiv:** 2610.17015 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17015)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.17015.pdf)]

**Authors:** Hao Yu, Haoyu Chen, Hui Wei, ..., Nicu Sebe, Guoying Zhao

**Abstract:** Scaling up data and model capacity has driven substantial progress in visual representation learning. However, conventional pretext tasks exhibit diminishing returns on fine-grained facial analysis. We attribute this to a scaling bottleneck: these tasks model each image's appearance and are dominated by identity, pose, and illumination, so subtle within-identity facial differences contribute little to the training signal. We introduce DIME, a differential masked autoencoder for large-scale facial representation learning. DIME builds on a simple observation: two images of the same person share identity-specific appearance, so their difference exposes facial changes. DIME reconstructs both masked images in an identity-matched pair and, through an Explicit Differential Direction Supervision (EDDS) objective, aligns the direction of their reconstructed difference with the true difference, while source isolation makes each reconstruction depend only on its own image. Shared appearance cancels in this difference, and because EDDS ignores magnitude, subtle facial changes count as much as large pose or illumination changes. DIME uses identity labels only to form pretraining pairs, requires no dense annotations, and learns dense facial representations with emergent part-level semantics. We pretrain DIME on 20 million high-resolution facial images consolidated from existing face datasets. DIME's AU detection performance continues to improve as pretraining data scales from 6M to 20M images, with its BP4D F1 increasing by 5.9 points, while MAE gains only 0.5 points on the same subsets. DIME also outperforms specialized facial methods on AU detection, AU intensity estimation, cross-domain anti-spoofing, landmark localization, head pose estimation, face parsing, and face generation.

**Comment:** Criterion 2: DIME introduces differential masked autoencoding with Explicit Differential Direction Supervision for facial representation learning, improving BP4D AU-detection F1 by 5.9 points when scaling pretraining from 6M to 20M images, versus 0.5 points for MAE.

**Relevance:** 9
Back to [[topic](#topic-2)] [[top](#topics)]

<a id="2137-data-density-scaling-laws-for-image-self-distillation"></a>

### 2137\. [Data Density Scaling Laws for Image Self-Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19013)
**ArXiv:** 2610.19013 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19013)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.19013.pdf)]

**Authors:** Alvard Barseghyan, Ani Vanyan, Hakob Tamazyan, Hrant Khachatrian

**Abstract:** Current scaling law formulations suggest that increasing the number of unique samples in a dataset improves model performance, while repeated exposure to the same samples provides diminishing value. In this work, we examine this assumption for image self-distillation models. Through systematic experiments across diverse datasets, namely Maxar, SATLAS, Waymo, nuPlan, Ego4D, ImageNet, and YFCC15M, we demonstrate that the effects of sample repetition on downstream performance are non-trivial and dataset-dependent. We identify settings where repeatedly training on a smaller random subset can outperform training on a larger set of unique samples. This finding highlights a limitation of existing scaling laws. To address this gap, we propose a modified version of a Chinchilla-style scaling law that explicitly accounts for two factors: the number of unique samples and repetition per sample. This formulation provides a framework for understanding data-compute trade-offs and improves prediction in some regimes where repetition plays a central role, although its benefits are not consistent across datasets. Finally, we examine how subset selection and augmentation affect downstream performance.

**Comment:** Criterion 2: This paper systematically analyzes image self-distillation across ImageNet, Ego4D, and other datasets, showing that repeated smaller subsets can improve downstream performance and proposing a scaling law that explicitly separates unique-sample count from repetition.

**Relevance:** 9
Back to [[topic](#topic-2)] [[top](#topics)]

<a id="2140-learning-visual-speech-representations-via-cross-modal-distillation-and-joint-face-lip-modeling"></a>

### 2140\. [Learning Visual Speech Representations via Cross-Modal Distillation and Joint Face-Lip Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19559)
**ArXiv:** 2610.19559 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19559)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.19559.pdf)]

**Authors:** Jing-Xuan Zhang, Genshun Wan, Jianqing Gao, ..., Yumei Zhang, Lichen Zhang

**Abstract:** Lipreading remains challenging due to the inherent ambiguity of visual speech cues and their sensitivity to head pose and video resolution. In this work, we propose FLARE, a framework for robust visual speech representation learning via cross-modal distillation and joint face-lip modeling. FLARE leverages semantically rich Whisper representations as the acoustic teacher signal, converting them into discrete distillation targets through a random-projection quantizer and pretraining the model with a masked prediction objective. To capture complementary visual information, FLARE employs a dual-stream architecture that jointly processes lip region-of-interest (ROI) and full-face inputs, where the lip stream captures finegrained articulatory motion and the face stream encodes broader facial dynamics. During pretraining, auxiliary audio input is incorporated to facilitate cross-modal alignment, and modality dropout is applied to encourage robust visual-only representations. For downstream lipreading, FLARE serves as a visual encoder coupled with either a lightweight Transformer decoder or an LLM-based decoder. Evaluated on LRS2, LRS3, and the out-of-domain WildVSR benchmark, FLARE achieves strong lipreading performance, with word error rates of 11.7%, 13.2%, and 34.3%, respectively. A progressive design study confirms the contribution of each major component. Further analyses demonstrate that joint face-lip modeling improves robustness under degraded video resolution and large head pose variation, while yielding more phoneme-discriminative visual representations. Code and pretrained models are available at https://github.com/jxzhanggg/FLARE.

**Comment:** Criteria 2 and 4: FLARE distills Whisper audio representations into a masked-prediction visual encoder with joint face–lip modeling, learning temporally fine-grained speech representations evaluated on LRS2, LRS3, and WildVSR.

**Relevance:** 9
Back to [[topic](#topic-2)] [[top](#topics)]

<a id="2149-why-dit-models-underperform-as-representation-learners-without-long-skip-connections"></a>

### 2149\. [Why DiT Models Underperform as Representation Learners without Long Skip Connections](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13344)
**ArXiv:** 2610.13344 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13344)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.13344.pdf)]

**Authors:** Benyuan Meng, Yiliang Zhang, Jin-Wen Wu, ..., Qingming Huang, Longtao Huang

**Abstract:** Diffusion feature, i.e., intermediate activations extracted from diffusion model backbones, has emerged as a promising approach for unifying generation and vision understanding (discrimination). Despite the advancement of diffusion backbones into Diffusion Transformers (DiT), it has been found in previous literature that DiT models tend to yield weaker features despite their stronger generation capability. This poses a threat to good generation-understanding unification. In this work, we show via controlled from-scratch training that long skip connections (LSCs) affect feature quality: a DiT with LSCs can produce better features. Furthermore, through detailed diagnosis, we reveal the mechanism causing this effect. Specifically, the input to a diffusion backbone contains both the latent image and the diffusion noise, where the diffusion noise, though appearing in the form of noises, actually reflects the information of the diffusion trajectory. To make the final prediction of a noisy variable ($v$ or $ε$), the diffusion backbone needs both signals. However, carrying both signals through the main backbone effectively "pollutes" the image signal with noises, leading to poor feature quality. In comparison, LSCs can shortcut diffusion noises directly to where they are needed, leaving much weaker noise signals in the main backbone, thus improving feature quality. To name the broader impact, this reveals that LSCs are not a mere trick but closely related to the diffusion objective. The code is at https://github.com/Darkbblue/dit-lsc.

**Comment:** Criterion 2: Controlled from-scratch experiments analyze diffusion-based representation learning, showing that long skip connections improve feature quality by routing diffusion-noise signals around the main backbone.

**Relevance:** 8
Back to [[topic](#topic-2)] [[top](#topics)]

<a id="2152-ev-jepa-elastic-v-jepa-for-any-device-and-cross-device-inference"></a>

### 2152\. [EV-JEPA: Elastic V-JEPA for Any Device and Cross-Device Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14324)
**ArXiv:** 2610.14324 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14324)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.14324.pdf)]

**Authors:** Anxhelo Diko, Jieyu Lin, Stefano Ambrogio, ..., Barbara De Salvo, Chiao Liu

**Abstract:** Foundation models like V-JEPA are increasingly deployed across a heterogeneous edge-cloud continuum spanning smart glasses, phones, robots, and servers. Yet, they are typically trained as rigid, fixed-capacity networks bound to one device, supporting neither reconfigurable compute budgets nor collaborative execution. Existing approaches address only one axis at a time: slimmable and NAS-based methods provide width elasticity but assume single-device inference, whereas split-computing methods enable cross-device collaboration only for fixed partitions and static capacities. We introduce Elastic V-JEPA (EV-JEPA), a single model trained once and deployable everywhere along the continuum. Its width is reconfigurable, and it is natively partitionable across heterogeneous devices. At its core is Transition-Aware Triplet Training (T3). At each step we pick a narrow and a wide width, and train three paths: each width alone, plus a hybrid path that runs the early layers narrow and the rest wide. The hybrid switch point is where one device hands off to another, so the same weights learn both single-device and cross-device execution. Across various benchmarks, EV-JEPA falls within 1 accuracy point of independently trained baselines while improving over previous elastic training approaches by up to 11 accuracy points. Narrow-to-wide cross-device hybrid execution recovers to within 1.1 points of the highest-capacity standalone model and supports many-to-one, any-to-any, and variable-split configurations. Finally, we show that the elastic principle extends beyond the backbone to an elastic token compression mechanism that exposes controllable trade-offs among on-device computation, latency, and accuracy.

**Comment:** Criterion 2: EV-JEPA introduces Transition-Aware Triplet Training for V-JEPA, jointly training narrow, wide, and hybrid execution paths while improving benchmark accuracy over previous elastic training approaches by up to 11 percentage points.

**Relevance:** 8
Back to [[topic](#topic-2)] [[top](#topics)]

<a id="2156-lehami-symplectic-latent-dynamics-for-world-models"></a>

### 2156\. [LeHami: Symplectic Latent Dynamics for World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15267)
**ArXiv:** 2610.15267 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15267)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.15267.pdf)]

**Authors:** Romina Yalovetzky, Randall Balestriero, Yann LeCun

**Abstract:** Joint-embedding predictive architectures (JEPAs) learn world models directly in latent space, but unconstrained autoregressive predictors may contract the latent distribution over long rollouts. We introduce Symplectic Hamiltonian JEPA (LeHami), which replaces the standard predictor with an action-conditioned Hamiltonian flow over a structured latent state (q, p) integrated using an exactly symplectic leapfrog step. This construction preserves phase-space volume at every rollout step and rules out uniform contraction, asymptotically stable fixed points, and merging of distinct latent states, independently of the learned parameters or action sequence. We obtain the momentum-like component from temporal derivatives of the learned position-like representation, providing an explicit second-order state for prediction and control. On conservative and actuated visual pendulum environments, LeHami substantially improves long-horizon velocity prediction over an unconstrained JEPA world model and empirically maintains constant latent volume where the baseline contracts. The learned flow also supports accurate integration at unseen sub-frame step sizes and numerical time reversal. These results show how geometric structure can provide inference-time guarantees that are complementary to representation regularization in latent world models.

**Comment:** Criterion 2: LeHami modifies JEPA self-supervised learning with an action-conditioned Hamiltonian predictor and exactly symplectic leapfrog integration, preserving latent volume and improving long-horizon velocity prediction over an unconstrained JEPA on visual pendulum environments.

**Relevance:** 8
Back to [[topic](#topic-2)] [[top](#topics)]

<a id="2157-approximation-in-contrastive-representation-learning"></a>

### 2157\. [Approximation in Contrastive Representation Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15388)
**ArXiv:** 2610.15388 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15388)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.15388.pdf)]

**Authors:** Yuanfan Li, Zihan Zhang, Yiming Ying, Ding-Xuan Zhou

**Abstract:** Contrastive representation learning (CRL) is a fundamental paradigm for learning transferable representations. Recent work identifies its population target as the pointwise mutual information (PMI) $\log\frac{p(x,y)}{p_X(x)p_Y(y)}$ between two modalities $X,Y$, where $p$ is the joint density function and $p_X,p_Y$ are the marginals. However, it remains unclear why standard inner-product scores $f(x)^\top g(y)$ can effectively approximate a generally nonseparable population target PMI. In this paper, we address this question from the aspect of approximation for CRL. By linking PMI to a compact operator, we show that $D$-dimensional inner-product models perform rank-$D$ spectral approximation, with error controlled by the spectral tail. We then study neural network realizations: in general settings, we show that deep neural network scoring functions are universally consistent, and the approximation error rates depend on the smoothness of the PMI function; under structured low-rank data models we obtain improved rates attained by moderate-width encoders. Finally, we establish a fast calibration rate from contrastive excess risk to downstream retrieval error under a density-ratio Tsybakov noise condition.

**Comment:** Criterion 2: This paper rigorously analyzes contrastive representation learning as rank-D spectral approximation of pointwise mutual information, deriving encoder approximation guarantees and calibration bounds linking contrastive excess risk to downstream retrieval error.

**Relevance:** 8
Back to [[topic](#topic-2)] [[top](#topics)]

<a id="2169-lifuse-frequency-spatial-unified-self-supervised-pretraining-for-liver-disease-analysis-on-non-contrast-ct"></a>

### 2169\. [LiFUSE: Frequency-Spatial Unified Self-Supervised Pretraining for Liver Disease Analysis on Non-contrast CT](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18243)
**ArXiv:** 2610.18243 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18243)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.18243.pdf)]

**Authors:** Hao Zhang, Yi Huang, Wei Liu, ..., Ling Zhang, Ke Yan

**Abstract:** Given the growing global burden of liver tumors and diffuse liver diseases, non-contrast computed tomography (NCCT) is increasingly used for opportunistic screening because of its low cost and favorable safety profile. However, downstream tasks such as tumor detection and fine-grained disease diagnosis remain challenging due to NCCT's inherently low tissue contrast, lack of hemodynamic enhancement, and complex anatomical background. These limitations make subtle, heterogeneous, and multifocal hepatic lesions difficult to identify. Although large-scale self-supervised pretraining can learn robust representations from unlabeled NCCT scans, conventional voxel-wise reconstruction does not fully exploit the complementary information available in NCCT. Under weak NCCT contrast, low-level intensity patterns become less informative for pathology recognition, limiting the capture of lesion-specific discriminative features. In this paper, we propose the Liver Frequency-Spatial Unified Self-Supervised Pretraining (LiFUSE). The frequency branch applies random band-stop masking and spectrum reconstruction to encourage the model to capture subtle differences between lesions and surrounding tissues, while the spatial branch performs hybrid-granularity masked volumetric reconstruction to accommodate lesions of diverse sizes and morphologies. Finally, a cross-domain consistency scheme couples the spatial and frequency branches, yielding more discriminative representations for hepatic structures and lesions. Extensive experiments on three clinically relevant tasks across internal cohorts and a public target-domain transfer setting show that LiFUSE consistently outperforms strong baselines. These results suggest that unified frequency-spatial learning can improve liver disease analysis on NCCT, highlighting its potential for large-scale screening.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 2 by proposing frequency–spatial self-supervised pretraining that combines random band-stop spectrum reconstruction, hybrid-granularity masked volumetric reconstruction, and cross-domain consistency, outperforming strong baselines on three NCCT analysis tasks.

**Relevance:** 8
Back to [[topic](#topic-2)] [[top](#topics)]

<a id="2178-enhancing-latent-world-models-with-associative-memory"></a>

### 2178\. [Enhancing Latent World Models with Associative Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19683)
**ArXiv:** 2610.19683 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19683)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.19683.pdf)]

**Authors:** Yuwei Sun, Siyu Zhu

**Abstract:** World models trained via latent dynamics offer a scalable path for embodied planning, yet existing methods suffer from representational collapse, computational overhead, and limited long-horizon capability. We introduce a memory-enhanced latent world model that augments JEPA-style prediction with a content-addressable memory bank. Our approach learns high-level representations directly from observations, operating at multiple temporal scales within a shared latent space, and conditions predictions via adaptive layer normalization. To prevent collapse and ensure effective memory usage, we design two complementary losses that encourage slot diversity and balanced temporal utilization. Experiments on PushT, Tworoom, Reacher, and OGBCube demonstrate that our method achieves state-of-the-art performance with a lightweight architecture, outperforming conventional latent world models on long-horizon planning tasks.

**Comment:** Criterion 2: improves JEPA-style self-supervised representation learning with content-addressable memory and slot-diversity and temporal-utilization losses to prevent collapse, reporting state-of-the-art long-horizon planning results on PushT, Tworoom, Reacher, and OGBCube.

**Relevance:** 8
Back to [[topic](#topic-2)] [[top](#topics)]

<a id="4148-unimergetrack-hierarchical-model-merging-for-general-rgb-x-visual-object-tracking"></a>

### 4148\. [UniMergeTrack: Hierarchical Model Merging for General RGB-X Visual Object Tracking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13053)
**ArXiv:** 2610.13053 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13053)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.13053.pdf)]

**Authors:** Shiao Wang, Xiao Wang, Yuhe Ding, ..., Yonghong Tian, Jin Tang

**Abstract:** RGB-X tracking leverages complementary modalities, such as thermal infrared, event, or depth, to improve robustness under challenging conditions. Existing general-purpose RGB-X trackers typically follow two paradigms: independent task-specific training and joint training across modality combinations. However, the former suffers from knowledge isolation and model redundancy, while the latter introduces optimization interference across heterogeneous tasks. In this work, we present UniMergeTrack, a new model merging paradigm that preserves task-specific optimization while enabling effective cross-task knowledge integration. UniMergeTrack first trains modality-specific trackers independently and then performs hierarchical model merging. Specifically, transferable backbones are merged across RGB-X tasks via high-pass-filtered merging, and complemented by lightweight task-specific experts that preserve modality-specific cues. Tracking heads are merged within each task using optimal transport-guided channel alignment to mitigate hidden channel mismatch. A subsequent distribution alignment fine-tuning stage further improves compatibility between the unified backbone and task-specific heads. Extensive experiments on the LasHeR, RGBT234, VisEvent, VOT-RGBD22, COESOT, and CDTB benchmarks demonstrate that UniMergeTrack achieves state-of-the-art or competitive performance across RGB-Thermal, RGB-Event, and RGB-Depth tracking tasks, while exhibiting strong generalization to unseen datasets. The source code will be released upon acceptance. The source code of this paper will be released on https://github.com/Event-AHU/OpenEvTracking.

**Comment:** Criterion 4: UniMergeTrack transfers knowledge across RGB-thermal, RGB-event, and RGB-depth video tracking through high-pass-filtered backbone merging, achieving state-of-the-art or competitive performance on benchmarks including LasHeR, VisEvent, and VOT-RGBD22.

**Relevance:** 8
Back to [[topic](#topic-4)] [[top](#topics)]

<a id="4159-slotavs-object-centric-audio-visual-segmentation-via-bi-modal-slot-attention"></a>

### 4159\. [SlotAVS: Object-Centric Audio-Visual Segmentation via Bi-Modal Slot Attention](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15764)
**ArXiv:** 2610.15764 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15764)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.15764.pdf)]

**Authors:** Arnav Sharma, Anand Mishra, Renu Rameshan

**Abstract:** Audio-Visual Segmentation (AVS) requires predicting pixel-wise masks of sounding objects by grounding acoustic signals in visual space. In multi-source scenes, concurrent sound sources often remain entangled within shared visual representations, leading to merged masks or unstable specialization when using standard alignment-focused transformer architectures. To address this, we present SlotAVS, an object-centric framework driven by competitive bi-modal slot attention. While robust foundation models provide rich semantic priors, SlotAVS shifts the paradigm from global cross-modal fusion to explicit competitive binding. By initializing discrete object slots from temporal audio sequences and enforcing strict slot-wise competition over spatial visual tokens, our method structurally mitigates the blending of distinct acoustic entities. Further, to stabilize instance-level learning, we introduce a Hungarian matching strategy that optimally aligns slots with ground-truth objects using spatial and boundary overlap. Coupled with a numerically stable, confidence-aware mask fusion mechanism, SlotAVS robustly aggregates multi-source predictions. Extensive experiments on the AVSBench dataset demonstrate the efficacy of our approach, achieving 88.6% mIoU on the Single-Source (S4) split and 71.7% mIoU on the Multi-Source (MS3) split. These results show that explicit competitive binding is critical for complex audio-visual scene understanding and effectively leveraging foundation-model priors without relying solely on their capacity.

**Comment:** Criterion 4: SlotAVS uses temporal audio to initialize object slots and competitive bi-modal attention to produce pixel-wise sounding-object masks, achieving 71.7% mIoU on AVSBench’s multi-source split.

**Relevance:** 8
Back to [[topic](#topic-4)] [[top](#topics)]

<a id="4182-what-makes-a-good-visual-representation-for-spatial-understanding-in-mllms"></a>

### 4182\. [What Makes a Good Visual Representation for Spatial Understanding in MLLMs?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17844)
**ArXiv:** 2610.17844 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17844)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.17844.pdf)]

**Authors:** Xiaohu Huang, Yumin Guo, Kai Han

**Abstract:** Spatial understanding is a necessary step in moving multimodal large language models (MLLMs) toward reasoning about the 3D physical world. Their visual pretraining data, however, is almost entirely 2D, so the visual tokens processed by MLLMs often lack inherent 3D structural information. A growing number of methods inject such structure by transferring representations from external vision or geometry models, and collectively establish that such transfer helps. Behind these methods lies a largely underexplored question: what makes a visual representation good for spatial understanding? To answer it, we first separate what is transferred from how it is transferred, and then trace the resulting differences back to properties of the representations themselves. What we transfer comes from 3D geometry models, self-supervised image and video models, vision-language models, and generative models, in the form of latent features or explicit depth and point maps. We transfer it either by distillation, using it as a supervision target, or by fusion, feeding it as an additional input. We find that a representation's 3D structure correlates strongly with how much it helps, measured by cross-view consistency for latent features and by depth and reconstruction accuracy for explicit geometry. We further show that the form of a representation dictates the mechanism it needs. Latent features gain as much from fusion as from distillation; explicit geometry gains little from fusion, and most of its distillation gain depends on a geometry decoding head. Guided by these findings, we distill both latent features and explicit geometry into a single MLLM. Taking only RGB video as input, it consistently improves over its base model on both static 3D and dynamic 4D spatial reasoning benchmarks.

**Comment:** Criterion 4: jointly distills latent visual features and explicit 3D geometry into an RGB-video MLLM, transferring geometric representations to improve performance on dynamic 4D spatial reasoning benchmarks.

**Relevance:** 7
Back to [[topic](#topic-4)] [[top](#topics)]
