<a id="7009-endorsi-physical-evidence-driven-recursive-improvement-of-endoscopic-robot-policies"></a>

### 7009\. [EndoRSI: Physical-Evidence-Driven Recursive Improvement of Endoscopic Robot Policies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13376)
**ArXiv:** 2610.13376 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13376)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.13376.pdf)]

**Authors:** Chi Kit Ng, Yidong Zhang, Tianchun Wu, ..., Huxin Gao, Hongliang Ren

**Abstract:** Artificial intelligence is beginning to automate scientific discovery, yet physical robot-policy research still relies on scientists and engineers to interpret experimental failures and decide how controllers should be revised. We present EndoRSI (Endoscopic Recursive Self-Improvement), a human-governed, self-improving robotic research system in which the embedded EndoForge coding agent iteratively develops and refines endoscopic robot policies using evidence from physical experiments. Physical execution remains isolated within a safety-constrained runtime under human authorisation. In motorised endoscopy, validation evidence first caused the agent to reject an initial action representation, while subsequent physical experiments falsified an offline-supported insertion strategy by revealing hazardous closed-loop behaviour absent from the source data. Failed physical rollouts were then incorporated into policy revision; insertion success subsequently increased from 2/21 in the first generation to 17/21 in the fourth. The frozen final policy retained competence across visual and hardware shifts and was the best performing autonomous policy across six independent ex-vivo porcine specimens. These results show that physical robot interaction can serve as experimental evidence for agent-driven robot policy research.

**Comment:** Criterion 7: EndoRSI uses the EndoForge coding agent to iteratively revise robot policies using physical experimental evidence under human authorization, improving insertion success from 2/21 to 17/21.

**Relevance:** 10
Back to [[topic](#topic-7)] [[top](#topics)]

<a id="7017-autobio-benchmarking-dexterous-manipulation-with-dynamic-experimental-phenomena-in-realistic-laboratories"></a>

### 7017\. [AutoBio+: Benchmarking Dexterous Manipulation with Dynamic Experimental Phenomena in Realistic Laboratories](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13643)
**ArXiv:** 2610.13643 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13643)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.13643.pdf)]

**Authors:** Xuanye Wu, Yue Shi, Yongcan Yang, ..., Yao Mu, Guangtao Zhai

**Abstract:** Automating biological laboratories requires robots to perform dexterous tool interactions under realistic visual conditions while handling dynamic experimental phenomena, presenting significant challenges for robotic manipulation. To enable systematic evaluation under these conditions, we introduce AutoBio+, a simulation benchmark and framework for dexterous manipulation in realistic biological laboratory environments. AutoBio+ comprises 14 tasks across 4 robot embodiments, spanning functional tool use, precision manipulation, language-conditioned visual grounding, and instrument operation. We introduce controllable dynamic experimental phenomena, including flames, vapor, and bubbles, to capture the evolving visual conditions of laboratory procedures. To further enhance environmental realism, we develop a hybrid rendering pipeline that integrates interactive simulated foreground objects with Gaussian-splatting backgrounds reconstructed from 6 real laboratory scenes. For efficient large-scale data generation and evaluation, we further build a parallel simulation framework on top of mjlab, proposing Parallel Finite-State Machine (PFSM) and integrating batched rendering with decoupled trajectory replay for demonstration generation and model-independent closed-loop policy evaluation. Our evaluation reveals substantial variation in policy performance across tasks, exposing distinct strengths and weaknesses of current manipulation policies. Real-world experiments further demonstrate a reduced sim-to-real gap, supporting the validity of AutoBio+ for realistic manipulation evaluation. Supplementary materials are available at https://autobio-plus.github.io/AutoBioPlus.

**Comment:** Criterion 7: AutoBio+ introduces 14 dexterous-manipulation tasks across four robot embodiments, parallel demonstration generation, and closed-loop policy evaluation, with real-world experiments demonstrating a reduced sim-to-real gap.

**Relevance:** 10
Back to [[topic](#topic-7)] [[top](#topics)]

<a id="7033-enactopt-diagnosis-guided-harness-evolution-for-embodied-agents"></a>

### 7033\. [EnactOpt: Diagnosis-Guided Harness Evolution for Embodied Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14977)
**ArXiv:** 2610.14977 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14977)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.14977.pdf)]

**Authors:** ZhiYuan Feng, Zhuoshi Pan, Zhaolu Kang, ..., Heng Wang, Han Li

**Abstract:** Embodied agents built on foundation models rely on an execution harness to organize observations, guide actions, and incorporate execution feedback into subsequent decisions. Failures can arise not only from limited model capabilities, but also from how the harness presents evidence and guides its use. These model-external mechanisms provide an adaptation target without updating model parameters, but task-specific interaction experience can be costly to collect. Task-level outcomes alone do not reveal whether relevant evidence was missing from the model input or was available but inadequately used. Moreover, these two aspects are coupled: effective action guidance may require evidence that the current context omits, while exposing that evidence alone does not ensure an appropriate response. We introduce EnactOpt, which uses limited execution feedback to jointly evolve reusable textual skills and context-construction code while keeping the underlying models and runtime fixed. It diagnoses adjacent decisions by relating action feedback to the actual model input and subsequent action. Structured episode summaries guide joint revisions through prompt-level edit operators, and candidate harnesses undergo compatibility checks and validation-based retention. Across VLABench, ALFWorld, and Embodied Agent Interface (EAI), EnactOpt improves task success by approximately 10-27 percentage points over the initial harnesses and outperforms the evaluated GEPA, TextGrad, and Trace2Skill baselines. These gains extend to task conditions not used for harness evolution or candidate selection. The results support a complementary route to embodied adaptation: turning limited execution experience into reusable behavioral improvements by jointly refining how evidence is presented and acted upon.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 7: EnactOpt uses execution feedback to jointly evolve reusable textual skills and context-construction code for embodied agents, improving task success by approximately 10–27 percentage points across VLABench, ALFWorld, and EAI.

**Relevance:** 10
Back to [[topic](#topic-7)] [[top](#topics)]

<a id="7040-exploring-the-limits-of-combinatorial-generalization-in-embodied-manipulation"></a>

### 7040\. [Exploring the Limits of Combinatorial Generalization in Embodied Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15425)
**ArXiv:** 2610.15425 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15425)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.15425.pdf)]

**Authors:** Xupeng Zhang, Yantai Yang, Chang Guo, Zhaokai Yin, Zhipeng Zhang

**Abstract:** In embodied manipulation, Vision Language Action (VLA) models can execute atomic skills, but long-horizon manipulation requires recombining these skills without demonstrations for every skill combination. The full instruction route treats skill chaining as long-horizon imitation. In a controlled study, full instruction training on limited skill combination sequences reaches 40.0% ordered success on covered combinations but only 7.7% on recombination, suggesting memorization of sequence templates rather than robust skill recombination. We introduce Hierarchical Subtask Execution (HSE), a diagnostic framework that exposes skill recombination through a shared modular interface over the planner, trigger, and policy, decomposing long-horizon failures into separable modes. We introduce RoboCombine, a benchmark for skill recombination without combined skill training trajectories, with metrics for ordered success and transition conversion across subtask boundaries. RoboCombine reveals that atomic competence alone does not ensure ordered execution. Failures concentrate at transitions where downstream subtasks launch from handoff states from preceding subtasks rather than canonical atomic starts. We identify this mismatch as the start state gap. To address it, we introduce Pose Robust Task Initialization (PRTI), a lightweight atomic task post-training strategy that broadens start state coverage beyond canonical starts. Under oracle timed HSE on RoboCombine, PRTI reaches 18.16% ordered success, compared with 4.21% under random perturbation and 9.47% under a robot-reset oracle, without combined skill training trajectories. These results show that combinatorial generalization requires handoff robust atomic skills that convert first stage progress into reliable downstream execution, not merely decomposition into known subtasks. Code: https://github.com/AutoLab-SAI-SJTU/RoboCombine.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 7 by introducing the HSE planner–trigger–policy framework and RoboCombine manipulation benchmark, with PRTI post-training improving ordered skill-recombination success to 18.16% versus 4.21% with random perturbation.

**Relevance:** 10
Back to [[topic](#topic-7)] [[top](#topics)]

<a id="7044-retrace-cross-stage-failure-attribution-for-self-improving-robotic-manipulation"></a>

### 7044\. [ReTrace: Cross-Stage Failure Attribution for Self-Improving Robotic Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15814)
**ArXiv:** 2610.15814 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15814)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.15814.pdf)]

**Authors:** Yue Yu, Bin Zhu, Junhui Li, ..., Shijie Zhou, Jingjing Chen

**Abstract:** General-purpose multimodal foundation models are increasingly capable of directly controlling robots, yet improving their manipulation capabilities through autonomous interaction remains challenging. A key challenge to such self-improvement is learning from failures in complex manipulation tasks involving cross-stage dependencies, where an action may succeed locally but cause a later stage to fail. To address this challenge, we introduce ReTrace, a self-improvement framework for robotic manipulation through autonomous physical interaction and retrospective reasoning, without provided demonstrations or model-parameter updates. ReTrace retrospectively traces downstream failures to earlier decisions, proposes targeted corrections, and evaluates them through subsequent interactions. Across attempts, ReTrace progressively consolidates validated corrections into a reusable manipulation skill that guide subsequent execution. We evaluate ReTrace on four real-world manipulation tasks using a Franka Research 3 robot. On the two tasks with strong cross-stage dependencies, GPT-6 Astra-low improves from 0% to 80% success on plug insertion and from 0% to 100% on ball pouring, while GPT-6 Sol-xhigh improves from 0% to 10% and 0% to 90%, respectively. The learned skills also transfer across model and reasoning configurations and reduce inference-time decision cost in most evaluated settings.

**Comment:** Criterion 7: ReTrace enables autonomous robot self-improvement through retrospective cross-stage failure attribution and reusable skill consolidation, improving GPT-6 Astra-low success from 0% to 80% on plug insertion and from 0% to 100% on ball pouring with a real Franka Research 3 robot.

**Relevance:** 10
Back to [[topic](#topic-7)] [[top](#topics)]

<a id="7051-embodied-skill-bench-benchmarking-how-well-agents-with-embodied-skills-perform-on-physical-tasks"></a>

### 7051\. [Embodied Skill Bench: Benchmarking How Well Agents with Embodied Skills Perform on Physical Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16238)
**ArXiv:** 2610.16238 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16238)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.16238.pdf)]

**Authors:** Zhi Han, Zihan Guo, Chenxi Zeng, ..., Weinan Zhang, Yang Li

**Abstract:** LLM agents increasingly control robots using VLA policies, perception models, and procedural knowledge. These modules are difficult to reuse because their interfaces, embodiment assumptions, and conditions of use differ, and their benefits on physical tasks remain unclear. We package them as embodied skills that extend agent skills with explicit embodiment assumptions and conditions of use, building 250 action, perception, and experience skills from 1,052 resources. On this basis we introduce EmbodiedSkillBench, which runs an agent in a closed observation-action loop and records success, actions, tokens, and wall-clock time. It supports three modes: native control, skill-only execution, and Agent-Directed Reuse (ADR), a mode we propose in which the agent keeps its native controls and decides for each skill whether to invoke, modify, or ignore it. Across four agents on 100 perturbed LIBERO-Pro tasks, we find that skills help an agent only as far as they exceed what it can already do. Skill-only execution lifts GPT-5.6-Sol from 11% to 63% and Kimi-K3 and Claude-Opus-5 by about 20 points, while cutting their token usage by 58% to 85%. Yet all four agents then end up between 61% and 64%, and GPT-6-Astra falls from 92% under native control to 61%. This suggests that as agents grow stronger, the role of embodied skills shifts from supplying missing capability to reducing cost: GPT-6-Astra already solves most tasks through native control, so restricting it to skills removes more than it adds. Under ADR, which lets it keep native control and call skills selectively, GPT-6-Astra recovers to 85%, within 7 points of native control, while using 14.1% fewer tokens and 11.9% less time. With Jev as a skill choosing motion directions and gripper actions, it reaches 81% with 50% fewer tokens and 57% less time. The code is available at https://github.com/HanZhi306/EmbodiedSkill-Bench.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 7 by introducing EmbodiedSkillBench, a closed-loop robotic-agent benchmark with 250 embodied skills and Agent-Directed Reuse, evaluated across four agents on 100 perturbed LIBERO-Pro tasks.

**Relevance:** 10
Back to [[topic](#topic-7)] [[top](#topics)]

<a id="7060-d-world-an-explicit-world-language-action-model-with-recursive-self-improvement-under-dual-control"></a>

### 7060\. [D-World: An Explicit World-Language-Action Model with Recursive Self-Improvement under Dual Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17114)
**ArXiv:** 2610.17114 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17114)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.17114.pdf)]

**Authors:** Xuan Wen, Baichao Wang

**Abstract:** Frontier large language models (LLMs) now reason about space well enough to program robots, yet the world models behind robot policies are implicit, held in network weights or predicted video, and cannot be inspected or corrected directly. We present D-World, an explicit world-language-action model built on the Sense, Plan and Act decomposition. A perception agent writes the scene as an executable digital twin using generated vision and geometry code. A planning agent searches for actions in simulation and, after each executed action chunk, revises the twin's object states and named physical parameters against what the robot measured. A parameter revision is accepted only if replaying the latest chunk does not increase any measured residual beyond its tolerance, and every chunk is checked in the updated twin before it runs. Structural mismatches return to perception for reconstruction. We call this measurement-grounded cycle of correction and revision recursive self-improvement (RSI), in which the LLM stays frozen while the world it acts in improves. We model the choice of actions that both advance the task and expose model error as a dual control problem and show that the cost of incorporating evidence into the twin sets the threshold at which probing pays, so cheap local revision counteracts the turn-off of cautious controllers. Each episode runs a real-to-sim-to-real loop, and its recorded twins can be refitted and re-rendered offline into higher-fidelity environments for post-training. Because perception and planning communicate only through the twin and language, either can be replaced by a learned model, and since inference dominates the loop, the loop speeds up with faster models. On a dual-arm robot, towel and toaster tasks show local revisions, such as one reducing the bread's rendered-depth residual from 22.4 to 1.5 mm, that let execution continue without reconstruction.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 7 with a perception–planning agent framework that revises an executable digital twin using robot feedback and checks actions in simulation, demonstrated on dual-arm towel and toaster tasks.

**Relevance:** 10
Back to [[topic](#topic-7)] [[top](#topics)]

<a id="7062-generative-flow-on-symbolic-abstractions-for-embodiment-capability-discovery"></a>

### 7062\. [Generative Flow on Symbolic Abstractions for Embodiment Capability Discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17174)
**ArXiv:** 2610.17174 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17174)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.17174.pdf)]

**Authors:** Youwei Yu, Lantao Liu

**Abstract:** Achieving complex, long-horizon robot behavior remains challenging because successful execution depends on skills grounded in a robot's physical affordances. Existing training-task distributions are often handcrafted or sampled from large language models (LLMs), relying on semantic plausibility rather than embodiment-specific feasibility. This mismatch can produce curricula that are too difficult, redundant, or blind to non-obvious but learnable behaviors. We propose Generative Flow on Symbolic Abstractions (FOSA), a framework for discovering embodiment-specific capabilities by learning a grounded task generator. FOSA parameterizes an abstract policy as a Generative Flow Network that constructs symbolic task skeletons. Each skeleton is compiled into a simulation environment, where low-level policy-learning performance provides the reward signal for the abstract policy. Across heterogeneous robot platforms, FOSA discovers diverse and executable tasks near the robot's empirical capability frontier. After distilling the discovered task policies into a single language-conditioned policy, FOSA improves performance on unseen complex and long-horizon tasks. The code and demonstration videos are available at https://www.youwei-yu.com/fosa.

**Comment:** Criterion 7: FOSA learns a GFlowNet task generator that compiles symbolic tasks into simulation environments and uses policy-learning performance as feedback, improving generalization to unseen complex and long-horizon robotic tasks.

**Relevance:** 10
Back to [[topic](#topic-7)] [[top](#topics)]

<a id="7064-yubi-real2sim-3dgs-scene-verification-for-reliable-policy-evaluation"></a>

### 7064\. [YUBI-Real2Sim: 3DGS Scene Verification for Reliable Policy Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17521)
**ArXiv:** 2610.17521 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17521)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.17521.pdf)]

**Authors:** Hiroki Nishizawa, Takehiko Ohkawa, Nicola Scianca, ..., Shigeo Morishima, Kei Ota

**Abstract:** Evaluating VLA policies on real robots is costly, time-consuming, and difficult to reproduce, making real-to-sim simulation essential for scalable evaluation. While 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) enables rapid real-to-sim scene generation, the reconstruction quality required for reliable policy evaluation remains unquantified. In particular, policies relying on wrist-mounted fisheye cameras (e.g., YUBI handheld gripper data) are heavily exposed to visual artifacts (holes, floaters) in wide-angle views, directly degrading task performance. To address this, we present YUBI-Real2Sim, a unified framework for 3DGS-based scene reconstruction and real-to-sim policy evaluation under dual fisheye wrist views. We develop an integrated pipeline combining 200-degree fisheye camera capture, 3DGS training, 3D foundation models for object modeling, and MuJoCo-based physical simulation, reproducing the observed real-world policy ordering in eight of nine scene-task comparisons. Using this framework, we construct a benchmark across two bimanual robot platforms, three scenes, three tasks, and dual pi0.5-based policies. This systematically measures real-to-sim rank agreement and evaluates policy sensitivity against counterfactual 3DGS artifacts (ceiling holes, floaters, warping) and capture disturbances. We further analyze whether novel-view quality scores can indicate policy-ranking reliability under a scene-held-out evaluation protocol. The results show task-dependent performance and limitations of quality-based reliability prediction, highlighting the need to assess reconstruction quality when using Real2Sim for policy evaluation. These results do not yet establish a generally reliable scene-acceptance rule. Project Page: https://yubi-real2sim.airoa.io.

**Comment:** Criterion 7: YUBI-Real2Sim combines 3DGS reconstruction with MuJoCo for robotic policy evaluation across two bimanual platforms, reproducing real-world policy ordering in eight of nine scene-task comparisons.

**Relevance:** 10
Back to [[topic](#topic-7)] [[top](#topics)]

<a id="7067-towards-a-systematic-study-of-embodied-system-2-over-full-task-execution-cycles"></a>

### 7067\. [Towards a Systematic Study of Embodied System 2 over Full Task Execution Cycles](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17657)
**ArXiv:** 2610.17657 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17657)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.17657.pdf)]

**Authors:** Yuxi Wei, Ye Fang, Shuai Yang, ..., Tai Wang, Xiaojuan Qi

**Abstract:** An embodied brain, or System 2, must turn an abstract goal into actions while discovering relevant scene conditions and checking whether executed skills achieved their intended effects. Open-loop evaluations cannot test observations shaped by an agent's decisions, while some closed-loop evaluations provide limited evidence during execution or assume idealized skill outcomes. We introduce SCOPER, a suite for studying observation-grounded planning, execution, and recovery. SCOPER-bench combines high-level goals, ongoing visual observations, a shared rule-based System 1 for low-level actions, and controlled execution failures. Its construction and validation pipeline yields 870 tasks across BEHAVIOR and ProcTHOR, complemented by about 3,500 situated questions tied to the evaluated agent's execution. Benchmark results reveal difficulties in retaining scene evidence, monitoring progress, and recovering from unexpected outcomes. To address these difficulties, SCOPER-harness combines Evidence Memory and Progress Verification with a dynamic task agenda and bounded exploration, without additional model training. Across two evaluated open-source backbones and two simulators, the harness improves task success by 11.1-28.6%; a harnessed 9B model outperforms a directly executed 27B model on both simulators. In an offline next-action evaluation using observations from 200 real-robot demonstrations, the harness also improves full-call accuracy by 14.7% and 15.3%. SCOPER provides a setting for diagnosing and improving embodied reasoning throughout a complete task execution cycle.

**Comment:** Criterion 7: SCOPER introduces a closed-loop embodied task benchmark and an agent harness combining Evidence Memory, Progress Verification, and recovery, improving task success by 11.1–28.6% across BEHAVIOR and ProcTHOR.

**Relevance:** 10
Back to [[topic](#topic-7)] [[top](#topics)]

<a id="7068-agentic-robot-creation-through-generation-verification-and-revision"></a>

### 7068\. [Agentic Robot Creation through Generation, Verification, and Revision](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17881)
**ArXiv:** 2610.17881 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17881)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.17881.pdf)]

**Authors:** Xuebing Li, Yibo He, Sitong Liu, Ran Ji, Jiayuan Gu

**Abstract:** Can an AI agent turn high-level user intent into a functioning robot and teach it how to behave? We study this question from two complementary aspects: embodiment and behavior creation. For embodiment creation, we investigate how well current multimodal models capture mechanical requirements, and introduce computational metrics for evaluating generated designs before fabrication. For behavior creation, we present Roboforge, an agentic framework that turns task specifications into executable policies through motion generation, policy learning, and execution-grounded revision. Given a robot embodiment and task, the agent generates a reference motion, configures the learning objective, and trains a policy. Crucially, motion remains in the loop: rollout feedback can revise the reference motion, reward, or both. We evaluate a generated embodiment across multiple MLLMs and behavior creation across four diverse robots against reward-refinement and one-shot motion baselines. Our computational metrics expose recurring mechanical failures in generated designs, while behavior experiments show that execution-grounded refinement improves task-level behavior and execution quality. Finally, we fabricate an agent-generated Gorilla and directly deploy learned behaviors on the physical robot.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 7: Roboforge combines motion generation, policy learning, and rollout-feedback-driven revision of reference motions and rewards, improving task-level behavior across four robots and deploying learned behaviors on a fabricated Gorilla robot.

**Relevance:** 10
Back to [[topic](#topic-7)] [[top](#topics)]

<a id="7073-ladder-a-long-horizon-robot-manipulation-benchmark"></a>

### 7073\. [LADDER: A Long-Horizon Robot Manipulation Benchmark](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18053)
**ArXiv:** 2610.18053 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18053)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.18053.pdf)]

**Authors:** Petko Petkov, Giuliano Albanese, Nikolay Nikolov, ..., Jonathan Tremblay, Danda Pani Paudel

**Abstract:** General-purpose robot manipulation policies learn short-horizon skills effectively, but degrade rapidly on long-horizon tasks. Existing benchmarks cannot isolate failures because task horizon is either too short or entangled with scene and object variations. We propose LADDER, a long-horizon robot manipulation benchmark scaling from 1 to 10 subgoals within the same environment over three suites (SimplerEnv-Long, RobotArena-$\infty$-Long, and RoboLab-Long), 145 tasks, eight categories, two embodiments, and 2,024 simulated episodes per system. We evaluate two orchestration paradigms: text subgoals for learned low-level policies (VoLo) and tool selection orchestration (CaP-X, GPT-Policy, PSP-Agent, TiPToP). Given the full instruction directly, low-level policies achieve 85.4% on single-subgoal tasks, but drop to near zero on extended tasks. While orchestration remedies this, it remains bounded by physical execution errors of the low-level policy rather than high-level planning and achieves only ~25%. Tool selection orchestration with GPT-Policy on GPT-6 Astra significantly outperforms learned-policy methods, but incurs significant time and cost budget and succeeds only ~62%. Real-world experiments across two embodiments confirm the collapse of the low-level policy and the gain from orchestration. Project website: https://ladder.insait.ai

**Comment:** Criterion 7: LADDER evaluates planning and tool-use orchestration for long-horizon robot manipulation across 145 tasks with 1–10 subgoals, revealing execution bottlenecks and confirming orchestration gains through real-world experiments on two embodiments.

<a id="7077-let-the-mind-conceive-let-the-hand-achieve-following-multimodal-instructions-for-long-horizon-robotic-manipulation"></a>

### 7077\. [Let the Mind Conceive, Let the Hand Achieve: Following Multimodal Instructions for Long-Horizon Robotic Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18352)
**ArXiv:** 2610.18352 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18352)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.18352.pdf)]

**Authors:** Youpeng Wen, Gang Wang, Tianyu Huang, ..., Xieyuanli Chen, Yunhui Liu

**Abstract:** A robot following a multi-step instruction needs to act on the right objects in the right order, even as the scene changes. It must also maintain awareness of task progress and determine when to advance. To address these challenges, we introduce Orchestra, an agent harness that separates task planning and execution management from action generation. Orchestra decomposes a complete multimodal instruction into ordered subtasks, tracks completed steps, and updates the locations of relevant objects and destinations during execution. It maintains a shared context containing the active subtask, spatial grounding, and recent observations. An action policy uses this context to generate actions, while a separately trained progress evaluator estimates when to advance to the next subtask. To evaluate whether robots trained on atomic instructions and short trajectories can execute longer subtask sequences, we introduce a benchmark named RMBench-Compose. Orchestra achieves a mean success rate of $68.22\%$ across nine RMBench tasks, outperforming Mem-0 by $26.22$ percentage points. On RMBench-Compose, it achieves an Average Success Length (ASL) of $7.35$ subtasks, $2.46$ times that of HarnessVLA. Across four real-world multi-step tasks, it improves mean ASL by $25.0\%$ over Orchestra using the base $π_{0.5}$ policy. These results show that Orchestra helps robots carry out longer sequences of subtasks while preserving the requested subtask order and acting on the specified objects. Our code and project page are available at https://youpengwen.github.io/Orchestra.

**Comment:** Criterion 7: Orchestra combines multimodal task planning, shared execution context, and learned progress evaluation for long-horizon robotic manipulation, achieving 68.22% success on RMBench and a 7.35-subtask Average Success Length on the new RMBench-Compose benchmark.

<a id="7087-sear-self-evolving-agentic-robotics"></a>

### 7087\. [SEAR: Self-Evolving Agentic Robotics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18792)
**ArXiv:** 2610.18792 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18792)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.18792.pdf)]

**Authors:** Xinyi Wang, Bangzheng Li, Hanyu Wang, ..., Shyamal Buch, Dejia Xu

**Abstract:** Frontier language-model agents can now improve robots on their own: they write control code, test it, and keep what works as skills and memories for the next task. We introduce SEAR (Self-Evolving Agentic Robotics), a framework and benchmark built around two questions. Is the agent really learning, or just trying more often? And does what it learns still help on new tasks, in a new simulator, and on a real robot? The framework runs an agent in three modes: it writes code that becomes the policy (code-as-policy), acts live in the control loop (agent-as-policy), or works as a researcher that fine-tunes a vision-language-action model (auto-research). The benchmark adds controls that separate retained experience from repeated attempts, familiar tasks, privileged simulator state, larger budgets, and luck. Across seven frontier language models, 330 simulated and five physical tasks, and over four years of agent runtime, we highlight three findings. First, the learning curve of a robotic agent depends both on the overall time budget and the task difficulty relative to model capability. Harder tasks make longer sessions worthwhile; on easier tasks, re-rolling short attempts does just as well. Second, control code and shared routines transfer across tasks and simulators, while task-specific solutions do not. On a real robot, we observe a performance gap between agent-designed practice and novel human-designed scenes. Third, different models evolve more effectively in different ways. Some do best acting live, others writing code first. And a text-only model keeps pace with a comparable multimodal one: perception helps with error assessment, but agentic reasoning and tool use can matter more. We release the benchmark and codebase to support future research.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 7 through an agentic robotics framework combining code-as-policy, live agent control, autonomous VLA fine-tuning, and retained skills and memories, with controlled evaluations across 330 simulated and five physical tasks.

**Relevance:** 10

<a id="7088-embodiedevo-self-improving-coding-agents-for-physical-intelligence"></a>

### 7088\. [EmbodiedEvo: Self-improving Coding Agents for Physical Intelligence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18867)
**ArXiv:** 2610.18867 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18867)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.18867.pdf)]

**Authors:** Wenyi Li, Yizhe Chi, Youjie Zheng, ..., Kaisen Yang, Qinhuai Na

**Abstract:** Bringing general-purpose agents into the physical world calls for an executable representation of physical intelligence. Compared with vision-language-action (VLA) models, which map observations and language instructions directly to actions, code makes control logic explicit and reusable. Code can serve not only as a policy representation but also as the workspace in which the agent solves a task. Rather than following a prescribed workflow of planning, execution, and verification, the agent can autonomously inspect the state, revise and execute code, and use feedback to choose the next step. The success of this SWE-style loop in digital environments motivates its extension to physical tasks. Yet physical execution remains prone to recurring failures. To retain and refine experience for later scenes, we introduce EmbodiedEvo, where a coding agent evolves reusable Skills and Lessons through feedback on a single Playground scene, then freezes the resulting harness for diverse new scenes. On RoboDojo-Sim, EmbodiedEvo achieves an average score of 44.48 and a success rate of 37.82\%, surpassing all officially verified results. Paired tests show the largest gains on memory-dependent tasks and successful reuse of coordinated procedures, while fine contact control remains difficult. Experience from stronger models also improves weaker executors: Astra's experience raises Sol and Luna's success rates by 8.86 and 5.50 percentage points over no experience. Compared with raw interaction history, Skills and Lessons achieve comparable task performance at 53.0\% lower execution cost.

**Comment:** Criterion 7: EmbodiedEvo introduces a self-improving physical-task agent that evolves reusable Skills and Lessons through execution feedback, achieving 37.82% success on RoboDojo-Sim and demonstrating transfer of experience to weaker executors.

**Relevance:** 10
Back to [[topic](#topic-7)] [[top](#topics)]

<a id="7090-chunkact-action-chunking-for-agentic-robotics"></a>

### 7090\. [ChunkAct: Action Chunking for Agentic Robotics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18874)
**ArXiv:** 2610.18874 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18874)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.18874.pdf)]

**Authors:** Bingxin Xu, Wenrui Bao, Xinxin Liu, ..., Yuzhang Shang, Emilio Ferrara

**Abstract:** Agentic robotics places a large language model (LLM) agent in the robot control loop, where it composes perception, motion, and vision-language-action (VLA) tools under the guidance of a memory and thereby generalizes to new tasks without retraining. This generality comes at a steep price in efficiency. Every primitive requires a round of LLM reasoning over the full interaction history, so the cost of an episode grows with the number of primitives. We find this reasoning largely redundant: once a sub-goal is chosen, its primitives follow a routine already prescribed by memory. We present ChunkAct, which introduces action chunking to agentic robotics. Online, a single call launches a chunk of chained primitives, which executes autonomously and returns control only upon failure. Offline, ChunkAct compiles the memory into these chunks, so execution is memory-free at test time and the planner is reserved for decisions that require reasoning. LLM computation therefore scales with the number of decisions rather than the number of primitives. On LIBERO-Pro, ChunkAct improves task success by 5.0 points over the state of the art in agentic robotics while requiring 2.9x fewer LLM calls, 4.4x fewer input tokens, and 2.3x fewer output tokens.

**Comment:** Criterion 7: ChunkAct combines LLM planning, memory compilation, and autonomous action chunks for robotic manipulation, improving LIBERO-Pro success by 5.0 points while requiring 2.9× fewer LLM calls.

**Relevance:** 10
Back to [[topic](#topic-7)] [[top](#topics)]

<a id="7094-robota-embed-unified-vla-embeddings-for-robot-in-context-learning-and-evolution"></a>

### 7094\. [Robota-Embed: Unified VLA Embeddings for Robot In-Context Learning and Evolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19495)
**ArXiv:** 2610.19495 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19495)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.19495.pdf)]

**Authors:** Yunze Liu, Jing Wang, Shenrui Wu, ..., Song Han, Han Cai

**Abstract:** Retrieving useful context for a robot requires matching its task, execution stage, and the continuation that should follow. We introduce Robota-Embed, to our knowledge the first vision-language-action (VLA) embedding model to jointly encode all three modalities in both current-state queries and recorded future continuations. Predictive retrieval training aligns these endpoints, enabling historical continuations to serve as context at inference time. We construct a multi-source corpus of 1,695,684 cleaned training anchors and introduce Robota-Embed-Bench, with 4,000 queries and a shared 8,000-entry gallery. Robota-Embed achieves 47.33% clip-level and 81.77% episode-level R@1, exceeding action-free Gemini Embedding 2 by 22.09 and 33.47 percentage points. Controlled retrieval studies show that removing native action degrades retrieval more than removing vision. We study two complementary applications. Embed2ICL uses retrieved experience for training-free and training-based policy conditioning, increasing success from 47.0% to 56.3% in DROID-Sim and from 86.4% to 92.8% on the LIBERO difficult subset, while improving task progress on real robots. Embed2Evolve uses execution feedback to grow task-local memory and refine context use around a frozen GPT-6 Astra policy. Across five ManiSkill tasks, it achieves 68% success, compared with 24% for GPT-6 Astra Direct and 33% for a harness independently evolved with Omni-Embed-Nemotron-3B under the same protocol. Relative to the latter, it uses approximately 79.5% fewer policy tokens per test rollout, counting both successes and failures. These results show that a unified, action-aware retrieval space can support robot in-context learning and task-local context evolution.

**Comment:** Criterion 7: Embed2Evolve uses execution feedback to grow task-local memory around a frozen robot policy, achieving 68% success across five ManiSkill tasks versus 24% for the direct policy.

**Relevance:** 10

<a id="7097-cracking-mobile-manipulation-from-failure-diagnosis-to-agentic-robot-system"></a>

### 7097\. [Cracking Mobile Manipulation: From Failure Diagnosis to Agentic Robot System](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19600)
**ArXiv:** 2610.19600 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19600)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.19600.pdf)]

**Authors:** Zixuan Wang, Jia-Ning Li, Yinzhou Tang, ..., Xin Li, Yiwu Zhong

**Abstract:** Recent vision-language-action (VLA) models have shown strong performance on local robot manipulation, yet their success does not readily translate to long-horizon mobile manipulation. In our experiments, a pretrained subtask VLA achieves 80% success rate on atomic manipulation tasks but only 19% when these skills are composed into mobile manipulation. We argue that this gap stems from failures across the execution process rather than from local manipulation capability alone. To uncover these bottlenecks, we systematically diagnose failures in long-horizon mobile manipulation and identify three dominant regimes: task planning, navigation and base positioning, and local manipulation. Based on this diagnosis, we develop failure-guided agentic intervention that augments a fixed subtask VLA with demonstration-based planning guidance, adaptive visual memory, accessibility-aware base adjustment, and local end-effector adjustment. On RoboCasa365, these interventions progressively improve task success from 19% to 47%, with the finalized system reaching 49% on held-out episode seeds, compared with 18% for the baseline. The same design improves performance from 14% to 37% on BEHAVIOR-1K and from 5/20 to 13/20 on real-robot tasks. These results suggest that reliable mobile manipulation can be advanced by diagnosing and intervening on the current bottleneck, rather than uniformly scaling the underlying policy. Our code will be released at https://github.com/PKU-VaLuE-Lab/Cracking-Mobile-Manipulation.git.

**Comment:** Criterion 7: A failure-guided agentic manipulation system combines demonstration-based planning, adaptive visual memory, and execution adjustments around a fixed VLA, achieving 49% success versus 18% for the baseline on held-out RoboCasa365 episodes.

**Relevance:** 10
Back to [[topic](#topic-7)] [[top](#topics)]

<a id="7098-acro-actor-critic-rollout-orchestration"></a>

### 7098\. [ACRO: Actor-Critic Rollout Orchestration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19677)
**ArXiv:** 2610.19677 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19677)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.19677.pdf)]

**Authors:** Junhoo Lee, Seungyeon Kim, Baekseung Kim, ..., Jimyeong Kim, Nojun Kwak

**Abstract:** Executing a vision-language-action (VLA) policy involves deciding when to continue an attempt and when to intervene to improve its chances of success. We introduce ACRO, an Actor--Critic rollout orchestration layer that makes these decisions for a fixed VLA. ACRO seeks interventions that offer a more promising continuation, are physically feasible, and leave sufficient time to complete the task. A Critic learned from policy rollouts estimates success prospects to decide when to intervene and select a re-entry target along the executed trajectory. Trajectory Retraction shortcuts the visited path to return the robot to that target, where the same VLA resumes execution from a fresh observation. We evaluate ACRO with GR00T N1.7 and $π_{0.5}$ across SimplerEnv, RoboCasa, and real-robot manipulation. Under the same physical execution budget, including all return motion, ACRO improves success by 10.4, 7.3, and 21.7 percentage points, respectively. It also completes additional tasks as the execution budget grows. These results show that controlling when and where a policy resumes can turn its existing capabilities into more successful task executions. Project page: https://junhoo.me/acro

**Comment:** Criterion 7: ACRO introduces a learned critic and trajectory-retraction mechanism that orchestrate interventions and retries for fixed VLA policies, improving manipulation success by 10.4, 7.3, and 21.7 percentage points on SimplerEnv, RoboCasa, and real robots, respectively.

**Relevance:** 10

<a id="7106-make-05-learn-to-call-astra-for-reliable-robot-manipulation"></a>

### 7106\. [Make π0.5 Learn to Call Astra for Reliable Robot Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14117)
**ArXiv:** 2610.14117 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14117)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.14117.pdf)]

**Authors:** Jianli Sun, Chengxiang Li, Yonglin Tian

**Abstract:** GPT-6 Astra provides robust embodied reasoning, but frequent external queries increase inference time and token costs. We equip a frozen $π_{0.5}$ policy with a lightweight CALL head that learns to predict when assistance is necessary. CALL reuses features from the standard policy forward pass and operates locally without consulting external models. Astra is activated solely for critical recovery events and returns control to $π_{0.5}$ upon stabilization. Evaluated across 40 LIBERO tasks (200 episodes), our system achieves a 97.5% success rate, outperforming the 95.5% baseline of pure $π_{0.5}$. In the Object, Goal, and Spatial suites, Astra executes only 5.49% of environment steps, consuming approximately 5,027 tokens per test episode. This selective intervention preserves the fast ordinary execution of $π_{0.5}$ while seamlessly integrating Astra's reasoning on demand. Our project page is available at https://kykgeek.github.io/Pi05_CALL_Astra/.

**Comment:** Criterion 7: a lightweight CALL head selectively invokes Astra for recovery during robot manipulation, achieving 97.5% success across 40 LIBERO tasks versus 95.5% for the frozen policy alone.

**Relevance:** 9

<a id="7113-learning-to-see-through-action-predictive-curiosity-for-robots"></a>

### 7113\. [Learning to See through Action: Predictive Curiosity for Robots](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14302)
**ArXiv:** 2610.14302 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14302)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.14302.pdf)]

**Authors:** Stelios Zarifis, Panagiotis Filntisis, George Retsinas, Petros Maragos

**Abstract:** Humans and animals do not learn vision from a fixed set of data: they move, interact, and learn from the visual consequences of their actions. Motivated by this developmental view, we study whether robots can improve visual learning by actively collecting experience through curiosity-driven interaction. We introduce DySCOVER, a self-supervised framework for active robot visual learning. A robot observes RGB frames, learns a visual autoencoder that encodes and reconstructs them, and trains a recurrent world model to predict the next visual latent state conditioned on the current state and action. The agent's policy is trained only with intrinsic rewards: reconstruction error encourages visually novel observations, while the world model's error encourages transitions whose consequences are not yet predictable. This prediction error biases the policy toward more intricate trajectories that enrich the agent's set of experiences with observations beyond those encouraged by visual novelty alone. Our experiments conducted with robotic arms as the embodied agents show that adding predictive curiosity improves the vision model over using visual curiosity alone. Across variations in initial conditions, experience capacity, embodiment, and scene content, adding predictive curiosity consistently improved visual learning. These results show that predictive curiosity can improve active visual learning beyond reconstruction-based curiosity alone.

**Comment:** Criterion 7: DySCOVER enables robotic arms to collect experience autonomously using reconstruction error and action-conditioned world-model prediction error as intrinsic rewards, consistently improving visual learning across embodiments and scene variations.

**Relevance:** 9
Back to [[topic](#topic-7)] [[top](#topics)]

<a id="7117-stateskill-agent-an-effective-and-efficient-embodied-agent-via-state-conditioned-skill"></a>

### 7117\. [StateSkill-Agent: An Effective and Efficient Embodied Agent via State-conditioned Skill](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14601)
**ArXiv:** 2610.14601 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14601)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.14601.pdf)]

**Authors:** Changxu Wei, Jingyi Zhang, Zhiyu Zheng, ..., Zhi Hou, Jiaxing Huang

**Abstract:** In this work, we aim to develop an embodied MLLM agent that understands and solves embodied tasks by learning, abstracting, and reusing transferrable and generalizable behavioral patterns. To this end, we propose StateSkill, a new skill representation for embodied agents, which introduces the concept of "finite-state machines" into skill modeling for effective and efficient skill construction and learning. The core idea of StateSkill is to model embodied interaction experiences as a finite-state machine, where a skill is organized around a set of task-progress states, state transitions, and state-conditioned guidance, enabling embodied agents to dynamically adjust their skills as the task progresses while keeping skill contexts compact and stage-relevant. We further develop StateSkill-Evolve, an experience-driven evolution mechanism that iteratively refines StateSkill through retrospective analysis of new successful and failed state-guided trajectories. Upon this, we introduce StateGRPO, which integrates StateSkill evolution and model optimization via state-conditioned GRPO into a unified iterative learning loop, enabling state-conditioned skills and model capabilities to co-evolve and reinforce each other. Extensive experiments demonstrate the effectiveness of our proposed methods and trained model (i.e., StateSkill-Agent) across various embodied tasks.

**Comment:** Criterion 7: StateSkill-Agent combines finite-state-machine skill representations, experience-driven refinement from successful and failed trajectories, and state-conditioned GRPO in a unified learning loop evaluated across various embodied tasks.

**Relevance:** 9

<a id="7128-evaluating-long-horizon-robot-planning-under-uncertainty"></a>

### 7128\. [Evaluating Long-Horizon Robot Planning under Uncertainty](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17326)
**ArXiv:** 2610.17326 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17326)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.17326.pdf)]

**Authors:** Harsh Muriki, Gayathri Aravindan, Yuchen Zheng, Sonia Chernova

**Abstract:** Household robots must successfully navigate im-perfect circumstances, both in their internal models and the external world. We introduce a benchmark of seven long-horizon household tasks in Habitat that vary initial robot memory and environment configurations. We evaluate adaptations of ReAct, VLM-TAMP, and Tru-POMDP within a shared execution framework across 90 tasks-environment-memory combinations, totaling 1,350 episodes. Our evaluation measures task success, partial completion, and action efficiency, along with rule-based analysis of execution traces. ReAct achieves the highest overall task success (45.3%) and completion (71.7%), while having accurate initial memory does not consistently yield the highest performance for every planner. We identify recurring placement errors, precondition errors, and redundant actions through trace analysis. These findings highlight differences in how the evaluated planners use initial knowledge and respond to uncertainty.

**Comment:** Criterion 7: Introduces a Habitat benchmark for long-horizon household robot planning under memory and environmental uncertainty, evaluating ReAct, VLM-TAMP, and Tru-POMDP across 1,350 episodes with task-success metrics and execution-trace analysis.

**Relevance:** 9

<a id="7135-libero-rig-a-hardware-setup-robustness-benchmark-for-vision-language-action-models"></a>

### 7135\. [LIBERO-RIG: A Hardware-Setup Robustness Benchmark for Vision-Language-Action Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18843)
**ArXiv:** 2610.18843 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18843)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.18843.pdf)]

**Authors:** Andrea Yaoyun Cui, Hanyu Wang, Xinyi Wang, ..., Dejia Xu, Matthew Tancik

**Abstract:** Robot policies are expected to generalize across deployment environments, including variations in the robot itself: cameras are bumped and remounted, calibrations drift, controllers run at different rates, and end-effector pads wear down. We present LIBERO-RIG, a benchmark that perturbs only the robot's hardware and setup while holding every LIBERO task fixed. The benchmark spans eleven perturbation axes, including camera mounting and optics, camera-to-arm calibration, actuation gain, control rate, proprioceptive noise, base pose, table height, and gripper pads. Each axis has five severity levels, yielding 210 conditions across the four LIBERO suites. We evaluate nine recent vision-language-action models using 500 episodes per condition and substantiate each finding with rigorous statistical tests. Pooled success falls from 0.932 to 0.767 at the middle severity and 0.509 at the highest severity. Performance degradation varies substantially across perturbation axes and action interfaces, and training on visual perturbations does not mitigate faults that leave camera images unchanged. We release the benchmark together with manifests specifying the fixed perturbation parameters for each episode.

**Comment:** Criterion 7: LIBERO-RIG benchmarks nine vision-language-action robot policies across 210 hardware/setup perturbation conditions on LIBERO manipulation tasks, finding that pooled success falls from 0.932 to 0.509 at the highest severity.

**Relevance:** 9
Back to [[topic](#topic-7)] [[top](#topics)]

<a id="7139-active-generative-simulation-meta-learning-what-to-simulate-for-sim-and-real-policy-co-training"></a>

### 7139\. [Active Generative Simulation: Meta-Learning What to Simulate for Sim-and-Real Policy Co-Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19479)
**ArXiv:** 2610.19479 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19479)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.19479.pdf)]

**Authors:** Liqian Ma, Vaibhav Saxena, Utkarsh Aashu Mishra, ..., Yongxin Chen, Danfei Xu

**Abstract:** Sim-and-real co-training is an effective way to scale robot learning by augmenting limited real-world demonstrations with simulation data. However, existing approaches typically treat simulators as fixed data sources, relying on uniform generation, offline retrieval, or manual parameter alignment by domain experts. We formulate \textit{Active Generative Simulation}: optimizing a simulator's data-generation distribution to maximize downstream real-world performance. To solve this problem, we propose MetaGenSim, a meta-learning framework that trains an auxiliary rater network via meta-gradients to automatically discover high-utility data-generation distributions within a user-specified simulation parameter space, without manual parameter alignment. Across simulation and real-world manipulation tasks, MetaGenSim outperforms uniform generation and retrieval baselines, achieves a 150% relative improvement in real-world full-task success over uniform generation, and matches expert manual alignment. We further show that distributions curated with a lightweight proxy policy transfer to VLA fine-tuning, and that MetaGenSim can exploit partial alignment through atomic skill stitching and object-receptacle composition when exact sim-and-real task alignment is unavailable. Project website: https://meta-gensim.github.io

**Comment:** Criterion 7: MetaGenSim uses meta-gradients to optimize simulation data-generation distributions for robot manipulation learning, achieving a 150% relative improvement in real-world full-task success over uniform generation and transferring the curated distributions to VLA fine-tuning.

**Relevance:** 9
Back to [[topic](#topic-7)] [[top](#topics)]

<a id="7147-joyinside-vlm-agent-product-conditioned-multimodal-policy-learning-for-embodied-agents"></a>

### 7147\. [JoyInside-VLM-Agent: Product-Conditioned Multimodal Policy Learning for Embodied Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12957)
**ArXiv:** 2610.12957 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12957)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.12957.pdf)]

**Authors:** Gangyi Li, LuYao Wang, Jingfu Sun, ..., Jianyong Wang, Xu Yang

**Abstract:** Embodied agents must translate multimodal observations and user requests into executable actions while adapting to product constraints, changing goals, and execution feedback. We present JoyInside-VLM-Agent, a ReAct-style multimodal agent developed through product-conditioned trajectory construction and staged policy learning for robotic platforms and smart hardware. A closed-loop sandbox generates multimodal interaction trajectories covering goal revisions and failure recovery, which are checked against execution outcomes and semantic criteria. Supervised fine-tuning and multi-teacher on-policy distillation precede reinforcement learning with DPER (Dual-Source Privileged On-Policy Self-Distillation with Token-Level Evidence Routing). DPER routes token-level evidence between a frozen privileged teacher and a privileged student sharing the rollout policy's parameters, modulating reward-derived token advantages while preserving their signs. Training rewards combine generative process evaluation, multi-judge rankings, and task-queue consistency. JoyInside-VLM-Agent achieves a BFCL v4 overall score of 77.44, exceeding the evaluated baselines; results on the other public benchmarks are mixed. On JoyInside-Devices and JoyInside-Robotics, the evaluated system, which includes domain-specific skill refinement, leads the baselines on all reported metrics. Two real-world quadruped case studies illustrate visually grounded search, replanning after execution failure, and explicit failure reporting.

**Comment:** Criterion 7: Introduces a ReAct-style robotic agent trained through closed-loop trajectory generation and DPER reinforcement learning for feedback-driven execution, outperforming baselines on all reported JoyInside-Robotics metrics.

**Relevance:** 8
Back to [[topic](#topic-7)] [[top](#topics)]

<a id="7153-before-we-act-learning-action-conditioned-team-response-beliefs-for-decentralized-multi-robot-manipulation"></a>

### 7153\. [Before We Act: Learning Action-Conditioned Team-Response Beliefs for Decentralized Multi-Robot Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14551)
**ArXiv:** 2610.14551 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14551)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.14551.pdf)]

**Authors:** Zihao Li, Yanan Zhou, Ranpeng Qiu, Guoqiang Ren, Weiming Zhi

**Abstract:** In multi-robot manipulation, an action's value depends on how teammates respond. We present \method{} (Cooperative Action--Response Belief Estimation), a framework for learning \emph{team-response beliefs} from local history. A predictive belief encoder represents the interaction and conditions a nominal action. Matched reactive and replay continuations separate each candidate's direct advantage from the utility contributed by teammate adaptation. A candidate-conditioned readout learns this response distribution alongside total advantage, and calibrated selection chooses an alternative or retains the nominal action. Our analysis characterizes when response changes the preferred action and how prediction and source errors limit the recoverable gain. With the representation, proposals, and data fixed, explicit response supervision improves candidate ranking and reduces selection regret relative to learning the same information through branch utilities. The benefit is concentrated in decisions where teammate adaptation changes the preferred action. Across RoboFactory, the MARS Control Track, and physical collaboration tasks, CARE achieves the highest average success among the evaluated policies, combining a capable nominal policy with selective coordination adjustments. Together, these results show that CARE turns predictions of teammate adaptation into effective decentralized action selection. Project page: https://jeong-zju.github.io/care/.

**Comment:** Criterion 7: CARE combines local-history team-response beliefs with calibrated candidate-action selection for decentralized multi-robot manipulation, achieving the highest average success among evaluated policies across RoboFactory, the MARS Control Track, and physical collaboration tasks.

**Relevance:** 8

<a id="7162-proactive-physical-ai-through-continuous-observation-and-timely-intervention"></a>

### 7162\. [Proactive Physical AI through Continuous Observation and Timely Intervention](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16967)
**ArXiv:** 2610.16967 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16967)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.16967.pdf)]

**Authors:** Zhenhua Yang, Jiafeng Liang, Xingyu Zhou, ..., Duyu Tang, Bing Qin

**Abstract:** Physical AI is commonly formulated around an explicitly specified task: given a goal or instruction, a system perceives, reasons, and acts. In many real-world settings, however, important tasks emerge from continuously changing world states before a person explicitly formulates them. We study this complementary problem as Proactive Physical AI, where an AI system continuously observes the physical world, identifies situations that warrant intervention, and initiates appropriate actions at the right time. We develop ProAgent, an end-to-end system built around two components: a Jev-style proactive timing model (dubbed StreamingJev) that determines whether and when intervention is needed, and a Response Model that generates responses for people or invokes available device capabilities for physical execution. To support learning at scale, we develop an automated data pipeline that converts videos into supervision for proactive timing. Experiments demonstrate strong proactive performance under both explicit objectives and standing roles, combining more accurate intervention timing with fewer false triggers than the source-backbone baseline. Real-robot evaluations further demonstrate ProAgent's ability to identify emerging needs and translate them into appropriate physical actions.

**Comment:** Criterion 7: ProAgent combines learned StreamingJev intervention timing with a Response Model that invokes device capabilities, with real-robot evaluations demonstrating proactive identification of emerging needs and execution of physical actions.

**Relevance:** 8
Back to [[topic](#topic-7)] [[top](#topics)]

<a id="7163-declarative-reactive-robot-behaviors-from-imperative-language-models"></a>

### 7163\. [Declarative Reactive Robot Behaviors from Imperative Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17083)
**ArXiv:** 2610.17083 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17083)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.17083.pdf)]

**Authors:** Vincent Berenz, Tasha Kim, Bernhard Schölkopf

**Abstract:** Large Language Models (LLMs) are predominantly trained on imperative programming languages, and existing work on LLM-based robot programming reflects this: it focuses on goal-directed tasks expressed as plans. However, many robotic applications require not tasks but behaviors--open-ended, continuous patterns of sensory-motor engagement with no termination condition (e.g., greeting visitors, patrolling an area). Such behaviors are better served by declarative and reactive programming, on which LLMs have virtually no training. We investigate whether this gap can be bridged. Using Playful, a reactive scripting language, we show that an LLM-based coding agent can generate correct declarative and reactive programs for robotic behaviors. These programs are produced from under-specified natural language descriptions, given suitable context and a set of reusable programming guidelines. The guidelines encode design principles that redirect the agent from imperative defaults toward proper reactive patterns. Developed once by an expert, they generalize across robots and behaviors. Starting from only the generic robot operating system API, the agent jointly designs both the imperative component (Python modules for sensing, acting, and evaluating) and the declarative component (the reactive script that composes them into behavior logic). The declarative nature of reactive programming supports both incremental refinement through conversational turns and one-shot generation of complete behaviors.

**Comment:** Criterion 7: An LLM-based coding agent jointly generates Python sensing, acting, and evaluation modules and Playful reactive robot scripts, with reusable guidelines demonstrated to generalize across robots and behaviors and support conversational refinement.

<a id="7164-dexninja-learning-robust-dexterous-cutting-policy-with-a-real-to-sim-to-real-data-engine"></a>

### 7164\. [DexNinja: Learning Robust Dexterous Cutting Policy with a Real-to-Sim-to-Real Data Engine](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17208)
**ArXiv:** 2610.17208 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17208)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.17208.pdf)]

**Authors:** Haozhe Lou, Runyi Yang, Wanqi Zhong, ..., Hang Zhao, Yiming Li

**Abstract:** Cutting a piece of food looks effortless in human hands. For a robot, however, every cut is a high-contact interaction with a deformable, elasto-plastic object whose behavior changes with pose, contact mode, and cutting stage. Collecting real-world data is expensive and wasteful: each trial typically contaminates or damages the food, so obtaining scalable real world data for dexterous cutting is expensive and difficult. Simulation is an appealing alternative, but a large sim-to-real gap exists in both physics and perception. This gap is especially severe for topology-changing interactions like cutting, where small errors in contact, friction, or material and elasticity responses quickly compound. We propose \textsc{DexNinja}, a real2sim2real framework that turns a handful of real demonstrations into a dense and realistic training distribution. \textsc{DexNinja} (i) reconstructs object instances from real captures, (ii) randomizes physically meaningful parameters under category-level constraints, and (iii) produces augmented dexterous manipulation episodes using a custom differentiable simulator that couples robot dynamics, tactile contact, and deformable cutting. We evaluate \textsc{DexNinja} on a dexterous food-slicing task and show that (1) with tens of real demonstrations and hundreds of augmented simulator data, the success rate could reach more than 60\%. (2) the augmented data consistently improves success rates when only using real data with more than 20\% via sim-to-real, and (3) the resulting policies generalize to out-of-distribution objects with varying shapes and sizes.

**Comment:** Criterion 7: DexNinja introduces a real-to-sim-to-real robot-learning data engine with a custom differentiable simulator for tactile contact and deformable cutting, achieving over 60% success using tens of real demonstrations and hundreds of augmented simulation episodes.

**Relevance:** 8
Back to [[topic](#topic-7)] [[top](#topics)]

<a id="7166-value-preserving-action-quantization"></a>

### 7166\. [Value-Preserving Action Quantization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17252)
**ArXiv:** 2610.17252 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17252)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.17252.pdf)]

**Authors:** Max Muchen Sun, Jueun Kwon, Benjamin Riviere, Todd Murphey

**Abstract:** Search-based policy learning, such as Monte Carlo tree search (MCTS), offers the flexibility to trade runtime computation for data efficiency and adaptation to novel environments. For robot learning, however, applying search requires discretizing the continuous action space and associated dynamics, typically through action quantization. While reconstruction-based quantization is sufficient for representing the continuous action space alone, policy learning additionally requires the quantized actions to preserve their effects on future states and values. We show that such requirement is not necessarily captured by action reconstruction error. Instead, we prove that the error in the value function resulting from action quantization is tightly bounded by the reconstruction error of state reachability under the quantized actions. Based on these results, we propose value-preserving action quantization (VAQ), which quantizes actions by preserving state reachability. We further combine VAQ with MCTS (VAQ-MCTS) as a value-preserving, search-based policy learning framework for behavioral cloning. Using a wide range of object pushing tasks as a controlled benchmark, we evaluate VAQ-MCTS against state-of-the-art behavioral cloning methods. VAQ-MCTS matches or exceeds their performance under standard settings, while substantially improving data efficiency and robustness to heterogeneous demonstrations, while enabling zero-shot adaptation to novel environments. Hardware experiments further demonstrate the effectiveness of VAQ-MCTS, completing all 30 real-world object pushing trials with only 10 minutes of demonstration data.

**Comment:** Criterion 7: VAQ-MCTS combines reachability-preserving action quantization with Monte Carlo tree search for robot policy learning, enabling zero-shot adaptation and completing all 30 real-world pushing trials with only 10 minutes of demonstration data.

**Relevance:** 8
Back to [[topic](#topic-7)] [[top](#topics)]

<a id="7168-i3l-iterative-interactive-imitation-learning-for-whole-body-mobile-manipulation"></a>

### 7168\. [I3L: Iterative Interactive Imitation Learning for Whole-Body Mobile Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17748)
**ArXiv:** 2610.17748 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17748)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.17748.pdf)]

**Authors:** Wensi Ai, Christopher Luey, Karthik Dharmarajan, ..., Li Fei-Fei, Ruohan Zhang

**Abstract:** Policies trained offline from teleoperated demonstrations can degrade outside their training distribution. This gap is particularly acute in long-horizon whole-body mobile manipulation, where long horizons amplify compounding errors and coordinated arm, torso, and base motion makes exhaustive demonstration coverage impractical. We introduce I3L, an integrated hardware and learning system that combines whole-body correction with iterative policy refinement to address this last-mile reliability gap. Its low-cost, open-source JoyLo+ interface uses policy-tracking leader arms and touch-triggered takeover to enable coordinated corrections across the body parts, while iterative deployment and retraining allow operators to target failures of the updated policy. We evaluate I3L across three robot embodiments and eight simulated and real-world tasks, including four long-horizon whole-body mobile manipulation tasks. Controlled simulation experiments show that four-round HG-DAgger achieves the highest final success on both tasks. Under this protocol, I3L improves autonomous success over the base policies by an average of 68.3 percentage points and achieves at least 90% success on every task, outperforming demonstration-only training by 35.6 percentage points on average with the same number of training trajectories. Averaged across tasks, takeover duration per episode decreases by 72% over four rounds. Together, these results show that I3L helps close the last-mile gap between offline training and reliable deployment and provides practical insights into collecting and using corrections for long-horizon whole-body mobile manipulation. Videos, visualizations, and appendix are at https://i-3l.github.io/.

**Comment:** Criterion 7: Introduces an interactive robot-learning system combining JoyLo+ whole-body corrective feedback with iterative HG-DAgger training, improving autonomous success by 68.3 percentage points on average and reaching at least 90% success on every evaluated task.

**Relevance:** 8
Back to [[topic](#topic-7)] [[top](#topics)]

<a id="7170-reenvision-future-guided-selective-action-repair-for-long-horizon-manipulation"></a>

### 7170\. [ReEnvision: Future-Guided Selective Action Repair for Long-Horizon Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17977)
**ArXiv:** 2610.17977 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17977)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.17977.pdf)]

**Authors:** Xiyin Zeng, Xuewei Huang, Yi Lu, Weihao Yuan, Hao Wang

**Abstract:** Long-horizon robotic manipulation requires robust execution over sequences of interdependent actions. Existing methods typically handle execution deviations by replanning the remaining action chunk or correcting actions step by step as new observations arrive. However, a local deviation may affect only a few actions under distribution shift, yet replanning regenerates the entire chunk from an unfamiliar state, potentially introducing errors into otherwise valid actions. Step-wise correction may react too late, as it does not explicitly anticipate how the deviation affects subsequent actions. We propose ReEnvision, a future-guided selective action repair framework that updates a joint future-action belief using execution feedback. It then learns how the resulting belief revision affects each unexecuted action. The resulting belief revision enables selective repair of only the affected actions within a cached action chunk, preserving valid actions and reserving full replanning for severe deviations. On CALVIN, L-CALVIN, and RLBench, ReEnvision achieves 86.6% 5-step success, improves 10-step success from 56% to 63%, and reaches an average success rate of 88.8%, respectively. On five real-world manipulation tasks, it achieves an average success rate of 82.8%. Controlled repair experiments further demonstrate the benefits of localizing affected actions and preserving valid ones during execution.

**Comment:** Criterion 7: ReEnvision combines execution feedback, cached action chunks, and learned selective action repair in a long-horizon manipulation framework, achieving 82.8% average success across five real-world tasks.

**Relevance:** 8
Back to [[topic](#topic-7)] [[top](#topics)]

<a id="7179-rescue-is-this-alarm-worth-acting-on-counterfactual-evaluation-of-vla-runtime-monitors"></a>

### 7179\. [RESCUE: Is This Alarm Worth Acting On? Counterfactual Evaluation of VLA Runtime Monitors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13516)
**ArXiv:** 2610.13516 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13516)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.13516.pdf)]

**Authors:** David Ahmedt-Aristizabal, Abdelwahed Khamis, Mohammad Ali Armin, Lars Petersson

**Abstract:** Runtime failure monitors for robot policies are typically evaluated by how accurately they distinguish failed from successful executions. Yet an accurate alarm may arrive too late for recovery, while an unnecessary intervention may disrupt an execution that would otherwise succeed. We introduce RESCUE, a counterfactual framework for evaluating whether a monitor's alarms enable useful interventions. By restoring simulator and policy states at execution checkpoints, RESCUE compares alternative actions, identifies the latest checkpoint satisfying a specified recovery criterion, and evaluates whether acting on alarms reduces expected task-completion cost. We evaluate RESCUE with OpenVLA-OFT and $π_{0.5}$ over LIBERO-Spatial and LIBERO-Plus using 128,340 counterfactual branches. Recoverability depends on the state--intervention pair rather than on robot state alone: at $τ=0.5$, 14 of 66 OpenVLA-OFT/LIBERO-Plus failures contain a recoverable checkpoint under continuation, compared with 34 of 66 under continuation or scripted recovery, while recovery reduces recoverability in other conditions. On the primary strong-policy conditions, favourable detection scores do not establish lower intervention cost. Under the evaluated stop-and-reset model, even an idealised detector can afford only a 2.0--3.3% false-alarm rate before unnecessary resets outweigh the savings from stopping failures early. These results show that monitor value depends jointly on the intervention available and the workload in which it is deployed.

**Comment:** Criterion 7: RESCUE introduces a counterfactual framework for evaluating robotic-policy monitoring and recovery, restoring simulator and policy checkpoints to compare interventions across 128,340 branches with OpenVLA-OFT and π0.5 on LIBERO-Spatial and LIBERO-Plus.

**Relevance:** 7
Back to [[topic](#topic-7)] [[top](#topics)]

<a id="7183-dexmimic-a-generalist-dexterous-hand-object-tracking-policy-via-value-guided-reset-allocation"></a>

### 7183\. [DexMimic: A Generalist Dexterous Hand-Object Tracking Policy via Value-Guided Reset Allocation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19089)
**ArXiv:** 2610.19089 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19089)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.19089.pdf)]

**Authors:** Zhexi Luo, Zhixuan Xu, Rui Yang, ..., Yiwen Hou, Lin Shao

**Abstract:** Achieving human-like dexterous manipulation remains a central challenge in robotics. Learning from human hand-object interaction (HOI) datasets offers a promising pathway to diverse dexterous skills, yet existing physics-based approaches train specialist policies for individual trajectories, thereby limiting skill reuse at scale. We introduce DexMimic, a generalist dexterous hand-object tracking policy jointly trained across diverse HOI trajectories. Efficient joint training requires allocating each rollout to both an undertrained phase and a starting state that supports continued tracking. DexMimic realizes this insight through Value-Guided Reset Allocation, which adapts the initialization distribution online to prioritize difficult phases and select reset states with higher expected tracking returns. We further introduce a benchmark spanning training, fine-tuning, and generalization. Experiments show improved tracking success and training efficiency, faster fine-tuning, and the potential for zero-shot generalization to unseen trajectories and objects; larger training sets further improve efficiency and generalization. Finally, we validate DexMimic on a real-world dexterous manipulation platform.

**Comment:** Criterion 7: Introduces a dexterous hand–object tracking benchmark spanning training, fine-tuning, and generalization, alongside Value-Guided Reset Allocation that improves robot policy training efficiency, with real-world dexterous manipulation validation.

**Relevance:** 7
Back to [[topic](#topic-7)] [[top](#topics)]

<a id="7184-instruction-conditioned-motion-control-for-robotic-arms-language-to-code-decomposition-and-reinforcement-learning"></a>

### 7184\. [Instruction-Conditioned Motion Control for Robotic Arms: Language-to-Code Decomposition and Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19617)
**ArXiv:** 2610.19617 [[page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19617)] [[pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.19617.pdf)]

**Authors:** Fan Huang, Dezhong Tong, Changyu Chen, Haewoon Kwak, Jisun An

**Abstract:** Instruction-Conditioned Motion Control (ICMC) separates parameter specification, instruction generation, and code synthesis for language-driven robot control, making execution outcomes traceable to instructions and programs. We compare one-shot generation with ICMC under o1 and o4-mini on cube pick-and-place and letter-reshaping tasks for a UR5e arm in MuJoCo. We then train an open-weight instruction policy by GRPO through a frozen code generator on cube placement onto two- and five-cube letter layouts, averaging the rewards of five programs per instruction. The logged positive-reward rate rose from 31.6% to 35.3% (253/800 to 282/800) between the first and last five training steps. A log audit identifies positive binary labels associated with geometric-failure messages: at least 19 in that run, 18 in the dense-reward run, and 5 in the run with one program per instruction. In one case, all five rewards were 1.0 while the log reports a 0.9515 m miss against a 0.05 m tolerance; the verdict came from the program's own output stream. We distinguish logged reward rates from independently verified task success and report descriptive results for each configuration's single training trajectory. We introduce an independent evaluation protocol that checks task completion against fixed specifications, separating robot control from success assessment to support reproducible evaluation.

**Comment:** Criterion 7: ICMC combines language-to-code decomposition with GRPO-trained instruction policies for UR5e manipulation in MuJoCo and introduces independent task-success evaluation after identifying unreliable execution rewards.

**Relevance:** 7
Back to [[topic](#topic-7)] [[top](#topics)]
